# Description of the Volume

->**The Physical Binding**<-

The image displays the fore-edge of this historical volume, showcasing its structural integrity and the methods used to preserve the contents within. The book is bound in a durable, light-colored material—most likely vellum <note>Vellum is a high-quality parchment made from calfskin, prized for its longevity</note> or heavy parchment—which has aged to a creamy, off-white hue.

The thickness of the text block suggests a substantial collection of leaves, indicating a work of significant length or a compilation of multiple treatises. To protect the pages from warping and to keep out dust, the volume is equipped with two functional ties. These white cords, threaded directly through the covers, wrap around the front edge to hold the boards firmly against the paper. This was a common security and preservation feature for manuscripts and early printed books intended for scholarly use.

# Johannes Reuchlin of Pforzheim,
## the most celebrated Doctor of Laws: *Sergius*,
## or, *The Head of the Head*, with the com-
### mentary of Georg Simler
### of Wimpfen.

<note>Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522) was a massive figure in the Northern Renaissance, famous for his mastery of Greek and Hebrew. "Sergius" is a satirical play he wrote in 1496, originally titled "Scaenica Progymnasmata." The subtitle "Capitis caput" is a pun on the name of his patron, Johann von Dalberg, the Bishop of Worms, whose name "Dalberg" was associated with "Tal" (valley) and "Berg" (mountain/head).</note>

<summary>This is the title page for Johannes Reuchlin's satirical Latin comedy "Sergius," featuring a commentary by the scholar Georg Simler.</summary>

<meta>This page begins the commentary by Georgius Simler on the play "Sergius" by Johannes Reuchlin. It establishes the theoretical framework for comedy based on classical authorities.</meta>

# ON COMEDY PAGE II

### The commentaries of Georgius Simler on the *Sergius* of Johannes Reuchlin of Pforzheim, Doctor of Laws.

<margin>Theophrastus</margin>
<margin>Donatus</margin>
¶ Since it is customary when explaining authors to investigate and seek out what kind of verse the poet has written, I believe I should remain silent regarding comedy rather than say too little. This is partly because the Greek author Theophrastus, a man of divine eloquence, composed a whole volume on that subject. Furthermore, regarding Aelius Donatus <note>A 4th-century Roman grammarian and teacher of St. Jerome</note> in his introduction to the reading of Terence, I have seen no one to this day who has more extensively traced the origin of that kind of poetry. He has recounted its beginnings so copiously that he alone ought to be thought to have perfectly satisfied the entire eager company of grammarians. Therefore, I judged it a fairer condition for the anxious reader to understand what Donatus left in writing, since that most learned commentator always wished his entire industrious career to be occupied in dispelling the errors of readers. Nevertheless, I am content to add a few things—as is customary to taste at the doors of books—concerning the subject matter of comedy, provided I first touch upon a brief little preface before I impart the explanation for each individual verse.

### ON COMEDY

<margin>Definition of comedy</margin>
<margin>Comedy and Tragedy</margin>
<margin>Emotions to be sought in comedy</margin>
<margin>The comic character</margin>
Comedy belongs to that genre of poem which is called by the Greeks *dramatikon* <note>original: δραματικὸν</note>, that is, "active." In this genre, the characters act alone without any interruption from the poet. By those Greeks it is defined thus: *komoidia esti idiotikon kai politikon pragmaton akindynos periochē* <note>original: κωμῳδία ἐστὶ ἰδιωτικῶν καὶ πολιτικῶν πραγμάτων ἀκίνδυνος περιοχή</note>. This roughly means: Comedy (for it is written with a long 'o' <note>omega</note> with an 'i' <note>iota</note> subscript in the second syllable, which letter is turned into the 'oe' diphthong) is, I say, a treatment of private and civil affairs without danger. It is named after the gatherings of rustics in the villages, which they call *komas* <note>original: κώμας</note>, that is, villas or villages. But how far it is removed from tragedy, he who does not know may learn from the words of Xenocrates of Chalcedon. When he was rebuked by Bion, he said, "I will certainly not answer you. For tragedy does not deign to answer comedy when it is provoked by it." Donatus teaches that comedy contains the emotions of civil and private life, through which one learns what is useful in life and what, conversely, should be avoided. From those words of Donatus, we elicit that the emotions in comedy must be sought and explained; the worthy interpreter himself everywhere skillfully searched these out, subtly discussing the inclinations of the heart (as they say) more than the mere inflections of words. For this reason, I cease to wonder why it is less taken up by "grammatists," that is, by elementary teachers. And when Marcus Cicero mentions the type of narrative which is placed in characters, he wants it to contain a diversity of minds, which he especially observes in the acts of comedies. Furthermore, in style, comedy is what they call a *charaktēra* <note>original: χαρακτῆρα</note> most similar to everyday speech: restrained, not in sublime oratory, but thin, light, common, and creeping along the ground. I see that Horace felt this when he said:

->For this reason, some have questioned whether or not comedy<-
->was a poem, because neither its words nor its subject matter<-
->possess a fiery spirit or force, and except that it differs<-
->from prose by a fixed meter, it is mere prose.<-

<summary>Georgius Simler introduces his commentary on Reuchlin's play by defining the genre of comedy using classical sources like Donatus and Horace. He emphasizes that comedy deals with the "safe" private affairs of ordinary people in a style resembling everyday speech.</summary>
<keywords>Comedy, Literary Theory, Johannes Reuchlin, Georgius Simler, Donatus, Horace, Definition of Drama, Classical Education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion on the nature of comedy, transitioning from general definitions to the specific moral and political purpose of the present work.</meta>

# ON COMEDY III

<margin>The plots of comedies</margin>
For this reason, comic poets have been called "popular" by many. In Old Comedy <note>The "Old Comedy" of ancient Greece, such as the works of Aristophanes, known for sharp political satire.</note>, the themes—which we call "arguments" or plots—were once actual historical events openly performed on stage. However, the abuse of this freedom led to a law being passed to restrain such defamation. Consequently, this genre of poetry was changed into Satire, but when that too began to fall under suspicion, it was likewise abolished. Finally, this "New Comedy" was invented, which uses a fictional premise <note>original: "hypothesi"</note>. Yet, these plots are not devised at random, as the unlearned mob constantly shouts; Cicero, in his speech *In Defense of Sextus Roscius*, most skillfully refutes such ignorance. There, he shows that these stories are fashioned by poets so that we might see our own morals portrayed in the persons of others, providing a vivid image of our daily life. I believe the purpose of comic writers can be perfectly examined through this little verse of Cicero's.

I suspect something similar has happened with our own author. He was incessantly reminded of that word from Ecclesiastes <note>The author refers to the biblical book of Ecclesiastes (Coheleth) 10:16.</note> crying out: *Woe to you, O land, whose king is a child.* <margin>The plot of the present comedy</margin> Therefore, I believe he turned his mind toward this goal: to provide wholesome counsel to princes through the warnings and persuasion found in the premise of this play. He suggests they must abstain from the "empty heads" to whom Plato forbade the administration of kingdoms. Lactantius—that "Christian Cicero"—introduced Plato's sentiment (which deserves eternal memory) in this most elegant phrasing: *Those states would be blessed where either philosophers ruled, or kings practiced philosophy.*

<margin>Alexander the Great</margin> Alexander the Great of Macedon also felt this, as he considered it better to excel in learning than in military glory. <margin>Zeno</margin> What do we think moved Zeno, or <margin>Aristotle</margin> Aristotle? They rendered the kingdom and the empire subject to no one except the wise. <margin>Socrates</margin> We read that Socrates pulled back Glaucon, the brother of Plato, when he wished to enter public life, on the grounds that he was unskilled and ignorant of affairs. <margin>Antisthenes</margin> Antisthenes, a philosopher famous throughout all Greece, advised the Athenians to vote that their donkeys were horses; when they replied that this was irrational, Antisthenes retorted: "What then are the generals among you, who have learned nothing and are merely appointed?" Does not Nestor, in the ninth book of the *Iliad*, argue that the kingdom was granted to Agamemnon by Jove so that he might consult for the others rightly and prudently? The Emperor Justinian thought the Republic ought to be armed with laws, not just decorated with weapons. Finally, Sacred History records that when the prophet Samuel sought counsel from God, the response was: *It is not physical beauty or appearance that makes a king, but true virtue without doubt.* <note>original: "facere regalem"</note> By that path, I say, Solomon succeeded David, and Joshua succeeded Moses as a leader for the people of Israel.

And indeed, Josephus left it most correctly written that those to whom the safety of subjects is committed ought to be full of every virtue. For if those who lead the people are ignorant of letters (which most become them) and neglect to gather the logic of good governance from the precepts of others (which cannot happen without learning and reading), they will be merely "stage-play" princes and imaginary ones. What else is an illiterate prince but the image of a lion ruling over other beasts? Therefore, one should not think it was by chance that the custom grew strong among the ancients for the elders to claim the kingdom for themselves, because counsel belongs more widely to that age...

<column-break/>

A ii <note>This is a signature mark used by printers to keep the pages in the correct order for binding.</note>

<summary>The author argues that comedy serves as a mirror for daily life and moral instruction, specifically warning that a state suffers when its ruler is unlearned or immature. He cites numerous classical and biblical authorities to prove that true leadership requires wisdom and education rather than just power.</summary>
<keywords>New Comedy, Cicero, Plato, kingship, education, Alexander the Great, Aristotle, Socrates, Antisthenes, Justinian, biblical kings, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the moral and political implications of comedy, transitioning from the general purpose of the genre to a specific critique of governance and a history of the great comic playwrights of antiquity.</meta>

# ON COMEDY

...they knew from experience. An unlearned prince is therefore a ruinous thing, especially if he colludes so ruinously with the most wicked courtiers, lest it be thought—as Lampridius <note>Aelius Lampridius, one of the traditional authors of the *Augustan History*, a collection of Roman imperial biographies.</note> testifies—that the principate is given by luck rather than by fate. And Vopiscus <note>Flavius Vopiscus, another purported author of the *Augustan History*.</note> says there is nothing that makes princes worse than foolish and detestable courtiers and ignorance of public affairs. They say Diocletian <note>Roman Emperor (r. 284–305 AD) known for his administrative reforms.</note> remarked, not without wit, that nothing is more difficult than to rule well. Hence the Greek Sage said: "Do not rule before you learn to be ruled" <note>original: μὴ ἄρχε πρὶμ ἀρχεσθαι μάθης (*mē arche prin archesthai mathēs*)</note>. For this reason, a prince should be learned. 

For if you look diligently into the theme of our poet, you will understand a prince very similar to Burtubatta <note>A character name, likely representing a buffoonish or incompetent ruler in the play being introduced.</note>, who must be thought to have "found a skull"—that is, an empty-headed counselor. If the prince himself cherishes such a man in his bosom, the rest of the crowd of courtiers never cease to shower him with kisses (a "crowd," I say) not unlike actors in such a principality. But these "skulls" are the reasons why kingdoms fall; virtue and integrity perish, and the commonwealth is put at risk. By these men, liberty is exterminated, and a savage tyranny—intent on being exported to the most remote lands—is stirred up. 

Returning from this digression, I leave these matters aside. Among the most ancient comic poets are Susarion, Rullus, and Magnes; these men performed the jests of the old discipline neither skillfully nor gracefully. <margin>The most ancient comic poets.</margin> The second age had Aristophanes, Eupolis, and Cratinus, who wrote the most biting comedies to attack vices. In the third age were Menander and Philemon, who mitigated all the bitterness of comedy. When the Roman poets judged it more valuable to translate and "make Latin" the plays of these men rather than to forge new ones, they took care to illustrate the lights and ornaments of the Greek style with Latin speech; yet, they could not achieve what they set out to follow. For they translated too unsuccessfully and unhappily—so much so, that if you wish to compare them to the Greek wit and the "lights" with which those plays shine so immoderately, you will understand that a vast difference exists in our nearly impoverished and un-eloquent tongue. 

Who does not know that Terence followed in the footsteps of Menander and Apollodorus? Rightly, the former—Menander, that is—did not displease him, for he was a great poet in the "comic shoe" <term>socco: the low-heeled slipper worn by comic actors, symbolizing the genre of comedy</term>, sought by the kings of Egypt and Macedon through fleets and ambassadors. Among us Romans, however, Sedigitus <note>Volcacius Sedigitus, a Roman literary critic known for his ranking of comic poets.</note> treated the ranking of Latin comic poets with no small clarity. He suggested in his verses the place and honor in which each should be held; there, he placed Terence in sixth place, after Plautus (whom he ranked second). But our Servius <note>Servius Marius Honoratus, a 4th-century grammarian.</note>, a member of the "triumvirate" of grammarians, placed Terence before all other comic poets for another reason: the propriety of his Latin speech. 

Therefore, I would wish those who profess their learning by elementary weighing of texts to look not so much at the reading as at the talent. <margin>Comic writer vs. Comic actor</margin> I would warn them that comedy is sacred to Apollo, just as it is handed down as sacred to Father Liber <note>An Italian god of fertility and wine, often identified with Dionysus/Bacchus.</note>. And the distinction between a *comicus* and a *comoedus* must not be neglected, in order to correct and heal the error of certain "bearded little men" <note>A derogatory term for self-important but ignorant scholars.</note> who wander immoderately in this matter. A *comoedus* is called a "player"—an actor and reciter of comedy. But a *comicus* is the poet himself—the author and maker of the comedy. Roscius was a *comoedus* <note>Quintus Roscius Gallus, the most famous actor of ancient Rome.</note>. Plautus and Terence were *comici*. As Suetonius <note>referred to here by his cognomen, Tranquillus.</note> says in his *Life of Claudius*: "And actors <note>original: *comoedi*</note> were introduced through imitation." And Juvenal in his third Satire: "The actor <note>original: *comoedus*</note> plays the wife."

<summary>This page discusses the political necessity of a learned ruler, warning against "empty-headed" advisors through a comparison to the play's characters. It also provides a brief history of Greek and Roman comedy, distinguishing between the roles of the playwright (comicus) and the actor (comoedus).</summary>
<keywords>kingship, courtiers, Burtubatta, Aristophanes, Menander, Terence, Plautus, literary criticism, comicus vs comoedus, Roman history</keywords>

V

# PROLOGUE

->If ever you have turned your feet toward jesting<-
->Or if you have lent your ears to playful things,<-
->In this new poet’s play, I pray,<-
->Deign to be more attentive than before.<-
->Here there shall be no place for lewdness or lust,<-
->For harlots or the gloomy cares of old men,<-
->But rather an army of actors and witty gibes.<-

---

### ON THE PROLOGUE

**If ever.** This prologue performs the role of an introduction, which the Greeks call *miktos* <note>original: μικτὸς; meaning "mixed."</note> and we call "mixed." For it commends the play, explains the plot, and, following the poet’s plan, reports to the people why he has written differently from the customs of the ancients.

<margin>Games.</margin>
**If ever.** He entreats the spectators learnedly and affably through that which might entice them; indeed, he invites them to pleasantness and a playful matter. We know that the Roman people were so refreshed by the games once provided by officials that they would sell their votes for them; thus says the **Satirist** <note>Juvenal, the Roman poet famous for the phrase "bread and circuses."</note>: "Long ago, ever since we stopped selling our votes to no one." **Paulus Orosius** <note>A 5th-century historian and priest.</note>, distinguishing between the city of Sodom and the city of Rome, writes freely that the Roman people exclaimed that the swords of the Goths had achieved nothing at Rome if only they could have their circus back. Here it should be noted: **Vitruvius** is the authority in Book V of *On Architecture* that the ancestors established games for the sake of pleasure, not only to honor the immortal gods. Very frequent mention of games is made among writers; see **Herodotus** in *Urania*, **Josephus** in *Antiquities* Book XVI, **Statius** in Book VI, **Pliny** in Book XIX, and **Plutarch** in *Timoleon*. Content for a long time with the spectacles of the Circus, Rome finally admitted theatrical plays under the pretext of worshipping the immortal gods; these we also call "scenic" plays. **Valerius Maximus** <margin>Valerius.</margin> records their origin in Book II. We read that the time of Tiberius was marked by a public calamity: for by the collapse of a theater, twenty thousand people perished <note>Referring to the disaster at Fidenae in 27 AD, where a cheaply built wooden amphitheater collapsed.</note>. **Toward jesting.** <margin>place</margin> **Marcus Cicero** admits jesting provided it is "liberal" <note>Meaning "gentlemanly" or fitting for a free person.</note>. **Aristotle** agrees in Book IV of the *Ethics*, where he explains that jesting is necessary for men, like a soothing ointment for those exhausted by labor. The grammarians **Phocas**, **Sergius**, and **Servius** are authorities that the word for "jest" (*iocus*) can have the plural forms *ioci* or *ioca*. From this comes the verb "to jest" <margin>To jest</margin> (*iocari*), which signifies doing trifling things. Furthermore, a jest is that by which we provoke laughter in speech—as **Quintilian** says <margin>Play</margin> in his section on the ridiculous—just as "play" (*ludus*) consists in action; they are very often confused. **Catullus** used "to jest" (*iocari*) in the place of "to play" in his poem on the weeping for the sparrow. **Jest** is fashioned by the poets as the son of the goddess Venus. <margin>To lie</margin> He does not lie <note>original: *non mentitur*; here meaning the poet's personification is not without truth.</note>. For if you remove the figurative language, I do not know how much you would leave even in Holy Scripture. We read that **Pythagoras** abstained from laughter, witticisms, and jesting. For more on jests, see **Macrobius** in the *Saturnalia*. **Of a playful matter.** Full of pleasure, easy, and worthy of play. **Virgil**: "For neither light nor playful things are sought." **Catullus**: "Whom the Hamadryad goddesses nourish as a playful thing for themselves in the dew." **Marcellus** uses "playful" (*ludicrum*) to mean "lightweight."

A iii

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on the Prologue, moving from the history of Roman games to a philological analysis of specific words in the text, such as "pray," "fable," and "old age." It cites various classical authorities to define the nature and purpose of theater.</meta>

<header>PROLOGUE VI</header>
<page-num>VI</page-num>

receives it. **Servius** <note>A 4th-century grammarian and commentator on Virgil.</note> interprets it as "sight." **Cornelius Tacitus** interprets it as "a game," for he says: "In the fourth consulship of Nero and Cornelius Cossus, a five-year festival of games was established after the manner of Greek competition." **Lent your ears.** You have been attentive. This is a metaphor; **Terence** says: "Prick up your ears, Pamphilus." **In this new [poet's play].** We make the listeners attentive when we promise to speak of new things.

<margin>
I pray
I beseech
</margin>
**I pray.** We will likewise have the audience attentive if we ask that they be willing to be attentive; therefore, this prologue performs the role of an introduction. To "pray" (*obsecrare*) signifies to ask more earnestly. For strictly speaking, to pray is to seek help from sacred things. **Festus** <note>A 2nd-century Roman lexicographer.</note> says that **Donatus** <note>A famous 4th-century grammarian.</note> remarks that we "entreat" (*oramus*) those who are peaceful, but we "pray" (*obsecramus*) to those who are angry. **New poet.** We will soon begin a small discussion on poets below. **Play.** **Cicero**, in the first volume of the *New Rhetoric* (if we believe **Jerome** that this work is Cicero's) <note>He refers to the *Rhetorica ad Herennium*, which was attributed to Cicero in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note>, has defined and described the "play" (*fabula*) abundantly; some believe it was the invention of Mercury. Myth-makers probe the secret reasons of fables. **Aristotle** says a play consists of wonders. **Hermogenes** <note>A 2nd-century Greek rhetorician.</note> defined a play thus: "It is a fictional speech exhibiting an image of truth through a plausible arrangement." You will weigh these words, reader, and by reading plays frequently, you will by no means convict poets of lying—which the senseless, lazy, and illiterate multitude shouts out hour by hour and day by day. Orators are accustomed to handing down the fable first to boys, so that they may easily mold their still-soft minds toward better habits of life. Even the orator himself uses the fable among his examples. "Fable" (*Fabula*) is a word that sounds like a diminutive; the Greeks call them "the dramas" <note>original: τα δράματα</note> and the Latins call them *fabulae*, as if they were "doing-tools" (*factibulas*) from "doing" (*faciendo*). For a play is said to be "acted," not merely "recounted." Whether it is fitting for a philosopher, a professor of truth, to use a fable may be read in **Macrobius** <note>A 5th-century Roman provincial and writer.</note>, in the first book of the *Dream of Scipio*. "Fable" also signifies a matter that has been vehemently spread abroad. It differs from an *apologue* in that the latter contains something allegorical besides; the *apologue* pertains to life and morals, such as those of the most witty **Aesop**.

<margin>
Worthy
To mark
Before
</margin>
**Deign.** The verb "to deign" (*dignari*) means both to hold as worthy and to judge as worthy. **Virgil**: "Deigned to be joined in marriage with the proud Anchises." "To mark" (*dignorare*) is another thing; it means to place signs upon something, as one might see in the case of cattle. **Before.** He places this among compound adverbs, composed from "before," which itself lacks grammatical cases and acts as an adverb; it signifies what the Greeks call "earlier" <note>original: πρότερον</note>, that is, "prior." **Here there shall be no place for lewdness.** He proves that the audience ought to be attentive since here there are no harlots’ lewdnesses, which delight more than they benefit. For poets wish either to delight or to be useful. **Or the gloomy cares of old men.** That is, the representation of the severity of parents toward their children. **Lewdness.** A slippery, soft, and idle license.

<margin>Care</margin>
**Gloomy cares.** From its effect, care is called "gloomy" because what burns the heart makes it sad; or perhaps the old men are sickly and therefore gloomy. **Naevius** <note>An early Roman dramatist.</note> calls old age a "source of grief," for it is harmful and, as **Aristotle** says, it is a natural disease. The sentence of **Apollodorus** is celebrated: "Old age is itself a disease" <note>original: τὸ γῆρας αὐτὸ νόσημα</note>. **Terence** translated this when he said, "Old age itself is a sickness." And the poet **Virgil** used a fitting epithet for old age: "Diseases and gloomy old age steal in." Two things, authors write, diminish the strength of the body: disease and old age. **Servius** says in his eleventh commentary, citing **Sallust**, that "old age" refers to a specific stage of life. **Juvenal** collects a few of the inconveniences of old age, whose lot is hard. **Virgil**, in Book VI, writes that old age dwells in the vestibule of the underworld.

<summary>This page provides a philological and historical commentary on the prologue's opening lines, defining the nature of a 'play' (fabula) as a tool for moral education rather than mere lewdness. It draws heavily on classical authorities like Virgil, Cicero, and Aristotle to explain terms related to theater and aging.</summary>

<header>PROLOGUE VII</header>
<page-num>VII</page-num>

<margin>Army</margin>
**An army**, but an army of actors. **Groups of stage-players.** This is a festive metaphor <note>original: "translatio"</note>, since the word "army" is usually said of a company of soldiers. In a metaphor, one must ensure it is not harsh 
<margin>
Stage-players
Actors
Stage-craft
A stinging joke
Kinds
of wit
</margin>
nor dissimilar; this one is not so much soft as it is highly ornate. **Terence** <note>A Roman playwright (c. 185–159 BC) whose comedies were standard school texts.</note> says: "I now dismiss the army of actors. Farewell." **Maximus** in book II mentions the origin of the games, which are called *ludii* and *ludiones* <note>terms for stage-performers or dancers</note>, and *ludiae* for women practicing the stage-craft. **Martial** <note>A Roman poet known for his epigrams.</note> writes: "Hermes is the care and labor of the actresses." **Stinging jokes.** <note>original: "Scommata"</note> These are unavoidable jests of a bitter urbanity. There are two kinds of wit: one is found in the facts, the other is handled in the word. This latter type **Cicero** and **Pomponius** teach us to call a "witticism" <note>original: "dicterium"</note> when we achieve a laugh through a spoken word. Therefore, we divide it in two: into the "reproach" <note>original: "ledoria"</note> and the "stinging joke" <note>original: "scomma"</note>. **Macrobius** <note>A 5th-century Latin writer.</note> denies that Roman names can be found for these, unless perhaps you call the "reproach" a criticism aimed at insult, while the "stinging joke" is a figurative bite. **An army of actors.** This is a most elegant metaphor. So says **Plautus** in the *Asinaria*: "These now are the legions, troops, and armies of thieves."

## For Buttubatta is believed to have found
## The skull of a certain old-womanish Sergius
## Who was the teacher of Muhammad in the first place,
## From which Sergius this comedy takes its name.

<margin>Buttubatta</margin>
**For Buttubatta.** A prologue's duty is to look out for the spectators by reciting a little summary of the plot. **Plautus** observed this in the *Menaechmi*; **Terence** complains that he was not allowed to do so. Our most cultured poet had no haters to whom he needed to respond. It should certainly be known that the particle "For" <note>original: "Nam"</note> is either subjoined to the previous statement or used as a turning point. **Buttubatta** is the name of a foolish, light, and trifling person—that is, someone of no dignity, according to **Naevius** <note>An early Roman dramatist.</note>. **Found.** <note>original: "repperisse"</note> we double the letter 'p' in the word *repperi* so that the meter of the poem stands. **Ausonius** in a single four-line poem both lengthened it by doubling the letter and shortened it by taking it away. **Is believed.** That is, it is thought. **Terence** in the *Adelphoe*: "Just now, I believe, they were all ashamed." 
<margin>Skull</margin>
**The skull.** The upper bone of the head is called the *calvaria*. **Apuleius** mentions this in book III of the *Metamorphoses*. **Caper** the grammarian calls it the *calva* or the *cranion*. **Caelius** and **Varro** call it the *calvaria*. **Livy**: "They encased the skull in gold." **Arthemon** drank water from a spring at night out of the skull of a man who had been killed and cremated, as a remedy for the falling sickness <note>epilepsy</note>. **Cornelius Celsus** <note>A Roman encyclopedist known for his medical work.</note> in book VIII, and **Pliny** say that earth from a skull is a "depilatory" <note>original: "psilotrum"</note> for the eyelashes. Those who suffer in half of the head are said to labor under the disease of 
<margin>Migraine</margin>
**migraine** <note>original: "hemicranea"</note>, just as **cephalea** is when the whole head is afflicted. **Old-womanish.** <note>original: "Anilis"</note> A derivative of "old woman," meaning old and somewhat dirty. **Virgil** in book VII: "He transforms himself into the face of an old woman." **Muhammad.** 
<margin>Of Sergius. The Law.</margin>
This man is most impure against our law; more about him shortly below. **Of Sergius.** We read of a certain Sergius, a leader of a sect <note>original: "haeresiarcha"</note>, who restored the heresy of the "Headless ones" <note>original: "acephalorum"</note>. 
<margin>Sergius</margin>
The bad teacher and director easily took hold of a most shameful disciple, and the heretic easily listened to his heretical instructor. For if you add evil to evil, you create a "contribution" <note>original: "symbolum"</note>. **Teacher** <note>original: "Magister"</note> means a director or ruler, from the act of presiding <note>original: "magistrando"</note>. **Festus** says this, and **Virgil**: "the masters of the flock." **In the first place.** That is, first. **Lucilius** in book V.

<page-num>VIII</page-num>
<header>PROLOGUE IV</header>

<margin>
From what
sources the
names of
comedies
are taken
</margin>
**Wherefore, for which reason.** **Terence** in the *Andria* says: "Wherefore I am fairer to Pamphilus," and elsewhere: "They pretend to give birth for this reason, to frighten Chremes away." **The name of the comedy.** All written comedies are derived from four specific things:
1. **By Name**, such as *Phormio*, *Hecyra* <note>The Mother-in-Law</note>, *Curculio* <note>The Weevil</note>, or *Epidicus*.
2. **By Place**, such as *Andria* <note>The Girl from Andros</note>, *Leucadia*, or *Brundusina*.
3. **By Action**, such as *The Eunuch*, *The Comedy of Asses* <note>original: "Asinaria"</note>, or *The Captives*.
4. **By Outcome**, such as *Those Dying Together* <note>original: "Commorientes"</note>, *The Accusation*, or *The Self-Tormentor* <note>original: "Heautontimorumenos"</note>.
A prologue ought to indicate the name of the comedy if it recites the plot summary <note>original: "argumentum"</note>. **Plautus** <note>An early Roman playwright known for boisterous humor.</note> does this repeatedly in *The Comedy of Asses*.

->The writer himself confesses that for the ancients it was otherwise,<-
->And that there was a different custom for the moderns as well;<-
->That those former men wrote in trimeter and tetrameter,<-
->But these more recent ones in a freer, loose speech.<-
->The most recent author has departed from the path of both—<-
->This man of ours—for in all things he has used a unique<-
->Style, which the small number of acts necessitates,<-
->Driven by the brevity of the time of purification.<- <note>The "time of purification" likely refers to the festival of Februa, or February, suggesting the play was performed during a specific, short holiday.</note>

<margin>
To confess
To acknowledge
</margin>
**He confesses.** <note>original: "Fatetur"</note> He excuses his omission of the ancient comic custom, because he has written the comedy differently than has been the habit of others until now, using only a single quality of meter. Grammarians circulate a very vain distinction: they say that to "confess" <note>original: "fateri"</note> is to be compelled, while to "acknowledge" <note>original: "confiteri"</note> is voluntary. However, the author **Servius** <note>A famous 4th-century commentator on Virgil.</note> in his commentary on the *Bucolics* and the *Aeneid* (Books II, III, and IV) refutes this. There is a passage in **Cicero**'s speech *For Aulus Caecina* that strikes down and weakens this distinction; and in the speech *For Manilius* he says: "This I not only acknowledge <note>confiteor</note> but even openly declare <note>profiteor</note>." From the words of **Marcus Tullius Cicero**, we learn that to "confess" <note>fateri</note> can belong to one who affirms something of his own accord, and "to acknowledge" <note>confiteri</note> can be used for one who is compelled. **Catullus** in his *Marriage of Peleus and Thetis* writes: "Mothers at the funeral of their sons shall often confess <note>fatebuntur</note> his extraordinary virtues and famous deeds." And **Ovid** in the ninth book of the *Metamorphoses* <note>original: "lib. maioris"</note> says: "Or if shame held her mouth, a secret letter will confess <note>fatebitur</note> the hidden fires." From *fateor*, **Priscian** <note>A 6th-century Latin grammarian.</note> derives *confiteri* in his eighth book, because the compound changes the vowel 'a' to 'i'.

<margin>
Writers
Copyists
</margin>
**Writer.** <note>original: "Scriptor"</note> The poet is the author and founder of the song. Poets are called "writers." **Horace** refers to the "writer of the Trojan war," and "to write" is a verb that refers to the authors and creators of books. The common people incorrectly use the name "writers" for those they ought to call "copyists" <note>original: "librarios"</note>; for "to transcribe" <note>describere</note> and "to copy out" <note>exscribere</note> mean to transfer examples from one place to another.

<margin>
Ancient
Antiquarian
</margin>
**For the ancients.** To the old, the great, and the learned; for the word "ancient" <note>antiquus</note> signifies all these things. From this comes "antiquarian" <note>antiquarius</note>, a hunter of obsolete and excessively archaic words. In **Juvenal**, a woman is called an "antiquarian." "To have been for the ancients"—meaning the ancients possessed it. **Virgil** says: "I have [a pipe] of unequal [reeds]," etc. **Varro** <note>A prolific Roman scholar.</note> called an ingrained custom a "habit" <note>morem</note>. Virgil in Book VII says: "It is the custom in Hesperian Latium."

<margin>
Otherwise
</margin>
**For the moderns.** For the more recent ones. **Otherwise** <note>original: "Secus"</note> is a negative adverb. *Secus* contains a meaning of distinction or difference. Priscian, in Book XV, notes this. **That they wrote.** He shows in what manner the new writer has broken away from the ancients in his style.

<page-num>IX</page-num>
# ACT ONE

...and regarding the quality of the poem. In trimeter and tetrameter <note>Metrical lines consisting of three or four double-feet.</note>. **Priscian** <note>A famous 6th-century Latin grammarian.</note> wonders why some people either deny that there are meters in the comedies of **Terence**, or assert that they are a kind of secret known only to themselves and kept away from all other learned writers. To avoid the fault of either ignorance or arrogance, let us briefly explain the aforementioned meters from the authors, etc., which you will find for certain in the same **Priscian**, where he thoroughly teaches that there are hexameter and pentameter verses in comedies. Those called trimeters or tetrameters consist of either six or eight feet, just as monometers consist of two. These matters are very common and already quite obvious through everyday teaching. For meter gives birth to meter. **But these.** This refers to more recent writers, who, through a confusion of comic verse, more frequently produced <term>bad meters</term> <note>original: "cacometros", verses that fail to follow the strict rules of poetic rhythm</note>; **Rufinus** is the authority on this. **In loose speech.** By a "loosening," though not entirely released from metrical law. For even an orator constrains his 
<margin>Pedestrian speech</margin>speech—which is called **pedestrian** <note>original: "pedestris", meaning prose that stays "on the ground" compared to the "wings" of poetry</note>—with rhythmic feet. And **Diomedes** <note>A 4th-century grammarian.</note> established a long dissertation concerning oratorical feet. Nor does **Marcus Cicero** release the orator from rhythmic feet when he says, "The poet is very close to the orator, though a bit more loose in his freedom of words," etc., which you will find in *On the Orator*, Book II, chapter 1; and that it was the invention of **Isocrates** to join rhythms to loose words—he discusses these oratorical feet in the third volume of the same work.
**Speech.** **Donatus**, **Servius**, and other leading grammarians are criticized for a clumsy etymology of that word, because they said <term>speech</term> <note>original: "oratio"</note> is, as it were, the <term>reason of the mouth</term> <note>original: "oris rationem"</note>. In this, **Varro**—easily the prince of Latin literature—**Scaurus**, **Diomedes**, and **Licentius** agree. **More freely.** More loosely and without restraint. **The most recent of both.** That is, he yielded to both. **The most recent.** Meaning more recent. **Departed from the path.** That is, he turned aside from the path of the ancients. "He departed from the path" <note>original: "excessit uia"</note> is a refined expression. **Virgil** says: "And note, I depart from the region of the paths." The verb "I go out" <note>original: "egredior"</note> is also constructed with refined elegance in the ablative case; **Virgil** says: "There is a mound for those who have gone out from the city." **This man.** **Priscian** says figuratively that other pronouns are joined to different persons <term>appositively</term> <note>original: ἐπιταγὰματικῶς (epitagamatikos), a Greek grammatical term for placing words side-by-side for emphasis</note>, but he proves in the second book of his *Minor Works* that this is most fittingly done with the pronoun "himself." **For indeed.** This proves our departure from the usual custom. "For indeed" <note>original: "nanque"</note> is a causal particle that shows the reason for what came before; **Virgil** says: "For indeed to you the father of the gods and king of men..." **Priscian**, in book 15: **In all things.** He used a **unique style** <note>original: "unico genere"</note> in the verses. He made all his little verses of the same kind. For "style" <note>original: "genus"</note> sometimes signifies "quality," according to the testimony of **Victorinus**.
<margin>Acts of comedy</margin>**The small number of acts.** For the present comedy is completed in three acts, while others consist of five. Of these: 
1. The **first** explains the plot. 
2. The **second** desires to bring the business already begun toward an end. 
3. The **third** brings despair regarding the thing desired. 
4. The **fourth** provides a remedy for the intervening evil. 
5. The **fifth** leads everything to the hoped-for outcome. 
The **Chorus**, which in "old comedy" had created the distinction between acts, is excluded from "new" plays; therefore, as **Donatus** writes, it is very difficult to detect the intervals of the acts in them. **Driven.** Forced or constrained. **In the times of February.** Of winter. For the month of February is contained within the winter solstice; it is named from "purifying" <note>original: "purgando"</note>, 
<margin>February</margin>in which month women were once "februated" <note>purified</note> by the **Luperci** <note>Priests of the Roman festival of Lupercalia.</note> with the "garment of Juno"—that is, a goat skin. **Ovid** says: "The Roman fathers called the means of purification *Februa*."

<page-num>X</page-num>

# ACT ONE

Now he asks you to favor his silliness <note>original: "ineptitudini"</note>.
If he feels his first-fruits <note>original: "primicias", meaning his first attempt at writing</note> have pleased you.
He will henceforth make complete comedies.

<margin>
To favor
</margin>
**Now he asks you.** He turns to the listeners, from whom he seeks favor; it is as if he says, "the poet has managed his business in this way, now it will be your duty to favor him," which is to say, to be silent. **Festus** <note>Sextus Pompeius Festus, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian.</note> says that *to favor* <note>original: "fauere"</note> is to speak good things, but the ancient poets used it to mean *to be silent*. **Seneca** writes that whenever mention is made of sacred letters, "favor" is required—not, as most people think, derived from "goodwill," but as a command for silence so that the sacred rite can be performed properly without any wicked voice interrupting. For **Cicero** writes that the ancient Pythagoreans observed the voices not only of gods but also of men, which they call "omens." Because our ancestors thought these had power, they would preface all business to be done with the formula: "that it might be good, auspicious, happy, and fortunate." In divine matters performed publicly, people were commanded to "favor with their tongues" <note>original: "fauerent linguis", a ritual command for silence to avoid speaking bad omens</note>. Hence **Hannibal**, about to depart, says to the wife he had left on the Carthaginian shore: "Spare everything, including your tears, most faithful wife," in **Silius Italicus**, Book 3 of the *Punic War*; and **Virgil**: "He binds him with the first omens," and in Book 5: "Favor me with your mouths, all of you," etc. **Silliness.** In follies. **First-fruits.** The first fruit of his labor in this matter. **Priscian** and **Diomedes** are the authorities stating that the word "first-fruits" <note>original: "primicię"</note> is derived from "first," is feminine in gender, and is expressed only in the plural. Properly, they are called the first crops offered to the immortal gods, but authors use the term for the first "taste" or libation of any matter. **Statius** in Book 11: "Alas for me, the first-fruits of arms." **Complete.** Perfect, whole, consisting of five acts, of the larger sort, and lacking nothing.

# ACT ONE

->THE GLUTTON<- <note>original: "HELVO". In Latin, a "heluo" is a glutton or a gormandizer; here it serves as the character's name.</note>

<margin>c</margin> Since for me and mine—as one who has looked down on my birth—
My father, paternal uncle, great-great-great-grandfathers, grandfathers,
Great-great-grandfathers, and likewise great-great-great-great-grandfathers, and all this
Growing-together of the family tree, and this
Splendor of our house, even from my greatest paternal uncles,
I must give thanks to two goddesses:
To Juno and her first-bearing attendant
Lucina <note>The Roman goddess of childbirth.</note>, because I was born of that lineage
Which is both the most noted and the most famous,
That I might be a true glutton from a line of gluttons, and no bastard.

<summary>This page concludes the scholarly commentary on the play's prologue, focusing on the ritual meaning of "favor" (silence) and the definition of "first-fruits" of labor. It then introduces the first scene, featuring a monologue by a character named "The Glutton" (Heluo) who boasts of his long ancestral line of epicures.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the general introductory remarks about the prologue into the specific analysis of the first scene of Act One. It introduces the character Heluo and begins a detailed philological discussion on character naming conventions and the etymology of terms related to birth and contemplation, maintaining the scholarly apparatus established in the preceding pages.</meta>

# ACT ONE

In this introduction <note>original: "prothesi"</note>, which does not narrate the plot through <margin>introductory character <note>original: "προατικὸν πρόσωπον"</note></margin> an outside persona (since the prologue has already instructed the listener by reciting the plot), as you will frequently find in the works of Terence, the poet instead introduces one who begins the play: Heluo. To this character, the poet most fittingly grants a speech that is wonderfully delightful in its comic wit, suited to his appearance, age, and office. The law of the persona is strictly observed. <margin>Names of characters in comedies</margin> However, one must remember that the names of characters in comedies are not without reason and etymology. For it is absurd for a comic poet to give a character an unsuitable name, or a duty that differs from the name. And our character's name is Heluo, a word which signifies a greedy devourer <margin>Heluo</margin> and a glutton. He is called by this term because he does not merely nibble at his goods, but wastefully and intemperately removes and swallows them down in expensive meals and foods—from the verb *heluando* (to gluttonize), a word strengthened by the spirit of a more vigorous sound. <margin>To gluttonize <note>original: "Heluare"</note></margin> **Catullus** says of Gaius Caesar: "Has he gluttonized too little?" **Varro**, in his satire *On foods and dishes* <note>original: "πεϱὶ ἐδεσμάτων"</note>, elegantly and skillfully described the exquisite delights of gluttons. We learn that the ancient laws punished those who, gluttonizing in their ample inheritances, washed away their family and money in the whirlpools of banquets, according to **Atteius Capito**. A verse of **Lucilius** became famous: <margin>Lurcho <note>A slang term for a glutton or "greedy-gut."</note></margin> "Live, you lurchos, you gluttons, live, you bellies!" <margin>gluttons</margin> Here, "bellies" (if we trust **Donatus** <note>A famous 4th-century grammarian and commentator on Terence.</note>) is used for greedy parasites, whose god is their belly. <margin>bellies.</margin>

**When me and mine.** This parasite trifles in a very vivid way; the name given to him fits his speech and life perfectly. He proposes a brief little speech about things he wishes to remember. **By himself.** <margin>Solitude</margin> It is solitude that makes a man return to himself, yet he does not withdraw from comic wit, with which he wonderfully delights the spectators by congratulating himself that he has by no means departed or degenerated from the splendor or zeal of his ancestors. He proves this very thing with small arguments, namely from the attributes of his person and lineage. For children are usually believed to be like their parents. Therefore he says, <margin>I contemplate</margin> **When I contemplate myself.** I behold, I gaze upon, I inspect. The word is derived from *templum* <note>temple</note>, that is, <margin>Temple</margin> a place that can be seen from all sides or from which every part can be seen, according to **Festus**. **By birth** <note>original: "Natalibus"</note>, by the brilliance of birth from the nobility of lineage. We accept "births" in the plural number to mean nobility of lineage. **Apuleius** says, "There was in the next city a youth of very noble birth." They are, however, <margin>indifferent things <note>original: "τῶν μέσων"</note></margin> among the "indifferent things." For we praise births as honorable, or defame them as sordid. **Pliny the Younger** in the third book of his letters; there is a title among the legal consultants regarding the "restoration of births," by which it is clearly shown that he who has been restored to his birthright enjoys the law of free birth <note>original: "ingenuitatis"</note>, as if the name of birth signifies free status itself. Grammarians want *natalia* to signify what *natalis* does, which is the birthday; **Donatus** objects to both being turned into a substantive from an adjective in his commentary on *Phormio*. To that line of **Virgil**, "my birthday is Iollas," he responds that Virgil was serving the character of a rustic, to whom he did not add the word "day." **Terence** wrote: "Where was the boy's birthday?" **Plautus** supports Donatus in the *Captives*, for he says, "because it is my birthday." This poet, whom I call the delight of the Latin language...

<sig>B ii</sig>

It provides a scholarly etymological breakdown of terms for gluttony, contemplation, and noble birth, citing numerous Roman authorities.</summary>

# ON COMEDY XII

<margin>
Reason
Authority
</margin>
I would value **reason** more than all the stale and foul-smelling definitions of the minor grammarians. For the **authority** of the greatest men of eloquence must be held as reason itself, and one must flee to it as if to an anchor. **A father of a father.** He is a *patrimus* <note>original: "patrimus"</note> who, although he is a father himself, still has another father living. **Great-grandfathers.** They are called *proavi* <note>original: "proaui"</note> as if they were "near grandfathers" <note>original: "prope auos"</note>. **Great-great-great-grandfathers** <note>original: "tritaui"</note> are named as if "tetra-grandfathers," because the *tritavus* is the fourth degree from the grandfather; the ancients called this the *stritavus*, perhaps by that custom where they said *silatum* for *latum* (wide), *stlocum* for *locum* (place), and *stlitem* for *litem* (a lawsuit). **Great-great-grandfathers.** The *atavus* is so named because *ata*, meaning father, is the father of the grandfather, as children are often accustomed to say. **Festus** <note>Sextus Pompeius Festus, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian</note> investigates the origin of the name for grandfathers <note>original: "auorum"</note> from three sources. It is the opinion of others that "grandfather" (*avus*) is derived from "age" (*aevum*), because he is older than the father. But "father" (*pater*) comes from "opening" <note>original: "patefaciendo"</note>, because he opens up his own seed, or from "performing" <note>original: "patrando"</note>.

<margin>
A father with a living father
Great-grandfathers
Great-great-great-grandfathers
Ancient great-great-great-grandfather
Great-great-grandfathers
</margin>

<margin>
Great-great-grandfather
</margin>
For "to perform" (*patrare*) means to give effort to children, a word which, according to **Quintilian**, was spoken in a holy and honest sense among the ancients. From this, **Sallust** was mocked because he said "to perform a war" <note>original: "patrare bellum"</note>. **Great-great-grandfather** <note>original: "abauum"</note>, meaning one who is now far off from the grandfather himself.

<margin>
Bud or seed
Full brother
The act of budding together
</margin>
**A budding of the same seed.** This refers to the series and offshoot of our lineage and race. Just as one born from a royal stock calculates every line of his blood most exactly. A **bud** (*germen*) is born from the shoots of trees, which is why those born from the same stock are called **full brothers** (*germani*). According to **Festus**, a "budding together" (*congerminatio*) is a shared kinship and a mutual connection of blood.

<margin>
Full brother
Full sister
A little boaster
</margin>
The ancients were accustomed to say **full brother** (*frater germanus*), because the term "full brother" implies more than if one were to simply say "brother." **Terence** says: *If I loved you in the place of a full brother.* **Plautus** in *The Captives*: *Look, your full brother is inside,* and in *The Pot of Gold*: *as is right for a full sister.* And he calls this the **splendor of our house**; he speaks of this "splendor" like a **little boaster** <note>original: "iactanticulus"</note>, quite proudly, as if to say: "Such a rare and celebrated honor in one family, both in my lineage and especially shining in me; therefore, I must give thanks above all to the immortal gods that I have not degenerated from the fame of my parents."

<margin>
House or Family
From as far as
</margin>
**House.** Meaning family. As **Virgil** says: *Here the house of Aeneas shall rule over all shores.* **Splendor** pertains to the greatness of honor.

<margin>
Up to
</margin>
This word is commonly found everywhere in **Marcus Tullius Cicero** and is most well-worn. **From as far as** (*abusque*) and **up to** (*adusque*) are adverbs of place. For since a preposition never clings to another preposition, *abusque* and *adusque* are freely admitted. **Apuleius** writes: *Up to the boundary of the neck.* It is certain, however, that prepositions are joined to adverbs through composition.

<margin>
From below
Greatest
Paternal uncle
Greater paternal uncle
</margin>
For they cannot stand separately. Therefore, words like *defursum* (from below), *desubito* (suddenly), *derepente* (unexpectedly), *exinde* (from then), *dehinc* (henceforth), *amane* (from the morning), *abhinc* (from this time), *posthac* (after this), *antehac* (before this), and *deimproviso* (unforeseen)—wherever we read them, I take them under one accent. However, in Plautus’s *The Captives*, it seems to be read incorrectly as "from suddenly." **Greatest paternal uncles**, not only "greater" but "greatest," so that he may trace the splendor of his blood back as far as possible and through all branches. We call a father's brother a **paternal uncle** (*patruus*). We call a grandfather's or grandmother's paternal uncle a **greater paternal uncle**. Here he has woven his speech starting from the "greatest," with a pleasant wit; he said "greatest" (*maximis*) in the ancient manner, a matter excused by the kinship of the vowels *i* and *u*. **Priscian** is the authority on this, noting that the ancients said *carnufex* (executioner) and *pessumus* (worst) <note>instead of the later *carnifex* and *pessimus*</note>. **Quintilian** in the first book of his *Institutes* records that through **Gaius Caesar** <note>Julius Caesar</note>, who was most learned in the grammatical art, the letter *u* was changed in certain words...

<margin>
Executioner
Worst
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's opening act. # ACT ONE

<margin>Most plainly?</margin> ...stated. And Plautus in the *Captives* <note>original: "Captiuis"</note> uses "most plainly" <note>original: "planissume"</note> instead of the standard "most plainly" <note>original: "planissime"</note>, and elsewhere. Tailor and 
<margin>Thanks</margin> sower of crimes and the greatest reaper <note>original: "Sartor satorque scelerum & messor maxume"</note>, thanks must be held <note>original: "Grates habendum"</note>. It is a wonder that even in actors there exists a sense of gratitude. But he returned to himself, as we said above, having set aside the company of his accomplices. "Thanks" <note>original: "Grates"</note> is used only in the feminine gender and in the plural, on the authority of Diomedes <note>a 4th-century Latin grammarian</note>; how "holding thanks," "giving thanks," and "returning thanks" <note>original: "habere grates agere et referre"</note> differ in many ways can be seen among the writers on elegance. But he said "it is necessary to hold thanks" <note>original: "habendum grates"</note>; one must not shun this phrasing, lest it seem a grammatical error <note>original: "solœcismus"</note> to some little-bearded grammarian. This word "holding" <note>original: "habendi"</note> does not refer back to "thanks," as it appears to those reading hastily and carelessly, nor is it placed as a participle,
<margin>Indefinite</margin> but it is an "indefinite," which the Greeks call an *aparemphaton* <note>an infinitive form used impersonally</note>, serving neither numbers nor genders, but free and impartial on all sides. Cicero, in his *Against Verres*, says: "they hope this matter will be a protection to them" <note>original: "hanc sibi rem præsidio sperant futurum," where "futurum" is used indefinitely</note>. And in the book of Valerius Antias: "The soothsayers said all would proceed according to the opinion" <note>original: "omnia ex sententia processurum"</note>. **To the two goddesses** <note>original: "Duabus deis"</note>. Correctly, for if he had said "to the gods" <note>original: "diis"</note> by synaeresis <note>the blending of two vowel sounds into one</note>, the verse would not have stood. Lucan in the sixth book of the *Pharsalia*: "O would that to the heavenly goddesses..." <note>original: "O utinā cœlicę deis"</note>. Priscian <note>a 6th-century grammarian</note> in book seven, on the second order of the dative case: 
<margin>To the gods / To the goddesses</margin> we say "to the gods" <note>original: "deis"</note> by analogy, but for the sake of distinguishing sex, contrary to reason 
<margin>Companion-born / Juno / Lucina</margin> and analogy, "to the goddesses" <note>original: "deabus"</note> is said. **To Juno and her follower.** The one who is "companion-born" <note>original: "socigenæ"</note> makes Lucina her follower. Servius <note>a famous commentator on Virgil</note> thinks Juno and Lucina <note>the goddess of childbirth</note> are the same. This is also what Terence is thought to have felt in his play *The Girl from Andros*. Marcus Cicero derives the name of Juno from "helping" <note>original: "iuuando"</note>, with whom Varro agrees. Other writers derive it as if from "Janona," that is, a "door" <note>original: "ianuam"</note>, because she opens the gates of mothers for those being born; and Ovid opines that she is called the "Mother Lucina" from the grove <note>original: "luco"</note> of Mars. Macrobius <note>a 5th-century philosopher and grammarian</note> says more in his seventh book. He appropriately joined Lucina to Juno as a follower, either because marriages precede them (over which Juno *Pronuba* presides) and Lucina follows (that is, the birth through which the infant comes forth into the light), or because it is always necessary for those who help parents to be present at hand; for this reason we read that nurses and attendants were called "followers" <note>original: "asseclas"</note>. 
<margin>Follower / First-time mother / Woman in labor</margin> However, twin goddesses are invoked in labor, for the safety of both the one being born and the one giving birth is required. **First-time mothers** <note>original: "Primíperæ"</note>. A compound word just like "woman in labor" <note>original: "puerpera"</note>. Pliny in book eight: "First-time mothers" <note>original: "Primíperæ"</note> are the primary and leading ones among those giving birth. Festus says that Venus presides over those giving birth. **Because of this**, he is about to explain the reason why he gives such thanks to the principal goddesses. **By that kinship** <note>original: "ea parentela"</note>, from the parents, whom we ought to understand as being of both sexes. On the testimony of Ulpian <note>a Roman jurist</note>, kinship <note>original: "parentela"</note> is established by usage. Julius Capitolinus
<margin>Kinship / Notorious / Famous / Renowned</margin> says: "Because of his eloquence, he thought him worthy of his kinship." **Most noted, most famous.** Both words can be taken in both a good and a bad sense. Apuleius in the eleventh volume of the *Metamorphoses* uses "famous" <note>original: "Famosum"</note> with praise and in a good sense, as does Cicero in his speech against Valerius, Firmicus Maternus in the second book of his *Mathesis*, and Pliny in his letters. In a bad sense, Cicero in book two of *On the Orator*: "For my mother forbade me to approach famous <note>here meaning 'notorious'</note> women." "Renowned" <note>original: "Famigerabilis"</note> stands for clear and illustrious, on the authority of Apuleius in book two. But "rumor-mongers" <note>original: "Famigeratores"</note> is used in a bad sense. Plautus in the *Trinummus*: "A glutton from gluttons" <note>original: "Ex heluonibus heluo"</note>, most excellently. For as Columella <note>a writer on agriculture</note> writes, there is no doubt that nature itself wanted the offspring to be similar to the mother, and the marks of the parents for the most part cling to the children.

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Act One, moving from the grammar of the prologue to the specific characterization of Heluo, the glutton. The commentator justifies the use of terms for illegitimacy and the character's excessive language by citing classical poets and ancient legal or linguistic distinctions.</meta>

# ACT ONE

<margin>
Illegitimate
Of mixed-birth
Mamzer
Children of the Wind
</margin>
The Satirist <note>Juvenal, a Roman poet known for his biting social commentary</note> alludes to this sentiment. Since he had produced a son similar to himself, not only in body but also in character. Horace <note>A leading Roman lyric poet</note> says: "The brave are created by the brave" <note>original: "Fortes creātur fortibus"</note>. An illegitimate child <note>original: "Spurius"</note> is called *nothus* in Greek. But some wish to distinguish between a *spurius* and a *nothus*, such that a *spurius* is born of an ignoble father, while a *nothus* is ignoble from the mother's side. Sacred Scripture calls the illegitimate child a *mamzer* <note>original: "mamzerē"</note> in the Hebrew tongue, in the twenty-third chapter of Deuteronomy, from the word *zar*, which signifies "alien" or "strange." The word *spurius* comes from the "seed" <note>original: "ἀπὸ τοῦ σπόρου"</note>, that is, from the seed or from the female nature; or rather, it comes from the Latin word for "filthy" <note>original: "spurco"</note> and "unclean." They are also called "children of the west wind" <note>original: "fauonii"</note>, as they are of an uncertain father. He uses "I might be" <note>original: "Siem"</note> for "I am" <note>original: "sim"</note> as a comic touch.

->Indeed I imitate ancestral customs, I drink,<-
->I drink again, I devour, I gulp down, as all those<-
->of the same name among my ancestors are accustomed to do.<-
->Yet I myself gorge more than the rest<-
->and I glut myself; therefore, Glutton is my name.<-

<margin>
Ancestral
Grandfatherly
Again
Fond of drinking
Drunken
Greed for drink
Greed for food

Wine

Of/From

Accustomed
Of the same name

To gorge
To glut oneself

My name is Glutton
</margin>
Indeed, through the pursuit of character, deeds, habit, and imitation, he proves by his likeness that he has in no way degenerated from the seed of his parents, and he does this by an attribute of his persona: namely, his obsession. "Indeed" <note>original: "Equidem"</note> means "I, for my part." "Ancestral" <note>original: "Patritos"</note> means "belonging to a father." Cicero, in his *Tusculan Disputations*, speaks of "that ancestral and grandfatherly philosophy" <note>original: "Patritā illam & auitā ... philosophiā"</note> (as Theophrastus calls it). "I drink, I drink again" <note>original: "Bibo rebibo"</note>. The particle "re" is iterative <note>indicates repetition</note>. Horace says: "you may drink back <note>re-drink</note> largely and kindly." Sometimes it is redundant, as in "I report" <note>original: "renuncio"</note> for "I announce" <note>original: "nuncio"</note>; Virgil says: "Aeneas remained" <note>original: "Restitit Æneas"</note>, and Cicero: "it is reported to me" <note>original: "renunciat̄ mihi"</note>. There are also places where "re" in composition signifies the opposite, as Horace says: "Provided you become my friend with your charms recanted" <note>original: "recantatis"</note>, that is, sung in the opposite direction. He shows himself to be a "drinker" <note>original: "Bibacem"</note> and "drunken" <note>original: "bibosum"</note> from "drinking-greed" <note>original: "bibesia"</note>, a word by which the ancients wished to signify a craving for drinking, just as "eating-greed" <note>original: "peredia"</note> refers to eating. "I drink, I drink again." He rightly repeats it, for it is sweet to drink, but most foul to become a drunkard. Plautus says of wine: "Like a crafty wrestler, it first trips the feet" <note>original: "pedes captat primo luctator dolosus"</note>. Terence says: "Wine has conquered me." To a certain boaster who said he could drink much without getting drunk, Isocrates responded: "A mule does this as well." "I devour, I gulp down" <note>original: "Voro deuoro"</note>. The "de" increases the meaning, as in "to gape wide" <note>original: "dehiscere"</note> or "deeply despised" <note>original: "despretus"</note>, that is, greatly despised. Servius teaches that the particle "de" sometimes diminishes and sometimes increases. "Is accustomed" <note>original: "Assolet"</note> means he grows used to it much, usually, and often. "Of the same name" <note>original: "Cognomines"</note> refers to men of the same pursuit and office, and those most like me, fellow-drinkers and "munchers" <note>original: "māducones"</note>. "Yet I more" <note>original: "Plus tamen"</note>. Exempt from the common lot of the illegitimate, which he considered filthy by comparison to himself, he now shows that he excels those born of the same lineage, being far more vehement in the work of his office than other devourers. "I gorge" <note>original: "Ingurgito"</note> means I drain greedily and cast it as if into a "whirlpool" <note>original: "gurgitē"</note> or abyss. Apuleius says: "Nothing of the matter which you are accustomed to gorge boldly into your raging belly." "I glut myself" <note>original: "Heluor"</note> means I devour and consume, I swallow down immoderately. The Greeks call this gluttony "profligacy" <note>original: "ἀσωτίαν"</note>. Marcus Cicero mentions that word in his speech *Against Piso*. "My name is Glutton" <note>original: "Nomen est mihi heluo"</note>, that is, a tavern-hunter, a shop-crawler, and a debauchee. He said "Glutton" in the nominative case. Plautus’s Mercury says his name is "of Mercury" in the genitive case. Priscian, in his work on construction, clearly teaches that this phrase "my name is..." can stand with almost any case. Plautus says in *The Ass-Dealer*: "the name of this play in Greek is *Onagos*."

<meta>This page continues the philological and moral commentary on the first act of the play. Having introduced the gluttonous character Heluo on the previous page, the commentator now examines Heluo's opening lines, which celebrate wine and the physical mechanics of swallowing, while weaving in classical warnings against intoxication and anatomical observations about the throat.</meta>

# ACT ONE

## Whenever there is some wine, if there is a plenty of it
## I do not stop until my whole gullet
## Is pregnant from the bottom and across the throat; if there is a scarcity,
## I either lap it up or lick it, as long as its keen scent lingers.

<margin>The use of wine</margin>
**Whenever there is some wine.** Regarding the intemperate use of wine, Androcydes <note>A physician and advisor to Alexander the Great</note>, famous for his wisdom, forbade the Macedonian King Alexander using these words: "King, when you are about to drink wine, remember that you are drinking the blood of the earth." Just as hemlock is poison to a man, so wine is like hemlock <note>Meaning it is toxic in excess</note>. Alexander did not obey this prohibition. We read that Lacydes of Cyrene <note>An Academic philosopher</note> died of paralysis brought on by immoderate wine drinking. The philosopher Antisthenes left behind a volume on the use of wine, and also on drunkenness. Among the three questions proposed by Darius, son of Hystaspes, one concerns the strength of wine, found in the Book of Esdras, chapters 3 and 4 <note>References to the apocryphal 1 Esdras</note>, and in the *Antiquities* of Josephus, book 11, chapter 4. The Wise Man <note>Solomon, in the Book of Proverbs</note> says: "Wine is a luxurious thing and drunkenness is riotous," etc. If you wish to settle a hangover <note>original: "crapulam"</note>, eat raw cabbage with vinegar before dinner; you may then drink as much as you like, and it will be as if you had drunk nothing. The authors of this advice are Cato and Aristotle. <margin>Settling a hangover</margin> <margin>The power of Bacchus.</margin> Aesop writes that Bacchus <note>The god of wine</note> possesses a triple power: "the first of pleasure, the second of drunkenness, the third of insolence and madness" <note>original: "τὴν μὲν πρώτην ἡδονῆς τὴν δὲ δευτέραν μέθης τὴν δὲ τρίτην ὕβρεως"</note>. Pliny says that from wine comes the forgetting of all things and the death of memory. By wine, wisdom is obscured, the intellect is overwhelmed, and the vigor of the mind is hindered. In this vein, Gellius <note>Aulus Gellius, a Roman grammarian</note> says that with frequent and enormous cups, he "drenched his entire intellect." <margin>Plenty.</margin> **Copia.** That is, abundance. It also signifies an "army" when used in the singular, which in Greek is called <term>enikōs</term> <note>original: "ἐνικῶσ"; meaning "singularly"</note>. Sallust writes "from every supply of Catiline"; Virgil in the second book of the *Aeneid*.

<margin>Not</margin>
<margin>Stop</margin>
**Haud cesso.** "I do not stop at all." Caper <note>Flavius Caper, a Roman grammarian</note> instructs that *haud* should be written with a *d* when it is a negative adverb, but it ends with the letter *t* when it is a disjunctive conjunction, though it is not aspirated at the beginning. "The lazy man stops (*cessat*), the tired man rests (*quiescit*)": Donatus distinguishes between these words in this manner. **The whole gullet.** In Greek it is called <term>tros</term> <note>original: "τρως"; likely referring to the throat or pharynx</note>. It should be known that there are two pipes, like little channels, proceeding downward from the throat. Through one of these, food and drink slide down into the stomach. From this, it is called the "food pipe." Through the other, breath passes from the mouth into the lung, and from there back into the mouth and nostrils; the passage of the voice also occurs through this same path. This pipe is named the "spiritual pipe" because of the breath <note>original: "spiritus"</note>, which the Greeks call the <term>tracheia arteria</term> <note>original: "τραχεῖαν ἀρτηρίαν"; the windpipe or trachea</note>. Cornelius Celsus calls it the "rough artery." Marcus Varro mentions it in the chapter on goats and kids. Lactantius, in his book *On the Workmanship of God*, calls it the "gullet" <note>original: "gurgulio"</note>. To ensure the air passage is not blocked while we eat, nature has provided a "little tongue" between the two pipes, the epiglottis. By more recent writers, it is called by the new term *sublinguium*, but by suitable authors it is called the "lesser tongue," which covers the spiritual path during eating and drinking, so that food does not wander into that narrow space. <margin>Spiritual pipe.</margin> <margin>Rough</margin> <margin>Gullet.</margin> <margin>Epiglottis</margin> <margin>Sublinguium</margin> <margin>Lesser tongue</margin> **Pregnans.** Filled up, swelling, heavy. *Pregnans* <note>pregnant/swollen</note> is properly said before a birth or before the mother. Terence in *The Lady of Andros*: "she was taken away from you while pregnant." <margin>Pregnant.</margin>

<summary>This page analyzes Heluo's boast about his drinking habits, using the opportunity to provide a moral commentary on the dangers of wine and a detailed anatomical explanation of the throat and epiglottis. It cites various classical authorities to explain the vocabulary and the physical mechanics of swallowing and breathing.</summary>
<keywords>wine, temperance, Alexander the Great, anatomy, trachea, epiglottis, Aesop, Pliny, Bacchus, gluttony, Latin grammar</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the character Heluo's speech. Having discussed the physical mechanics of drinking and the dangers of wine on the previous page, the author now turns to the anatomy of the throat and the social habits of the "parasite"—a stock character in classical comedy known for trading wit for food and drink.</meta>

.XVI

# ACT ONE

<meta>Catchword: nus (completing the word "clamdestinus" from the previous page's end)</meta>

<margin>Before</margin>
**Before.** For "before" <note>original: "Prę"</note> signifies "ahead of." **Across the throat.** This refers to the food pipe <note>original: "fistulā cibalis"</note>, which is called the "stomach" by Cornelius Celsus <note>A 1st-century Roman encyclopedist known for his medical treatise *De Medicina*</note>, the most elegant of Latin medical writers. For it is connected to the stomach. The jurist Ulpian <note>A famous Roman legal scholar from the 3rd century AD</note> mentions the gullet in the Digest under the Aquilian law. If there is a scarcity, he discusses the reasoning of his duty separately so that he may instruct 
<margin>Stomach</margin>
thoroughly and more precisely. **I lap up or I lick.** I consume greedily or I am fond of delicacies. Marcus Varro <note>An ancient Roman scholar and writer</note> thinks that "to lick" <note>original: "lingere"</note> comes from the Greek, because they say <term>to lick with the tongue</term> <note>original: "λιγμασθαι"</note>.
<margin>I lick</margin>
We are said to "lap" and "lick" when we touch something gently with the tongue. Horace says: "the places which the legendary Hydaspes laps"; Virgil: "it laps the stars." **While.** 
<margin>I lap</margin>
Until, or as long as. **Keen scent.** Attractive and traceable for my own pleasure.

->But not (as is the custom for most) in hidden<-
->And solitary places or caves<-
->Or secretly from men in some innermost pantry<-
->This is unseemly and detestable for a man.<-
->But with good children and companions<-
->Similar to myself, and likewise with Gnatos<-
->Just as I am, therefore I seek my fellow tribesmen here:<-
->Parasites, who practice the predatory art<-
->Of bones and jars, where the wine is pure,<-
->Who sometimes with more soothing jokes<-
->Steal the minds and senses of men.<-

**But not as is the custom for most.** He accuses others because he thinks it adds more to his own glory and praise. **But not.** That is, I do not gorge myself. Indeed, "but" <note>original: "at"</note> is a copulative 
<margin>But</margin>
particle that sometimes serves only for decoration. Priscian <note>A primary Latin grammarian of the late Roman Empire</note> is the authority that it was used by the Latins for "at least" or "but." It is established that it is a conjunction of oppositional power, which the Greeks call <term>ambiguous</term> <note>original: "επαμφιβωματικως"; referring to a word that can be used in two ways</note>. Virgil: "But the queen, heavy with care..." Priscian 
<margin>Most</margin>
examines this. **As is the custom for most.** This is a parenthesis, that is, an interpolation. The ancients sometimes uttered the word "most" <note>original: "plæriſq;"</note> without a conjunction. Cato says in 
the first book of *Origins*: "the plain of the territory which the Volsci held was mostly [original: *plerus*] Aborigine." **In hidden and solitary places.** That is, in corners, which is the habit of the most greedy 
gluttons <note>original: "lurchonū"</note>. **Or caves.** This word is of uncertain gender; Donatus is the authority, and Priscian alludes to this in his sixth book, where he strives to prove the gender with many authorities. **Secretly from men.** That is, in hidden places, separated and removed from people.
<margin>Secretly</margin>
To say "secretly from men" <note>original: "clam uiros"</note> with the accusative case is grammatically correct. Terence: "nor is it hidden from you." Plautus in the *Captives*: "secretly from his father." 
<margin>Secretly (adverb)</margin>
From this, grammarians derive "secretly" <note>original: "clanculū"</note> and "clandestine" <note>original: "clamdeſtinus"</note> with a sharp penultimate syllable. Lucretius: "And often flying in clandestine 
<margin>Clandestine</margin>
shapes"; likewise in the first volume: "To employ a clandestine and blind nature"; and elsewhere: "They signify that clandestine and blind things lie beneath."

<summary>This page provides a philological analysis of Heluo's speech, defining terms related to the throat and consumption while contrasting the social nature of the parasite's drinking with the solitary habits of gluttons. It cites numerous classical authorities, including Virgil, Horace, and various Roman grammarians, to explain Latin syntax and etymology.</summary>
<keywords>philology, parasite, gluttony, anatomy, Latin grammar, Priscian, Lucretius, etymology, social drinking</keywords>
