<meta>This page continues the linguistic and cultural commentary on the character Heluo. The previous page ended with the syllable "clandesti-", which is completed here as "clandestinus" (secret). The author continues to cite classical authorities like Donatus, Cicero, and Horace to define terms related to private storage, social companions, and professional jealousy.</meta>

XVII

# ACT ONE

<margin>
By force
Secretly
By entreaty
Inwardly
</margin>
**-ly** <note>original: "-nus", completing the word "clandestinus" or "secretly" from the previous page</note> **Donatus** <note>A 4th-century Roman grammarian and teacher of St. Jerome</note> **says there are those for whom business is not conducted properly: by force, secretly, and by entreaty.** <note>original: "vi, clam, & precario"</note> **Through these three means, one possesses in bad faith according to the law.** **Most inward** <note>original: "Penitissimo"</note> **means most intimate, most remote, and most secret; the word is derived from the adverb "inwardly"** <note>original: "penitus"</note>. **Apuleius** <note>Author of *The Golden Ass*</note> **writes: "you will remember clearly and always keep it stored in your inward mind." Plautus** <note>Roman comic playwright</note> **writes: "Come, I beg you by Hercules, from the very depths of your throat." Polished Latin writers use both the comparative and superlative forms. Aulus Gellius** <note>Author of *Attic Nights*</note> **notes: "marshes in the more inward parts, far from our world."**

<margin>
Pantry
</margin>
**Pantry.** <note>original: "Penu"</note> **A provision-room or cupboard in which the "penus"** <note>food stores</note> **is placed. The ancients used various grammatical genders for this word—masculine, feminine, or neuter—by which they signified things fit to be eaten. According to Aulus Gellius, these are all the foods and drinks** <note>original: "esculenta et potulenta"</note> **stored for the use of a whole year, which we call "provisions"** <note>original: "cibaria"</note> **using a common and worn-out word. Horace says: "let him carry grain and a pantry." But here he uses it for a storage cellar. Servius** <note>A famous commentator on Virgil</note> **notes many things in the books of the *Aeneid*: the ancients spoke of the "steward" and the "butler"** <note>original: "promum & condum"</note> **as those whom we now**

<margin>
Cellar
</margin>
**call a "cellarer,"** <note>original: "cellarium"</note> **as my most delightful teacher Johannes Reuchlin** <note>A famous German humanist and scholar (1455–1522) who helped revive the study of Greek and Hebrew in Europe</note> **left written with great clarity in his *Hebrew Rudiments*. In my opinion, I judge this work to be most worthy of the literary senate, for I have considered nothing to be more ancient.**

<margin>
Propriety
</margin>
**This is unseemly** <note>original: "indecorum"</note>—**vile, dishonest, and unbecoming. The Greeks call this** <term>propriety</term> <note>original: "to prepon"</note> **which we name "becomingly." Marcus Cicero discusses this at length in the first volume of *On Duties*, when he is about to speak on temperance. For a man who is an actor and a glutton** <note>original: "histrioni & heluoni"</note>, **who has most stubbornly emasculated his face, stripped of all shame and released into every kind of disgrace...** **Detestable** <note>original: "Detestabile"</note> **means abominable and worthy of being**

<margin>
Daughter
Companions
Fellowship
</margin>
**loathed by our testimony. Horace writes: "And wars loathed by mothers." "Daughters"** <note>original: "Natabus"</note> **is a respectable term to use, but because he joined it to "those like me," the meaning is plain. "Nata"** <note>daughter</note> **becomes "natabus" and "natis." Plautus says in *Stichus*: "He who is such toward his daughters." "Companions"** <note>original: "Sodalibus"</note> **refers to tent-mates, table-mates, or accomplices, because they sit and eat together, or because they feed on what is given by their own group, or because they take turns advising each other on what is useful. Cicero says in the second book of *On the Orator*: "That I should nevertheless defend him who was my companion." Apuleius, in his tenth volume, calls his ass a "companion" because they were together. In Greek, a table-companion is a** <term>syssitos</term>, **fellowship is** <term>syssitia</term>, **and the place of dining or the mess-hall is a** <term>syssiton</term>. **Marcus Cicero writes in his speech *For Plancius* that the "crime of sodality"** <note>forming illegal political clubs</note> **was punishable under the law against electoral corruption. "And to those like me"** <note>original: "Meiqꝫ consimilibus"</note> **refers to those not unlike my own manners.**

<margin>
Likeness of
manners
</margin>
**This is most justly said, for a likeness of manners is the most tenacious glue for joining a friendship. Just as good manners reconcile the honest, so do bad manners bring together the wicked. Whence Marcus Tullius** <note>Cicero</note> **most truly says in *On Friendship*: "Neither can the good be friends to the wicked, nor the wicked to the good, since between them there is the greatest distance of manners and interests." Thus Livy rightly says that "the bad is most suited to the bad." Plato writes that the understanding of likeness is implanted in all animals, and because of this, they feel an affinity for those of the same kind. But as the saying goes,** <term>potter is angry with potter, and builder with builder, and the beggar envies the beggar, and the singer the singer</term> <note>original: "kai kerameus keramei kotei..."</note>—**that is,**

<margin>
Potter
hates potter
</margin>
**the potter resents the potter and the craftsman the craftsman, and the doctor envies the doctor and the singer the singer. As Hesiod says, this happens for the sake of a third thing—namely, profit and gain. There are some here who say that Hesiod was remembering grammarians, and thus they clearly confess that they themselves are not grammarians.**

It notably includes a respectful reference to the humanist Johannes Reuchlin and discusses the classical idea that "like attracts like" in friendship, while also noting professional rivalry through a Greek proverb from Hesiod.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the detailed commentary on the character Heluo and the social circles of gluttons and dependents. While the previous page focused on private storage and the legal definitions of possession, this page shifts to the social aspect: the "Gnatos" or parasites who provide company in exchange for food. It draws heavily on classical Roman comedy and Greek lexicography to define the "art of the parasite."</meta>

XVIII

# ACT ONE XVIII

<margin>To the Gnatos. I go.</margin> **To the Gnatos.** <note>original: "Gnatonibus"</note> He explains himself, indicating what he wants to be understood by those similar to him; to the Gnatos, the sort of parasites found in the works of **Terence** <note>A Roman playwright (c. 195–159 BC) whose character Gnatho in the play 'The Eunuch' became the namesake for all parasitic flatterers</note>, such as the character **Gnatho**. **"I go"** <note>original: "Eo"</note> means "therefore." Since I am accustomed to delighting in those similar to myself, I seek therefore which accomplices I may bring in.

<margin>Fellow tribesmen. Tribal.</margin> The word is used here as a causal conjunction, according to **Priscian** <note>A late Roman grammarian</note> in the fifteenth book of his work *On Conjunctions*. Names, pronouns, prepositions, or even adverbs are sometimes found acting in the place of causal conjunctions. **Fellow tribesmen** <note>original: "Contribules"</note> refers to scoundrels, accomplices, and other parasites from the same "tribe," though the word is pronounced with the accent on the second-to-last syllable. **Horace** <note>Roman lyric poet and satirist</note> writes: "and with a small cap, you be a tribal dinner-guest." **Cicero** <note>Roman statesman and orator</note> in a letter to Gaius Titius writes: "Lucius Custodius is my fellow tribesman, a fellow citizen, and my friend." **Parasitic** <note>original: "Parasiticos"</note> refers to eaters and gluttons; it is a Greek word <note>original: "παράσιτος" (parasitos)</note> derived from the word for food <note>original: "ἀπὸ τοῦ σίτου" (apo tou sitou)</note>. For a

<margin>Parasite. Parasitic. To play the parasite. Prostitute. Gluttons.</margin> **parasite** is properly defined in Latin as one who, for the sake of food, says and does ridiculous things and lives at another's whim, much like a mouse always eating another's food, as **Plautus** <note>Roman comic playwright</note> says in his play *The Captives*. From this we get the words "parasitic," "parasiting," and **to play the parasite** <note>original: "supparasitari"</note>, a word used by Plautus which means to serve or to flatter. This is the duty of parasites who are present at dinners even when uninvited; for this reason, the parasite in Plautus complains that the youth have given him the name **"prostitute"** <note>original: "scorto"</note>, because when he is hungry, he is treated like a harlot or a piece of skin by everyone. In comedies, they are aptly called **gluttons** <note>original: "edaces"</note>,

<margin>Whirlpool of gluttony. Parasitic art. Predatory art.</margin> whose **whirlpool of gluttony** <note>original: "voratrina"</note> both Terence and Plautus describe most skillfully. **Lucian** <note>Greek satirist</note> left a small book about the parasite, where he teaches that it is a **"parasitic art."** Our poet calls it a **predatory art** <note>original: "prædonicā"</note>, and not without reason. For whatever is scraped together by the profit of that art is very much like plunder. Canon law forbids giving to such people, as seen in the chapter on simony. However, **Priscian** called parasites **"dinner-guests"** <note>original: "Δαιτυμόνας" (Daitymonas)</note>. Lucian mentions that Patroclus was the parasite of Achilles, and Meriones the parasite of Idomeneus, whom **Homer** calls **"attendants"** <note>original: "θεράποντας" (therapontas)</note>—meaning cultivators or servants—as **Suidas** <note>A 10th-century Greek encyclopedist</note> teaches. In Greek, such a person is also called a **flatterer** <note>original: "κόλαξ" (kolax)</note>, a **table-licker** <note>original: "τραπεζόλοιχος" (trapezoloichos)</note>, a **meat-table-hanger-on** <note>original: "κρεωστράπεζος" (kreostrapezos)</note>, and a **tray-licker** <note>original: "σελεμπώλοκος" (selempolokos)</note>. Of these, the most clever is **table-licker**, as if he were a "licker of tables." For parasites are the followers of refined tables:

<margin>Dinner-seekers. Whip-eaters. Predators.</margin> **dinner-seekers**, **lunch-seekers**, and **whip-eaters** <note>those who endure beatings for a meal</note>. The **predatory art** is that which is devastating and invades the food of another. **Predators** are those who invade another's province to devastate it. **Virgil** <note>Author of the *Aeneid*</note> writes: "The treacherous predator seeking the deep with the abducted maiden." This predatory art is spoken of legally, as it involves the most infamous kind of gain, which civil laws also recognize. **They cultivate**—that is, they practice. **Morsels of food** <note>original: "Offarū esculenta"</note> refer to the choice

<margin>Morsel</margin> foods that our impudence seeks out with no little effort. **Morsel** <note>original: "offa"</note>, "little morsel" <note>original: "offula"</note>, and in the third degree, "bit of a morsel" <note>original: "offella"</note>, which **Juvenal** <note>Roman satirist</note> adds in another way in Satire XI: "soaked with bits of a morsel." This signifies the choice pieces of food into which meat is cut, according to **Columella** <note>Roman writer on agriculture</note>. In **Suetonius's** biography of Claudius, it is understood as a bait of the most skillful flavor,

<margin>Amphora</margin> and an imperfect fetus is called a "morsel" by the satiric poet. **Of the amphoras** <note>original: "Amphorarū"</note> refers to the clever, capacious vessels, which **Horace** describes: "An amphora began to be formed as the wheel ran; why does a pitcher come out?" Remus Favinus measures the **amphora** by its own volume. Where

<margin>Undiluted. Pure wine.</margin> there is **undiluted wine** <note>original: "merax"</note>, meaning pure, tasty, keen, and less diluted wine. **Avienus** <note>Latin poet</note>, in **Macrobius's** *Saturnalia* book VII, discusses the problem of why a draft of wine seems more **pure** <note>original: "meracior"</note> to a fasting person than to one who has eaten. From this comes **pure wine** <note>original: "meracum"</note>, strong, etc.

<summary>This page defines the various classical terms for parasites and gluttons, drawing on Greek and Roman literature to describe their social behavior and the "art" of seeking food at the expense of others. It provides a linguistic analysis of terms for social dependents, gluttony, and high-quality wine.</summary>
<keywords>parasite, Gnatho, gluttony, Terence, Plautus, Lucian, Horace, Latin etymology, Greek terms, social class, predatory art, amphora, pure wine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological and cultural commentary on the life of a parasite. Having discussed the "Gnatos" (social parasites) on the previous page, the author now focuses on the "tools of the trade": pure wine (merum), the terminology of the mind and senses, and the scraps of food left over from a rich man's table. It uses wordplay and classical etymologies to paint a picture of a life of gluttony and refined excess.</meta>

XIX

# ACT ONE

...potent pure wine. **Horace** <note>Roman lyric poet (65–8 BC)</note> writes: "He expelled the sickness and the bile with pure hellebore," <note>original: "Expulit elleboro morbū bilem̄qʒ meraco"</note> from *merum*, that is, from wine. **Merone** <note>original: "Mero. onis."</note> is a name used through "nickname-making" <note>original: "περι προσωνομασίας" (peri prosonomasias)</note> or wordplay, about which **Fabius** <note>Quintilian (c. 35–100 AD), the Roman rhetorician</note> speaks in his ninth book; just as we say "bakers" <note>original: "pistores"</note> for the "Pisos," and "plowmen" <note>original: "aratores"</note> for "orators," and for **Tiberius** we say **Biberius** <note>from 'bibere', to drink</note>, for **Claudius** we say **Caldius** <note>from 'calidus', hot, usually referring to hot wine</note>, and for **Nero** we say **Merone** <note>from 'merum', pure wine</note>. **At some point** <note>original: "Qui aliquando"</note>; **Priscian** <note>A famous Latin grammarian</note> says this is an indefinite particle and is used absolutely, beyond the meaning of past and future. **Agroecius** <note>A 5th-century grammarian</note> teaches that it signifies "fully," and thinks two examples can be found of this; he himself added a third regarding the present tense: "Wake up **at last** and take up the republic." It has an acute accent on the third-to-last syllable, lest the two parts seem divided, and for the sake of 
<margin>Sometimes</margin>
distinguishing it from the word "somewhat" <note>original: "aliquanto"</note>. **In more soothing places.** <note>original: "Delinitiōribus locis."</note> That is, in pleasant, sweet, and delightful places. **Marcus Tullius Cicero**: "He, soothed by the allurements of pleasure." **They steal the minds,** <note>original: "Mentes furanť"</note> they occupy them, they hold them back.
<margin>The Mind</margin>
**And the senses.** He distinguishes "sense" from "the mind." The word "mind" <note>original: "mens"</note> draws its name from the Greek *myein* <note>original: "μυειν", to initiate into mysteries or to close</note>, that is, from the word for the **Moon**,
<margin>Senses</margin>
because it is overwhelmed by various affections and, like the moon, is accustomed to being restored again, as **Cassiodorus** <note>A Roman statesman and writer (c. 485–585 AD)</note> writes in his work *On the Soul*.
<margin>Perceptions</margin>
We call **perceptions** <note>original: "Sensa"</note> in the neuter gender those things which we feel. **Marcus Cicero** in the first book of *On the Orator*: "and we are able to express our **perceptions** in speech," which **Macrobius** <note>A Roman provincial who wrote the 'Saturnalia' in the 5th century</note> also indicates in the seventh book of the *Saturnalia*.

> That they may pay back the remains of the greasy plate and cup,
> The dripping ceilings of the leftover wine,
> And the scraps of the tables and the dutiful stipends
> To our empty throats and bellies,
> Not as if it were a beggar’s pittance.

<margin>The remains</margin>
**That the remains of the greasy.** Remains are usually divided among the servants and parasites. **Suetonius** is the source regarding the drinking and luxury of **Sergius Galba**. **Seneca** also alludes to this in his eleventh book of epistles.
<margin>Plates</margin>
The mention of **greasy plates** should be referred to the gluttony of the parasite.
<margin>Greasy</margin>
**Greasy,** that is, more sumptuous and shining, which the gaping parasitic hunger licks and surrounds. **Plate** <note>original: "Patella"</note> descends as a diminutive from *patina* (a pan), and is a small vessel suitable for
<margin>Drinking cup</margin>
performing sacrifices. **Drinking cups.** <note>original: "Canthari"</note> The *cantharus* is attributed to **Bacchus**, just as the
<margin>Scyphus</margin>
*scyphus* cup is attributed to **Hercules**. **Virgil** <note>original: "Maro"</note> mentions this in his sixth Eclogue. Both were handled vessels. Bacchus carries the *cantharus* in his right hand, a fitting emblem for a drinker. **Silius Italicus** in the seventh book of his *Punica*.
<margin>Dripping</margin>
**Dripping ceilings.** <note>original: "Vuida lacunaria."</note> Meaning the lips of the cups are wet with the sweetness of wine. **Dripping** <note>original: "Vuidū"</note> is said in contrast to **juice**.
<margin>Juice</margin>
The poets confound "dripping" (*vuidum*) and "moist" (*humidum*). "Dripping" means it has moisture on the inside, from which we get the word "grape" (*uva*), while "moist" is that which is wet on the outside, according to **Servius** <note>A 4th-century commentator on Virgil</note>.
<margin>Paneled ceilings</margin>
**Dripping ceilings.** That is, the "chambers" of the cups; we call both "lakes" (*lacus*) and **paneled ceilings** (*laquearia*) the upper places in buildings. Whence come the words *lacunar* and *laquear*. **Virgil**: "Lamps hang from the golden **paneled ceilings**." **Leftover.** <note>original: "Supſtitis."</note> Meaning remaining, surviving, or residual. **Wine** <note>original: "Meri"</note> is used as a noun for wine. The **Satirist** <note>likely Persius or Juvenal</note>
poet used it as an adjective: "While the ointments foam, drenched in Falernian **wine**." Our ancestors called the more noble wine *merum*. **Horace**: "The battle with the Centaurs and Lapiths was stirred up over **pure wine**," and we read of wine that is "two years old" and "four years old" <note>original: "bimū & quadrimū"</note>. **Scraps.** <note>original: "Quadras."</note> Fragments and remains of the tables collected bit by bit. **Juvenal** mentions "scraps" in his Satires: "If you are of such a mind..." **Virgil** mentions them in his seventh book, as does **Martial**.

It explains the social and linguistic origins of nicknames for Roman emperors and distinguishes between various types of moisture and household architecture as applied to dining.</summary>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and cultural commentary on the life of a parasite. The previous page explored the etymologies of names associated with heavy drinkers and the use of adverbs in classical Latin. Now, the author moves into Act One, focusing on the terminology of the parasite’s "employment"—distinguishing between honorable pay and the shame of begging, while citing the dining habits of the ancients.</meta>

XX

# ACT ONE

<margin>Two tables among the ancients</margin>
The number of tables among the ancients was twofold; one was provided for meat and the other for fruit. This is noted by **Virgil** <note>The great Roman epic poet (70–19 BC)</note> in the second book of the *Georgics* and by **Macrobius** <note>A Roman provincial writer (c. 400 AD) known for his "Saturnalia"</note> in the second book of the *Saturnalia*. **Pious stipends.** <note>original: "Pia stipendia"</note> He says "pious," not "warlike and bloody," but pious. Not **begging-bowls** <note>original: "mendicabula"</note>, because stipends are, as it were, what is owed to "table-soldiers" and those practicing the "lunch-seeking" military service.

<margin>Stipend and Stipendiaries</margin>
The word "stipend" <note>original: "stipendium"</note> takes its name from *stips*, which is a small amount of coin that is collected, as the legal scholar teaches in the *Digest* <note>original: "ff. de ver. sig." — a reference to the 'De Verborum Significatione' section of Roman law</note>. From this, those who pay a tribute are called "stipendiaries" <note>original: "stipendiarii"</note>, or by another name "tax-payers" <note>original: "uectigales"</note>, and there are "stipendiary cities." The "bearded little hallucinator" <note>A dismissive reference to a rival scholar or a specific character type</note> today calls those people "stipendiaries" whom he ought to call "mercenaries."

<margin>Mercenaries, Hungry holidays, and the Begging-bowl</margin>
**For our empty ones.** <note>original: "Noſtris inanibus"</note> That is: for the vacant, the fasting, and those with broken throats. We call them **hungry holidays** <note>original: "Eſuriales ferias"</note> whenever parasitic bellies wither from lack of food, a sight more pleasant than anything else. As **Juvenal** <note>A Roman satirical poet (late 1st century AD)</note> says: "For what comedy or mime is better than a crying gullet?" They should **repay** <note>original: "Repenſitēt"</note>; that is, let them pay back as if it were a well-earned little wage. Not as if it were a **begging-bowl.** <note>original: "mendicabulū"</note> The parasite blushes to beg, and he rejects the name "begging-bowl," which he has changed to "stipend." But the poet **Horace** <note>The Roman lyricist</note> spoke more truly when he wished for parasites to be included under the term "beggar," for whom it would be more honorable to beg, a point which Juvenal also proves in his satires: "If you still hold to your purpose." **Plautus** <note>The famous Roman comic playwright (c. 254–184 BC)</note> used the word "begging-bowls," and **Laberius** <note>A Roman knight and writer of mimes (105–43 BC)</note> used "beggary" and "beggar-monies."

> For this would be an infamy to my ancestors
> But a prize and a small wage for the playful art
> Even now the very hour, fixed and certain, looms
> In which these scoundrels, that lowest flock
> Might rush into some shared tavern or other
> Or shout proverbs to the people in the forum
> I must hurry there as quickly as possible.

<margin>Prize, Wage, and Pay-receivers</margin>
**For this would be.** This is why he rejects the "begging-bowl," for although he is most infamous himself, he dreads "infamy." **Of the playful art.** <note>original: "Ludicræ artis"</note> Meaning the art of parasitic buffoonery. **Prize and a small wage.** A prize <note>original: "premium"</note> is generally given for a good or a bad deed; **Donatus** <note>A 4th-century grammarian and commentator on Terence</note> is the authority for this in his commentary on *The Eunuch*. We derive "wage" <note>original: "mercedem"</note> from "meriting" <note>original: "merendo"</note>, just as "prize" <note>original: "premium"</note> comes from "prey" <note>original: "preda"</note>. From this come "pay-receivers" <note>original: "mercedones"</note> from the paying of wages, and writers testify that the ancients used "mercenary" <note>original: "mercedituum"</note> for someone who supports himself by a wage.

<margin>Fixed feasts</margin>
**Even now indeed the very hour.** The "hungry holidays" weigh upon parasitic bellies and announce the time and hour for seeking companions. So it is in Plautus. Likewise, other parasites walk about the forum in vain. **Fixed and certain.** <note>original: "Stata & certa"</note> Let the "and" be understood as explanatory; it signifies something ordered and fixed which cannot be delayed or changed, which is why we speak of "fixed feasts" <note>original: "stata feſta"</note> that have set days. According to **Valerius Maximus** <note>A 1st-century author of a collection of historical anecdotes</note> in his first book of *Memorable Deeds*: "The ancestors had fixed and solemn ceremonies, etc." **Statius** <note>A 1st-century Roman poet</note> in his poem *Achilleid*: "Until the fixed times reach their full limits." **Lucan** <note>A Roman poet (39–65 AD)</note> in his tenth book: "Whose winds have fixed times."

<margin>Scoundrels and Ox-beaten ones</margin>
**Looms.** <note>original: "Imminet"</note> It is urgent, it is near. **Scoundrels.** <note>original: "Flagriones"</note> That is, those most worthy of being beaten with whips <note>original: "flagris"</note>; they are also called **ox-beaten ones** <note>original: "bucedæ"</note> because people "write" upon their backs with ox-hide straps.

<summary>This page analyzes the vocabulary of Act One, specifically regarding the "professional" status of parasites who prefer to view their scraps as a "stipend" rather than "begging." It draws heavily on classical poets and grammarians to define the social and temporal structures of ancient dining and scoundrel behavior.</summary>
<keywords>parasites, stipends, ancient dining, hungry holidays, Roman satire, Plautus, Juvenal, etymology, social hierarchy, scoundrels</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the lifestyle and terminology associated with parasites and the lower classes in Roman comedy. While the previous page discussed the "salaries" of these professional dinner-guests, this page dives into the insults used against them and the military origins of the words for their social circles.</meta>

# ACT ONE XXI

<margin>Rod-bearers and Rogues</margin>
Using the word from **Plautus** <note>Titus Maccius Plautus (c. 254–184 BC), the famous Roman playwright known for boisterous comedies</note>, we hear them called **"beating-suffers"** <note>original: "plagipatidæ" — a mock-heroic Greek-style name meaning "sons of those who endure stripes"</note> and **"scoundrels"** <note>original: "uerberones"</note>, or in Greek, **"whip-rogues"** <note>original: "mastigiæ"</note>. From another direction, we hear them elegantly called "town-tinklers." **Rod-bearers** <note>original: "Virgatores"</note> or torturers are those who strike the guilty with lashes for their crimes. This is a fictional word created from the sound that the lashes make; hence, through onomatopoeia elsewhere, he calls this sound **"tax tax"** <note>The sound of a whip striking skin</note> when the whip-bearers are being beaten. 

<margin>The Lowest Ones</margin>
**That lowliest herd.** <note>original: "Infimatis ille grex"</note>
<margin>To lower</margin>
A company of the most worthless men, born in a lower position beneath all other men. Just as we call those of the highest condition and power **"the summits"** <note>original: "summates"</note>, as Plautus says in his play *Stichus*: "The high-ranking men will recline at the top; I, the lowliest of the low-born, at the bottom." **Apuleius** <note>A 2nd-century Roman author</note> in the eleventh book of his *Metamorphoses* writes: "Restored by the commands of the highest goddess." And the verb **"to lower"** <note>original: "infimare"</note> is used by authors to mean "to make low." Apuleius, in his work *On the Demon of Socrates* <note>The OCR reads "Isocratis," likely a scribal error for "Socratis"</note>, says: "these things lowered to miseries," and a little further down, "because it raises up and lowers." 

<margin>The Herd</margin>
**Herd.** <note>original: "Grex"</note> This is a word for an ignoble multitude. **Marcus Cicero** speaks of "a herd of weak cattle." In his speech *Against Piso*, he uses it more significantly when he says, "What with your most filthy herds?" And in the same oration: "No one complains that some Syrian or other from a herd of new recruits was made consul." We also take that line from the **Satirical Poet** <note>Juvenal, known for his harsh social critiques</note> as a reproach: "With a Chaldean herd." 

**All the way from that place.** "All the way" <note>original: "usque"</note> is an adverb of place. **Terence** <note>A Roman playwright (c. 185–159 BC)</note> says: "she is all the way from Ethiopia." At other times, it signifies "for a long time" or "much." 

**Into some companionship.** <note>original: "contubernium"</note> A living-together, a fellowship, or a banquet where table-companions and banqueters are gathered. It is a military word, as mentioned above regarding the "army of actors." 
<margin>Companionship</margin>
Furthermore, a **companionship** is a squad of ten soldiers living under one **tent** <note>original: "papilione" — literally "butterfly," the Roman term for a leather tent</note>, over whom a **Dean** <note>original: "Decanus"</note> presided, who was the head of the company. From this, the word **"fellowship"** <note>original: "contubernalitas"</note> is used elegantly by **Cyprian** <note>Early Christian bishop and writer (c. 210–258 AD)</note> for social bonding. 

<margin>To rush to the Forum</margin>
**Let them rush.** That is, let them flow together without restraint. It is familiar to actors, or rather to buffoons, to be gathered and flocked together in their own manner. **To the Forum.** Where you might see those whom you may accompany to their homes to eat together; he adds the time and place to the business. **"Forum"** is a general name derived from "bringing" <note>original: "ferendo"</note>, taking its specific meaning from what is added to it. 
Hence we speak of a forum for gamblers, the cattle market, the vegetable market, the Roman forum, the forum of Caesar, the forum of Augustus, the fish market, the Transit forum, or the forum of Cupid. Supper-seeking parasites know how to use the forum, as the proverb goes. 

**I must hasten there.** I must go by myself. 
<margin>To hasten</margin>
Terence says in *The Mother-in-Law*: "And I hasten myself there from that place," meaning "I have entered," as **Donatus** <note>A famous 4th-century grammarian and commentator on Terence</note> says. 

**Most seasonably.** <note>original: "Maturrime"</note> Rather quickly, in a hurry, as fast as possible. 
<margin>Seasonable</margin>
To be done **"seasonably"** <note>original: "mature"</note> is said of that to which both the speed of industry and the slowness of diligence are applied. **Augustus** expressed this matter most elegantly with two Greek words. For they say he was accustomed to say in his conversations and write in his letters: **"make haste slowly"** <note>original: "σπεῦδε βραδέως" (speude bradeōs)</note>, which means "hurry at a slow pace," that is, "act seasonably."

> For to them the greatest pleasure is
> To enjoy my presence in games, jokes, and laughter.
> I know that immediately for the more wretched ones,
> They are more wretched still with hunger and famine.

<sig>Ciii</sig>

It concludes with a discussion of the "Forum" as a social hub and Augustus's famous maxim on acting with "seasonable" speed.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological examination of the daily habits and physical conditions of characters in Roman comedy. While the previous page focused on the social status and insults hurled at "whip-bearers" and the lower classes, this page transitions into the physical sensations of their lives: the pleasures of eating, the nature of laughter, and the agony of hunger.</meta>

# ACT ONE XXII

<margin>I enjoy</margin> "For them." He added the cause: Pleasure, that great enticement. **To enjoy** <note>original: "frui"</note> means to delight oneself. But although "to enjoy" properly means to eat or feed—derived from the *frumen* <note>the upper part of the throat or gullet</note>—it is also said of things that are neither food nor drink. **Terence** <note>Publius Terentius Afer (c. 185–159 BC), a Roman playwright known for his refined style</note> says: "It is not right for me to enjoy any pleasure here."

<margin>Laughter</margin> In games, jokes, and laughter. We discussed games and jokes a little further above. Regarding laughter, let us add a few things here in passing. Natural philosophers say the seat of laughter is the **spleen**, just as the gall is the seat of anger and the liver is the seat of lust. The spleen is also a particular hindrance to running; **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder (23–79 AD), author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*</note> recorded that animals can survive if it is removed by a wound, but then laughter is taken away from the human. **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), a Stoic philosopher</note> advised that our laughter should be without loud cackling. Hence, **Apuleius** <note>A 2nd-century Roman novelist and orator</note> wittily used the term **"cackle-able"** <note>original: "cachinnabilis"</note> for laughter that is unrestrained and blameworthy. **Macrobius** <note>A 5th-century Roman writer</note> mentions a "censorious laughter" in the second book of his *Saturnalia*; just as unrestrained laughter shows contempt, a more modest laughter is fitting for dignity and majesty, according to **Virgil**.

<margin>Pythagoras</margin> We read that **Pythagoras** <note>The famous Greek philosopher and mathematician known for his disciplined lifestyle</note> abstained from laughter, wit, and jokes. And **Crassus** <note>Specifically, Marcus Licinius Crassus Agelastus</note>, the grandfather of the one who was captured by the Parthians, never laughed; for this reason, he was nicknamed **"the unsmiling"** <note>original: "agelastum"</note>. Similarly, many did not weep, whom the Greeks call **"apathetic"** <note>original: "apathos" — those without passion or feeling</note>, being more detached from human emotions. The Greek word **"to laugh"** <note>original: "γελάω" (gelao)</note> is the source of the nickname for **Democritus**, who was called **"the laugher"** <note>original: "γελασίνου" (gelasinou)</note> because he used to constantly shake his lungs with laughter, according to **Suidas** <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the ancient world</note>. We learn of only one person who laughed on the day he was born: **Zoroaster**, the inventor of the magic arts.

<margin>Zoroaster</margin> The **"laugh-leaf"** <note>original: "Gelotophylla"</note> herb grows in Bactria and around the Borysthenes river; if drunk with myrrh and wine, it causes one to see various visions and not know how to stop laughing. **Pliny** is the authority that in the disease of madness, laughter is a fatal sign. **Laertius** <note>Diogenes Laertius, a biographer of Greek philosophers</note> uses a proverb for one who rarely laughs, saying he **"makes a sacrifice to the Graces"** <note>meaning to seek some charm or humor to soften a stern nature</note>. "I know immediately." He says, "I know already that some of my fellow tribesmen in the forum are being tortured by hunger, more wretched than the wretched."

<margin>Immediately</margin> **Immediately** <note>original: "actutum"</note> means quickly, on the spot, without delay—an adverb of time. **Plautus** says in *Amphitruo*: "unless you go away from here immediately." Heroic poetry does not disdain this word. **Virgil**: "then he will return the rest immediately." **Pious Aeneas.** **Statius** <note>Publius Papinius Statius (c. 45–96 AD), a Roman poet</note> in the first book of the *Thebaid*: "Immediately he sees the royal vestibules."

<margin>Nouns in -ellus</margin> **More wretched** <note>original: "miselliores"</note>: nouns of the second declension form their diminutives in "-ellus," such as *miser* becoming **"poor little thing"** <note>original: "misellus"</note>. **Starvation** <note>original: "inedia"</note> is the lack of food and being famished.

<margin>Hunger</margin> And **Hunger**, which is called "the bad-persuader" by Virgil and "the vehement executioner" by Hegesippus. Pliny does not think lethal starvation occurs before the seventh day, though some have lasted beyond the eleventh. Among types of death, the most miserable is to perish by hunger. We find that **Silius** the poet, who wrote on the Punic Wars, died in this way. The famine of Casilinum, which Hannibal besieged, is most well-known. **Albertus Magnus** <note>A 13th-century German friar and polymath</note>—whom some nicknamed **"Aristotle’s Ape"** (if that counts as praise for the man)—recorded marvelous things about hunger in his *History of Animals*.

-> They look for where it is more convenient for their throats, <-
-> Where there is eating and drinking. But look, how convenient, <-
-> Look, they are almost in the middle of the forum, they double their pace, <-
-> They gather, they run to it, they have something playful. <-

<vocab>fruor, risus, Pythagoras, Zoroastres, actutum, misellus, fames, Plinius, Seneca, Virgilius, Plautus</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the philological and physiological aspects of laughter and hunger as depicted in classical literature. It cites various ancient authorities to explain the origins of terms for enjoyment, the physical seat of laughter in the spleen, and the dire nature of starvation.</summary>
<keywords>laughter, hunger, spleen, Pythagoras, Pliny the Elder, Zoroaster, diminutives, starvation, Roman comedy, Albertus Magnus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological and theatrical analysis of Roman comedy, moving from the physical sensations of the previous page—hunger and laughter—into the opening scene of a play. It focuses on the linguistic roots of terms for eating and movement while introducing a lively scene involving characters named Salax, Helvo, and Buttubatta.</meta>

# ACT ONE
### PAGE XXIII

Rather, I hasten with a swifter step
And run now even properly so that I may see as well.

<margin>Eating</margin> They seek where it is more convenient, more useful, and more fit for the **jaws** <note>original: "faucibus"</note>, which in parasites wither from long-lasting hunger and become **cobwebbed with starvation** <note>original: "araneantes esuritione"</note>. The Plautine Parasite, to express parasitic hunger most significantly, says: **"It is being eaten"** <note>original: "Estur"</note>. I am undone! I see poorly; I have a bitter mouth and my teeth are full; my jaws are blurry with hunger. Where it says "it is being eaten," it expresses where it might be more convenient for the jaws—namely, where eating and drinking are happening. This is an impersonal verb derived from "eating," according to **Diomedes** <note>A 4th-century Latin grammarian</note>, from which the verb "to eat up" (*comedo*) is also formed, which makes "eaten up" (*comesum* and *comestum*) according to the authorities **Didymus** and **Valgius**. 

<margin>Just now</margin> "But look!" The **Preparation** <note>original Greek: "Parasceue"</note> is for the next scene, to be spoken with pointing and a joyful gesture, because the thing he previously had in mind now appears. **Terence** in the *Phormio* says: "Look, Dromon is here with Syrus together for you." **Just now** (*commodum*) means "right now," "at that time," "at that moment"—an adverb very common among classical writers but well-known and understood by few. **Plautus** in the *Cassina* says: "By Hercules, I was going to you just now." 

<margin>Near</margin> **Donatus** <note>Aelius Donatus, a famous 4th-century grammarian and teacher of St. Jerome</note> in the *Phormio* notes that "near" (*prope*) or "almost" (*pene*) is an adverbial particle most frequently used by Plautus and **Marcus Cicero**. "They double." What do they double? **They come together**; they run toward each other. 

<margin>To come together</margin> **To come together** (*coire*), beyond its common meaning, also signifies to strike a bargain or to agree. Terence in the *Eunuch* says: "We came together in Piraeus." They have something playful. "To come together" for the purpose of playing together. **Swifter** (*citatiore*) means faster or quicker. **Properly so that I may see as well**: Not only will I run and be present, but I will see as well.

## SALAX, HELVO, BUTTUBATTA
## ARISTOPHORUS THE CAMP-FOLLOWER

SALAX: Hey you, our tribune, fly here! 
HELVO: O Salax, what is the matter? Why are you here in such a tumult?
SALAX: I don't know what good Buttubatta brings from over there; people run to him as if he had come from India bringing a phoenix bird.
BUTTUBATTA: Come here, look now, look now!

**Hey you.** The spectator is won over by the wonderful pleasantness of the jokes and the witty charms of the **glutton** <note>original: "heluonis"</note>, who with graphic nonsense has made everyone eager to listen. In this scene, the beginning of the drama occurs; it is entirely full of action and consists more in gesture than in speech. From this, it is most gracefully aided by pronunciation, as if by soul and spirit, and the matter reaches the point where the **cap is removed** <note>original: "caluaria retegatur"; literally "the skull is uncovered," likely referring to a character removing a head-covering or a bald joke</note>. Therefore, as the actors gather, Buttubatta intervenes and starts the play. **Hey you, tribune.** "Hey" (*Heus*) sometimes has the meaning of a name to call someone to attention or consideration. Terence says: "Hey, I am my own closest neighbor," but otherwise it is a voice for calling those from a distance. **You, tribune.** "You" must be heard with a beat, because it is spoken with emphasis. **Our.** That is, you are the tribune for the actors. Elsewhere in the *Persa*, a Plautine slave says he is a "tribune of floggings." You, tribune,

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's opening act, transitioning from the previous discussion on hunger and movement into an analysis of character introductions and the arrival of rare "merchandise" (the Phoenix) from India. It continues to cite classical authorities to explain specific Latin idioms and the origins of character names.</meta>

# ACT ONE
### PAGE XXIIII

<margin>Tribunes</margin> **Donatus** <note>A famous 4th-century Roman grammarian</note> says there is great force and meaning in pronouns. **Virgil** <note>The Roman epic poet</note> wrote: "By singing, you, that one." **Pomponius** writes that the **Tribunes** <note>original: "Tribuni"</note> were magistrates of the common people (plebeians). They were named such either because the Roman people were divided into three parts and they elected one from each, or because they were established by the vote of the **tribes** <note>original: "tribuum"</note>. "Rush over" (*Advola*), he says, do not just "approach" (*adi*). For this is a new matter, comparable to the merchandise of the Arabs, in which **Buttubatta** <note>A character in the play</note> exults. At the same time, the actor makes the spectators attentive. **O Salax.** The **Glutton** <note>original: "Heluo," likely a character name or description</note> is moved with joy by the presence of **Salax** <note>A character whose name implies lustfulness</note>. Thus he cries out, just as **Terence** <note>The Roman playwright</note> does: "O Ctesipho, O Syrus!" However, we use that exclamation when we see acquaintances from a distance. The name of Salax is derived from **lust** <note>original: "libidine"</note>. **Horace** calls a certain shameful body part "lustful" (*salacem*), and **Virgil** (or **Ovid**) in the *Priapeia* poem called **Priapus** <note>The god of fertility</note> the "lustful god," from "leaping" (*saliendo*), that is, copulating, a term taken from the behavior of livestock. "What is the state of affairs?" (*Quid rerum*). This comes from "thing" (*re*), while "little thing" (*recula*) is its diminutive.

<margin>Salax</margin> "How you are bustling here!" (*Ut tu tumultuanter hic*). He leaves out the verb, being filled with joy. "He runs" (*Currit*). This signifies that something is being done by everyone, as he mentions. For when we show that something difficult is being done by everyone, we use an impersonal verb. Terence says: "She is placed on the fire; there is weeping" <note>original: "fletur," literally "it is wept"</note>. "As if" (*Ceu*) is a particle of comparison, according to **Priscian** <note>A late Latin grammarian</note> in Book 15.

<margin>India</margin> "What good news?" (*Quid boni*). He mentions something good, making the listener eager to see. "If India had come forth" (*Exisset India*). India is the mother of gems; it is named after the river **Indus**. **Pliny** <note>The Roman natural historian</note> says in Book 7 that this land teems with miracles. **Herodotus** <note>The Greek historian</note> recounts wonders about the Indians in his book *Thalia*. **Theopompus**, **Hellanicus**, **Ctesias**, and **Megasthenes** are the authors who wrote about Indian matters, but **Strabo** <note>A Greek geographer</note> took much from the latter one.

<margin>Phoenix</margin> "The Phoenix bird" (*Auem phoenicem*). He speaks most attentively of the **Phoenix**, for no commodity is rarer. Herodotus writes in his book *Euterpe* that he never saw it except in a painting, and that it is sacred to the Egyptians, to whom it travels very rarely. This bird, if it does not differ from its image, resembles an eagle in appearance and size. When asked about the Phoenix, **Manilius**—that noble senator learned without any teacher—described it most diligently. **Ovid** follows the transformation of the Phoenix in the 15th volume of his *Metamorphoses*. Grammarians hold that the poet **Persius** made a metaphor from the Phoenix when he said, "since this bird is rare." **Ambrose** <note>Saint Ambrose, a Church Father</note>, who translated and compiled into his own Latin book, the *Hexameron*, the things said in Greek by **Origen**, says in the sixth volume of that work: "Let this bird teach us to believe in the resurrection by its own example, for without example or the instruction of reason, it renews for itself the signs of resurrection." "Come here, then!" (*Adeste ehodum*). We understand that the exulting Buttubatta is inviting the stage-players with a very rhetorical opening speech (*antelogo*), so that he may playfully mock those he has lured to him. He repeats "Ehodum" out of emotion. **Marcus Cicero** teaches in Book 4 that repetition pertains to emotion.

<margin>Come then</margin> "Come then" (*Ehodum*) is an **additive particle** <note>original Greek: "parelkon," a grammatical filler</note>, just as *adessum* is a lengthening of speech, a figure familiar to Terence. For example, in the *Andria*: "Come here to me" (*ehodum ad me*), where the particle *dum* is redundant; and in the *Phormio*, he added a whole word as a filler when he said, "it shall be done zealously" (*fiet sedulo*). There, Donatus teaches that "zealously" (*sedulo*) is a filler. For the meaning would have been simply "it shall be done."

->Come, leaders of thieves, of actors, likewise<-
->And of stage-players, of playfulness, and of rascals.<-

The author uses these references to discuss Roman social ranks, such as Tribunes, and the rhetorical use of repetition in Latin drama.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's opening act. Having previously discussed the arrival of the characters and the theme of hunger, the commentator now analyzes the specific language of the character's entrance, focusing on the speed and eloquence associated with the god Mercury, while providing etymologies for terms related to theft and punishment.</meta>

# ACT ONE
### PAGE XXV

**A**ttend mercurially, not like leaden men,
**S**wift as feathers, with winged feet; I know
**M**arvelously well that I introduce a thing wonderfully 
**I**dmirable, yet to you indeed ignoble, here within my
**S**om, ARIS, Paras, a preamble and a rhetorical
**E**legance, and quite a forensic babble [Buttubatta]; for with this
**C**oncinity of speech, no man—
**N**either Gorgias, nor Zeno, nor Protagoras—
**T**hink I, shall match you equally. But produce it: what is it?

---

<margin>I am present</margin> **Attend.** <note>original: "Adefte"</note> Be present, run hither. "Attend" also signifies to bring help. It is from this that advocates, patrons, and other friends are said to "attend" a case. The Prince of the forum calls upon famous men for any purpose. The word "Prince" <note>original: "Princeps"</note> is itself used in two senses. **Of thieves.** <note>original: "Furum"</note> **Varro** <note>A famous Roman scholar and writer</note>, in his work on the reasoning of words, expressed the etymology of that name, which **Aulus Gellius** <note>A 2nd-century Roman author of the 'Attic Nights'</note> recounts in his first volume. Furthermore, the jurist **Aristo** writes that among the ancient Egyptians, all thefts were legal and unpunished. Among the Spartans as well, authors of no small reputation have left records that the law and practice of stealing existed. This was because the skill and habit of stealing sharpened and strengthened the minds of the youth toward the cunning of ambushes, the endurance of vigils, and the speed of creeping up. **Of actors.** Of whom mention was made above. **Likewise.** In a similar way. **And of games.** That is, of sports. The ancients used the word "play" (*lusionem*) for a "game" (*ludo*). **Papinius** <note>The Roman poet Statius</note> is the authority who mentions ball-play, and **Tranquillus** <note>The biographer Suetonius</note> mentions it in his life of **Domitian**. <margin>Scoundrels</margin> **Of scoundrels.** <note>original: "Verberonum," literally 'those worthy of a beating'</note> Our ancestors also called those worthy of lashes "whip-bearers" (*flagriones*); however, "floggers" (*flagratores*) were those who were beaten with whips for pay. The Greeks used the term <term>mastiga</term> <note>original: "μαστιγα"</note> for a whip, and <term>mastigia</term> <note>original: "μαστιγια"</note> for a whip-bearer or scoundrel. **Plautus** <note>The Roman comic playwright</note> says in *The Captives*: "So that I might knock the life out of that scoundrel." <margin>Mercury</margin> **Mercurially.** Swiftly, quickly. For **Mercury** is the god of speed and eloquence, to whom they attach winged sandals. Hence **Virgil** <note>The Roman epic poet</note> wrote: "He flies through the great air on the oarage of wings and quickly stood on the Libyan shores." **Servius** <note>An ancient commentator on Virgil</note> says "quickly" (*citus*) is used because Mercury is fast in his course, so named because he "runs in the middle" (*medius currat*) of the merchandise over which he presides. From this, the poet’s verse should be carefully weighed: "He left mortal sights in the middle of his speech."

**Not like leaden men.** That is, not slow, with a tortoise-like gait and a sluggish walk, or with an "ant's pace" as **Plautus** says in *The Menaechmi*, but rather active and quick. **With winged feet.** Hurried and flying. Regarding this, **Priscian** <note>A 6th-century Latin grammarian</note> in his fifth volume discusses the gender of "winged" (*volucer*). **I know in a wonderful way.** Using every method, the speaker ensures he has an attentive audience because he is about to deceive them. Thus he says "in a wonderful way" and "admirable," and as if that were not enough, he adds "and to you indeed ignoble," meaning unknown. **I introduce.** <note>original: "Insinuo"</note> A word drawn from the "bosom" (*sinus*), just as "to swoop" (*involare*) comes from the "palm" (*vola*). **Aulus Gellius** and **Cicero** in the *Ancient Art* take this to mean to receive...

->C v<-

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's opening scene. Having previously discussed the entrance of characters and the nature of theft, the commentator now examines the specific rhetorical terms used in the dialogue, providing etymologies and classical precedents for words like "insinuare" and "concinnitas," while identifying the famous Greek orators mentioned in the text.</meta>

# ACT ONE
### PAGE XXVI

It is seen to stand for that which is done secretly, under a disguise and through a roundabout way. **Victorinus** <note>a 4th-century grammarian and rhetorician</note> adds, "and secretly by certain circuits." Whence in the "New Art" <note>likely referring to the *Rhetorica ad Herennium*, then attributed to Cicero</note>: "if the listener has been persuaded, we shall **insinuate** <note>original: "insinuabimus"</note> ourselves into the cause." Therefore, to **insinuate** means to creep into something gradually. Whence **Suetonius** <note>Roman historian</note> says: "insinuated to Macron through this," that is, won over into friendship. One **insinuates** oneself into another's friendship when one enters into that friendship not directly, but through certain "sinuses" <note>curves or folds</note> and, as it were, diversions, secretly and with dissimulation. **Plautus** "insinuated into friendship." **Firmicus** <note>a late Roman author</note> writes of a friend of the king "insinuated by the beauty of his body." **Quintus Curtius**, in his fifth book, says of the Phasis river: "it **insinuates** itself into the Persian sea." And **Lucretius** (for the ancients frequently used this word) writes that the soul is **insinuated** into those being born by divine power, or is born there; and elsewhere: "Thus you may see for yourself, one thing after another. In such matters you will be able to **insinuate** yourself into all blind hiding places." Likewise in his third book: "For the juice [flows] upward where the vapor **insinuates**," and he also says pain is **insinuated** into the eyes through the brightness of the sun because the seeds possess fire. **Virgil** in the second book of the *Aeneid*: "Fear **insinuates** itself," etc. **Onosander** <note>a Greek author on military tactics</note> teaches how to prevent the **insinuations** of enemies. The word **exinuare** <note>likely a variation or misspelling of 'insinuare' being discussed in the play</note> is used by the Parasite in the preface: struck by the pleasantness of the speech which **Buttubatta** uses, **Aristophorus** imputes a rhetorical style. The Parasite says: you produce, you prepare. **Anteloquium** <note>preface or prologue</note>: which the Greeks call a *prooimion* <note>original: "προοίμιον"</note>. Highly rhetorical, exceedingly urban, ornate and eloquent, and not unsuited for the forum.

<margin>Exceedingly</margin>
**Exceedingly** <note>original: "Admodum"</note> has an intensive <note>original: "ἐπίτατικη̄μ"</note> significance. **Terence** in *The Mother-in-Law*; at other times it retains the force of nodding or agreement; an example is found in the *Phormio* of the same poet. **Aulus Gellius** in his seventh book: "For by this elegance, harmony, arrangement, and refinement of speech," it is derived either from "concinere" <note>to sing together</note>, which means to agree, as one melody is made from many diverse voices in songs. Or it comes from "cinno" <note>a mixed drink or confusion</note>. **Marcus Cicero** in the "Old Art" <note>referring to *De Inventione*</note> speaks of an excess of splendor, festivity, and **elegance** <note>original: "concinnitudo"</note>. **No man** <note>original: "Nemo homo"</note>: although by "nemo" <note>nobody</note> a man <note>homo</note> is understood, nevertheless 

<margin>Gorgias</margin>
he adds "man" after the custom of the ancients, figuratively as an **archaism** <note>original: "ἀρχαϊσμὸμ"</note>. **Terence** in *The Brothers*: "That no man was a brother of the first arts." **Neither Gorgias.** This orator from Leontini <note>a famous Greek Sophist</note> is described in **Plato** as professing that he would debate on any matter that anyone wished to hear; he is said to have opened a school of speaking for the youth of Athens. **Cicero** mentions him in the third volume of *The Orator*, and 

<margin>Zeno</margin>
concerning famous authors. **Zeno**: the leader of the Stoics used to demonstrate with his hand what the difference was between dialectic and eloquence; there was also a Zeno (the one 

<margin>Protagoras</margin>
meant here), the **Eleatic** orator, who prepared the greatest strengths for speaking, having mastered the judicial genre alone. **Nor Protagoras.** This man from Abdera in Thrace was the most famous Sophist of his time, whom **Apuleius** in the fourth book of his *Florida* writes was widely learned and eloquent among the first inventors of rhetoric; **Marcus Tullius** [Cicero] mentions him in the third book of *On the Orator*, and **Diogenes Laertius** mentions him in his ninth volume. **But show us.** Greedy for seeing the merchandise and the most rare thing proclaimed by **Buttubatta**, he is urged to **show** or **exhibit** <note>original: "exhibendum"</note> it. 

<margin>Exhibit / Restore</margin>
However, to **exhibit** is less than to **restore**. He **exhibits** who provides the presence of the body, as the legal consultant **Gaius** writes. He **restores**, however, who additionally makes one a possessor. It is also more to **exhibit** than to "stand in court," as **Labeo** <note>a famous Roman jurist</note> prefers. **Cicero**, in the third volume of *On Duties*, seems to take "exhibiting" for that which is "doing business."

It also contrasts legal terms regarding the physical production of items or persons versus their legal restoration.</summary>

# ACT ONE
### PAGE XXVII

->**ACT ONE**<-

**BUTTUBATTA.** I shall produce it! But truly, it shall not be produced!
**LIXA.** No, you produce it! **HELIUS.** Produce it! **SANNIO.** Produce it now!
**BUTTUBATTA.** Violence! Violence! Triumvirs! Triumvirs! Hey! Ho!
I appeal to the judge’s tribunal. You are harming me!
**LIXA.** You’ll give it up. **SALARUS.** Do you have it? **BUTTUBATTA.** Stop, you gallows-birds, you thieves!
**SALARUS.** Do you have it? **LIXA.** Immediately! **ARISTO.** Do you have it? **BUTTUBATTA.** You wicked men!
O parricides, matricides, you shall by no means <note>sacrilegious ones</note>
seize it, even on this day! **HELIUS.** Drag him! Seize him!
**BUTTUBATTA.** Today, O greedy one, you cannot tear it away from me.

**"But truly it shall not be produced"** <note>original: "Enimuero nec exhibebitur"</note>. As if to say: I will not be driven to produce it by those words. For "but truly" <note>original: "enimuero"</note> is a part [of speech] that signifies a very moved and angry mind. It sometimes makes a beginning for someone about to say something in anger, but it must be assisted by the face and the pronunciation. **Cicero**: "Here, with others on the other side, it must truly be endured" <note>original: "enimuero ferendu est"</note>. **Terence** in the *Phormio*: "To court, truly" <note>original: "In ius enimuero"</note>. "**You**" <note>original: "Tu"</note> is used significantly; as if to say: do you think it is in your power not to produce it? **Buttubatta [cries] "violence, violence"** <note>original: "uim uim"</note>. That is, "I suffer [violence]"; it is elliptical <note>meaning some words are omitted</note> and exclamatory. He calls upon the **Triumvirs** <note>original: "triuuiros"; a board of three Roman magistrates</note> as the guardians of the prison. Thus **Plautus** in the *Asinaria*: "I will go to the Three Men, and I will make sure your names are marked for capital punishment there." And in the *Aulularia*: "Because I will now report your name to the Three Men." There were two types of Triumvirs: the **Monetales** <note>mint-masters</note>, by whose diligence coins were made, and those who had custody of the prison. **Pedianus** mentions them in his commentary on the *Verrines*. Others add the **Triumvirs for establishing a colony**.

<margin>Triumvirs</margin>
"Triumvir" is a word composed from "man" <note>Latin: "uir"</note> and a numerical noun <note>Latin: "tres"</note>. **Varro** in *On Analogy*, book II, and in book IV of *Epistolary Questions*; and **Priscian** in *On the Names of Weights*; also **Phocas**. **Sallust** proves in the *Catiline* that the Triumvirs were put in charge of punishments; and **Plautus** in the *Amphitryon* where he says: "What shall I do now if the Triumvirs thrust me into prison? From there I shall be brought out tomorrow from the storage cell to the lash." Hence they are called **Triumviral lashes** <note>flagella triuuiralia</note>. "**Hey! Ho!**" <note>original: "Heus ohe"</note> is the voice of one shouting like a Bacchic reveler. **Servius** on Book VII of the *Aeneid*: "Alas! Ho! Bacchus roaring." Regarding this verse, **Mariano** [notes that] **Donatus** in the [commentary on] *Phormio* wants satiety to be signified by this voice. **Juvenal** is satisfied when **Horace** says "Ho!" <note>original: "ohe"; implies "that's enough"</note>. Some say it is the interjection of one laughing, citing **Persius**: "Ho! what it is to have learned." "**I appeal to the tribunal**" <note>original: "Appello tribunal"</note>. I provoke an appeal to the...

<margin>Ho!</margin>
...tribunal. We say "I appeal to the judge's tribunal" without a preposition. With the word "provoke" <note>original: "puoco"</note>, we frequently add a preposition <note>such as "I appeal *to*"</note>. **Titus Livy** teaches that appeals were devised against the injuries of magistrates. The "**Tribunal**" took its name from the **Tribes**, because the tribes are called together to it so that they may receive justice from the judge; but it is a higher place from

<margin>Tribunal</margin>
where everyone can be heard. In the camps, **military and camp tribunals** were made from heaps of turf. **Lucan** in the fifth book of the *Pharsalia*: "He stood fearless on the mound of piled turf." "**You are harming**" <note>original: "Leditis"</note>. Not only do you offend, for "to harm" <note>original: "ledere"</note> is more than "to offend." **Vergil**: "By what divinity harmed?" <note>original: "quo numine leso"</note>. **Plautus** in the *Captivi*...

# ACT ONE
### PAGE XXVIII

->**ACT ONE**<-

<margin>I harm, I offend</margin> "I remember when he did not dare do it by word, now he may harm by deed." <note>This sentence seems to be a general observation on the changing boldness of the characters</note> Cease, rest, pause, desist. There is a proper difference between *to cease* <note>original: "cessare"</note> and *to rest* <note>original: "requiescere"</note>. <margin>I cease</margin> For the idle man "ceases," while the exhausted man "rests." <margin>Fork-bearer</margin> **Fork-bearers** <note>original: "furciferi"</note> are thieves. This must be explained more broadly here. Among the ancients, masters had such power over their slaves that they could not only bind them but even kill them; for a light offense, they were punished more for the sake of ignominy than for actual torment. They would carry a "fork" <note>a V-shaped wooden yoke</note> on their necks around the neighborhood, with their hands tied to it, and were forced to proclaim their sin and warn others not to commit anything similar. From this comes the name "fork-bearer" as a slave's insult and a term of reproach. The Greeks call them *skeuophorous* <note>original: "σκευοφόρους", meaning baggage-carriers</note>; Plutarch and Donatus are the authors who mention this. Those who bound these slaves by order of the masters were called *lorarii* <note>whip-bearers</note>, *Brutiani*, or *lictores*. **You hold, you retain** <note>original: "tenes, retines"</note>; properly, he "retains" who holds something against someone else’s will. In Terence’s *The Brothers*: "I believe it is better to retain children [by kindness]."

**Immediately** <note>original: "statim"</note>; without delay. **Wicked men** <note>original: "scelesti"</note>. In comic wit, these are the sharpest reproaches against actors, slaves, and maidservants in the works of comic playwrights. There are others that are not without charm, such as *venefica* <note>poisoner or witch</note>, *lupa* <note>she-wolf or prostitute</note>, *vipera* <note>viper</note>, and *sacrilega* <note>sacrilegious woman</note>; but "wickedness" <note>scelus</note> and "full of wickedness" <note>scelerosus</note> carry more weight than "wicked" <note>scelestus</note>. <margin>Parricide</margin> **Parricides**. By a common word, we call "parricides" those whom the Greeks call *patroktonous* <note>original: "πατροκτόνους", father-killers</note> and Plautus calls *parenticidas*. The Pompeian Law regarding parricides keeps watch over that class of men. **Matricides** are those who attack their mothers, like Orestes, about whom Sophocles writes in the play titled *Electra* <note>original: "Ηλέκτρα"</note>. Cicero used the word **sister-killer** <note>original: "fororicida"</note>; he says, "parricide, brother-killer, sister-killer." "By no means" <note>original: "neutiquam"</note> is comic and ancient. **You seize** <note>original: "prehenditis"</note>; used aptly and properly. For we "seize" those who are avoiding us and fleeing. Terence in *The Lady of Andros*: "Your father just now seized me." Sometimes the aspiration (the 'h') is removed from this word, and the three-syllable word becomes two. <margin>Even</margin> **Even** <note>original: "vel"</note>; also. **On this day** <note>original: "hoc die"</note>. The full form; *hodie* <note>today</note> is a compound of the ancient and comic *hoc die*. **Glutton, drag him, snatch him**; they urge each other on to seize him so that he does not slip from their hands and escape. <margin>Nouns in -ax</margin> **O rapacious one** <note>original: "O rapax"</note>. Grammarians note that verbal nouns ending in *-ax* signify both a habit and an abundance of the thing to which the habit belongs. Thus we say *fugacious* <note>tending to flee</note>, *bibacious* <note>tending to drink</note>, and *edacious* <note>tending to eat</note>, when we note both a habit and very frequent actions. **To tear away** <note>original: "avellere"</note>; to remove. Virgil: "And he tears the posts from the hinge."

**LIXA.** Let us all strive with all our might with great effort.
**BUTTUBATTA.** Alas! My cloak <note>original: "penula"</note> and hooded cape <note>original: "bardocucullus"</note> are being destroyed.
They are tearing the fold of my robe <note>original: "sinum"</note>, miserable me! **LIXA.** Give it up! **BUTTUBATTA.** I will not give it!
**HELIUS.** Allow us, so that we may gently look at whatever that thing is
which you are carrying hidden in the fold of your robe;
I pray you, we will return it of our own accord. **BUTTUBATTA.** Do you pledge
your word? **HELIUS.** I pledge it by my word. **BUTTUBATTA.** To be returned immediately? **HELIUS.** Immediately.
**BUTTUBATTA.** Truly, it shall be done now; only let Lixa pull back his hand,
I fear his gasping breath might choke my throat.

# ACT ONE XXIX

Let us try by all means. They whisper these words secretly into their ears, so that what **Buttubatta** suggested does not slip away but remains hidden longer. 

**Let us try** <note>original: "Conemur"</note>: this word implies difficulty. Plautus in *The Ass-Dealer*: "Nor did you previously dare to try to do that." When joined with an object in the accusative case, it signifies "without intermission," as in "night and day, with hands and feet." Virgil in the first book of the *Georgics*: "Three times they tried." 

**By all means** <note>original: "Omnibus modis"</note>: By every way, by every reason. 

**Strenuously** <note>original: "Enixim"</note>: Vehemently. **Strenuously** and **passionately** <note>original: "efflictim"</note> are very elegant adverbs signifying intensity, but **passionately** is more common, signifying vehemently and desperately, just as **abundantly** <note>original: "ubertim"</note> means plentifully and largely. 

**Alas** <note>original: "Heu"</note>: An expression of one grieving. 

**Cloak** <note>original: "Penula"</note> and **hooded cloak** <note>original: "bardocucullus"</note>. The **cloak** is a garment we wear over a tunic; I think it is elsewhere called a **mantle** <note>original: "Mantelū"</note>, a covering or wrap to ward off rain. The Greeks call it a *mandyam*. Plautus uses this word in *The Captives*, and Pliny in book 7 of his *Natural History*, although some deny it is a Latin word. 

The **hooded cloak** and the **Santonic hood** <note>a coarse wool hood from Gaul</note> are named by the Satirist <note>Juvenal</note> in Satires 4 and 6. Likewise Martial. 

**He perishes** <note>original: "Interit, perit"</note>. He is cut off, he is broken; but **to perish utterly** <note>original: "interire"</note> is a stronger term than simply **to perish** <note>original: "perire"</note>. 

**They tear the pocket** <note>original: "Rumpunt sinum"</note>: They strip away the covering. 

**Woe is me** <note>original: "Misero mihi"</note>: As if to say "to me, an innocent man." For we also call the innocent "miserable," according to Sallust. This is why he previously called upon the judge's tribunal, as if he could endure the law if he could escape violence. 

**Permit** <note>original: "Permittito"</note>: He tries another way, to see if the matter could be demanded. 

**Whatever of things** <note>original: "Quodcunq; rerum"</note>: whatever the matter may be that you carry hidden in your pocket. 

**Of one's own accord** <note>original: "Vltro"</note>: willingly, not by compulsion or extortion. 

**You command faith** <note>original: "Iubes fidem"</note>: that is, you wish to pledge your word. 

**I command by faith** <note>original: "Fide iubeo"</note>: that is, I command you by my word <note>a legal formula of guarantee or surety</note>. 

**Immediately** <note>original: "Statim"</note>: continually; and **immediately** signifies "perpetually," "equally," and "in one state." Plautus says: "the statues stand so still." 

**Truly it shall be done** <note>original: "Sane modo fiet"</note>. 

**I fear lest the breath** <note>original: "metuo ne anhelitus"</note>: the breathing, the exhalation. Plautus in *The Merchant*: "I cannot catch my breath." 

**Let him overwhelm** <note>original: "Opprimat"</note>: let him seize. We understand the throat being held as tightly as possible with narrowed jaws.

***

**Lixa**: Stand thus. **Aristarchus** takes flight. **Heliodorus** waits in the meantime.
**SAL**: Stay, you runaway! 
**BVT**: By Pollux, not if you let me go free! I'm fleeing! 
**LIX**: Hey, run swiftly, whoever is best! Grasp his curls with your hands!
You sycophant, by the greatness of your extreme treachery, are you returning now?
**BVT**: These greedy men were turning the way before, so that not even a mouse could gnaw through.
**LIX**: Bind the scoundrel's hands and feet at the same time.

***

**Lixa, stand thus** <note>original: "Lixa sic sta"</note>: He stops Buttubatta, but he escapes from the hands of those grabbing him and takes flight; he sees him fleeing. 

**In the meantime** <note>original: "Inter dum"</note>: The particle **inter** sometimes <margin>entirely</margin> signifies "entirely," as in "I drink entirely" <note>original: "interbibo"</note> in Apuleius, Book 1 of *The Metamorphoses*. "Entirely"... catch him while he waits. 

**Hey, swiftly** <note>original: "Heus perniciter"</note>: Quickly, rapidly, speedily.

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's vocabulary. Having previously discussed clothing and effort, the author now examines terms for physical agility, personal grooming (curls), and the character of a "sycophant." This linguistic analysis leads directly into a scene where Buttubatta attempts to use charming words to avoid being bound by his captors.</meta>

# ACT ONE
### PAGE XXX

->**ACT ONE**<-

<margin>Nimble</margin> **Nimble** <note>original: "Pernix"</note> is indeed swift. Virgil: "Swift on feet and with **nimble** wings." Silius: "A race fiery with **nimble** soles." According to the scholar Servius, **nimble** also signifies "persevering," derived from the word for "striving through" <note>original: "pernitendo"</note>.

<margin>Curl</margin> **Fold your hands in curls.** Entangled in hair. A **curl** <note>original: "cincinnus"</note> is hair twisted and softly bent like the tendrils on grapevines; Varro notes that these little hills [on the vine] are called "capreoli." Hence, the **curled ones** <note>original: "cincinnatí"</note> are long-haired people with twisted and frizzy hair, a term of reproach often cast at the delicate and those over-concerned with their locks. Jerome, writing to Demetrias, says: "Let a virgin avoid boys who are **little curled ones** <note>original: "Cincinnatulos"</note> and those with hair styled by curling irons." I wonder at the glossary of Plautus, which says "twisted and burnt by a curling iron," following the lead of Marcellus, especially as Plautus uses it in *The Captives*. Cicero, in his oration against Lucius Piso, says: "There were those groomed locks, and the moist edges of his **curls** <note>original: "cíncínnorū"</note>." The Satirist [Juvenal] asks: "Why is this **curl** higher?" Jerome, in his commentary on Ezekiel, notes that Martial calls twisted hair a "ring."

<margin>Sycophant</margin> **Sycophant.** They drag him back, seized by the hair. A **sycophant** <note>original: "Sycophanta"</note> is bold, wickedly deceptive, and a slanderer; the word is drawn from the Greek. For they use the word **to play the sycophant** <note>Greek: συκοφαντεῖσθαι</note> to mean "to slander." Thus, sycophants are those whose business it is to disturb a good situation and tend to a bad one—they are deceitful and treacherous. From this, the word **sycophantries** <note>original: "sycophantię"</note> is used as a term by Plautus for deceptions, craftiness, and tricks.

<margin>Greatness</margin> **Greatness.** Meaning magnitude. Accius in *Vacres*: "For it can neither be spoken, imagined, nor said because of its **greatness** <note>original: "magnitate"</note>," referring to extreme treachery.

<margin>Outermost / Extreme</margin> **Outermost** and **Extreme.** Meaning the last or furthest. From the word "extra" [outside], two superlatives are formed: **outermost** <note>original: "extimus"</note> and **extreme** <note>original: "extremus"</note>, as the ancient grammarian Priscian noted regarding superlative nouns.

<margin>Whether</margin> **Whether.** The particle **num** attaches to both the subjunctive and indicative moods. Diomedes is the authority on this. It also sometimes takes the suffix *-nam* for emphasis. Terence: "Ah! **whether indeed** <note>original: "num nam"</note> we are perishing?"

<margin>I precede</margin> **I precede.** They anticipate, they seize beforehand, they arrive first; derivations from the word "to turn" <note>original: "uerto"</note> used by the ancients include "to turn aside," "to prefer," and "to turn around."

<margin>Graspers</margin> **Graspers.** Plautus in *Pseudolus*: "Those **graspers** <note>original: "appetones"</note>, so greedy for me and **grasping** <note>original: "appetētes"</note>, who desire to restrain me with such greed." **Would gnaw away.** Would corrupt by gnawing, or would escape. **Bind the camp-follower.** Acting cautiously, they wish to bind the hands of the **camp-follower** <note>original: "Lixa"</note>.

**BUTTUBATTA.** No, my friend, camp-follower and other accomplices,
**Bind** your pleasant and well-suited **drunkard** <note>original: "bibesium"</note>,
**And** this excellent tavern-haunter and dry rival.
**ARISTIPPUS.** O flatterer, no one is a more charming stroker.
**SAL.** O sweet-talking words! **LIXA.** By the faith of the gods!
**Oh**, you are each worth **less than a tuft of wool** <note>original: "flocciōres uellere"</note>,
**Then** lighter than pumice stone, as if you do not apply
**Either** iron shackles or manacles to this man.

**Buttubatta [says] "No, my friend."** See how coaxingly he pleads against being put in chains; he says "friend" and "accomplices" and "your best" and "excellent," thus he begs them not to bind him. **Accomplices.** He uses a good and decent word for this company of actors; Sallust mentions it when he describes the dregs of Catiline's comrades.

<vocab>Pernix (Nimble), Cincinnus (Curl), Sycophanta (Sycophant), Magnitas (Greatness), Extimus (Outermost), Extremus (Extreme), Num (Whether), Anteuorto (I precede), Appetones (Graspers), Plautus, Cicero, Terence, Jerome, Lixa (Camp-follower)</vocab>
