--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the play's dialogue, moving from the physical description of the sycophant's hair to his character as a glutton and a flatterer. The commentator continues to cite classical authorities like Cicero, Plautus, and Virgil to define the specific insults and technical terms used in the scene where Buttubatta is apprehended.</meta>

### PAGE XXXI

->**ACT ONE**<-

<margin>Bibesius</margin> [The text describes him as] pleasant and fit. The opposite of "fit" <note>original: "apto"</note> is "unfit" <note>original: "ineptus"</note>, for which the Greeks lack a single equivalent term, as Cicero writes in the second book of *On the Orator*. **Bibesius** <note>original: "bibesius"</note> means one greedy for drinking, just as *peredius* means one greedy for eating; you can find this in Plautus’s *Casina*, though more common terms are **bibacious**, **bibous**, or **bibulous** <note>original: "bibax, bibosus, & bibulus"</note>. 

<margin>Popino</margin> An **excellent tavern-hunter** <note>original: "Popinonem egregium"</note> refers to a luxurious banqueter, a gluttonous fellow-feaster and companion, a tavern-dweller and notorious debauchee. The name comes from the *popina* <note>the Latin word for a cook-shop or tavern</note>, where such men devote themselves to snatching up delicacies. The ancients called them **plate-lickers** <note>original: "catilones"</note>, while the Greeks called them **profligates** <note>original: "ἀσώτους" (asotous)</note>. 

<margin>Catilones</margin> Our own writers call them eaters, devourers, gluttons, chewers, and big-eaters. 

<margin>Chewer / Big-eaters</margin> <margin>Manduco Mandones</margin> An imitator of Acaridus <note>likely a reference to a known gluttonous character</note>, one who is dry, thirsty, and far from sober. **Palpo** <note>a flatterer or "stroker"</note> uses **caressing words** <note>original: "blandicella verba"</note> as Aristophorus cries out both mockingly and laughingly, indicating that the man is forced to use charming pleas. 

**O palpo!** This means "O flatterer!" Plautus says in *The Merchant*: "you strike with a flatterer's hand." Horace writes: "To whom it goes badly if one strokes <note>original: "palpare"</note> him, as he kicks back, safe on all sides." It also means to soothe or soften. Apuleius, in the fifth book of *The Golden Ass*, speaks of "caressing words" <note>original: "blandicella verba"</note>; we call words "caressing" as a diminutive for "charming" and "soft" or "honeyed." Festus notes that a **Lixa** <note>originally a camp-follower or water-carrier</note> impatiently endures his accomplices being led astray and deceived by the slippery flatteries of Buttubatta. He therefore cries out to bind him and hinder him with chains. 

<margin>Proh</margin> **Proh!** This is an exclamation of one suffering an injury, almost tragic in tone. Ovid writes in book thirteen: "Proh! Jupiter, he said, before the ships..." Virgil writes: "Proh! Jupiter, will he go?" Sallust in *The Catiline Conspiracy* says: "Proh! By the faith of gods and men!" Priscian notes in book fourteen that when placed as an interjection, it receives a circumflex accent. 

<margin>Floccus</margin> **More trifling** <note>original: "flocciores"</note> means lighter or more trivial. It comes from **flocks** <note>original: "floccus"</note>, which are the small protruding bits of wool on cloth or a fleece that are clipped off with shears. Cornelius Celsus <note>a Roman medical writer</note> says it is a fatal sign in a sick man if he starts "picking at flocks" <note>fidgeting with the bedclothes</note>. Pliny mentions this very frequently. From "flock" comes "little flock," as drawn from Pliny's sixteenth book regarding oak trees. 

<margin>Floccifacio</margin> From this we get the verb **floccifacio**, meaning "to despise" or "to make of no account," or to value something no more than a stray bit of wool. 

<margin>Naucifacio</margin> Plautus says of a pimp in *The Rope*: "He did not give a flock's worth of credit." Furthermore, that most Latin of poets uses **naucifacere** <note>to value at a nutshell</note> for the same meaning. 

<margin>Deflocco / Deflacco</margin> Additionally, the verb **defloccare** means to wear down or rub away, though in the writings of Marcellus the word is corrupted to **deflaccare** in that sense. For *flaccus* means languid, or in ancient speech, shriveled and soft; hence *deflaccare*, *inflaccare* (ancient words), and "flaccid."

<margin>Pumice</margin> **By a pumice stone.** A light, porous stone. Virgil <note>referred to here as "Maro"</note> treated **pumice** as masculine in gender, while Catullus treated it as feminine. Plautus devised an even lighter weight: he compares a pimp's word to a piece of straw—the lightest of little creatures—and elsewhere says "lighter than a feather," used for the worst and most wicked men. There was an ancient proverb about "asking for water from a pumice stone" <note>meaning to try to get something from a person who has nothing to give, similar to "blood from a stone"</note>. Plautus uses this in *The Persian*. 

**Fetters** <note>original: "compedes"</note>. These are named not just from "feet" <note>pedes</note> but from "impediment." 

<margin>Fetters</margin> Juvenal writes: "The filthy ditch-digger loathes his great **fetter**." Among the types of bonds, the slave in Plautus's *Asinaria* counts "fetters"—that is, snares and shackles. Hence "fettered" <note>compediti</note> means "bound." 

<margin>To Fetter</margin> **Compedio** is a verb, just like *impedio* (to impede). Varro says: "I bind the feet with straps." There is a saying of Cato well worth remembering: "Thieves of private property spend their lives in the stocks and **fetters**; but thieves of public property spend theirs in gold and purple." 

<margin>Manacles</margin> **Manacles** <note>original: "manicas"</note> now refer to chains. Virgil says: "He orders the **manacles** and tight bonds to be removed." Elsewhere it signifies a part of clothing, as in Virgil’s eleventh book: "And the **sleeves** of the tunic."

<summary>This page provides a detailed philological commentary on insults and terms related to gluttony, flattery, and physical restraint (fetters and manacles). The author uses these definitions to clarify the character of the sycophant Buttubatta and the legal/social context of his apprehension.</summary>
<keywords>gluttony, flattery, tavern-hunters, floccifacio, pumice stone, fetters, manacles, Plautus, Cicero, Virgil, Latin etymology</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the play's dialogue. While the previous page focused on the physical and moral description of the characters (the drunkard and the glutton), this page delves into the specific verbal exchanges of Act One, providing a word-by-word breakdown of the insults and onomatopoeic sounds used as the characters confront Buttubatta.</meta>

### PAGE XXXI

->**ACT ONE**<-

**BIBESIUS.** Ble! **SALAX.** You mutter. **BIBESIUS.** Ble! **ARISTOPHORUS.** You babble. **BIBESIUS.** Ble! **LIXA.** You bleat.
**BUTTUBATTA.** Why not? Since I am a sheep, you pluck my wool so, and you skin my head; keep yourselves away from here!
**LIXA.** Why, you rogue, do you mock your faithful comrades with lying words and hold them in contempt?
**SALAX.** Furthermore, why do you keep the minds of this fellowship in suspense?
**HERMENEUS.** Are you not ashamed to hide what you had promised to show?

<margin>Ble</margin>
**Ble.** Diomedes <note>a 4th-century Latin grammarian</note> says that this is an "onomatopoeia," which is when we invent and shape a word to imitate a confused sound. This can be seen in the works of Donatus <note>a famous commentator on Terence</note> regarding the crying of infants in the play *The Mother-in-Law*, and regarding the "barrus" or elephant, named for the sound of its voice, which is said to "barrire" <note>to trumpet</note>. **You mutter.** Salax does not clearly understand what this sound [Ble] is meant to signify; hence he says "you mutter," using the word improperly to describe that sound. To **mutter** <note>original: "mussitare"</note> means to complain silently with a murmuring sound, derived either from the word "mute" or from the letter *M*, which is a sound produced by pressing the lips together and has almost no volume. Hence Apuleius <note>author of The Golden Ass</note> writes, "having muttered away the temporary insult," and Virgil writes, "King Latinus himself mutters."

<margin>To feast</margin>
However, the word **mutitare** <note>to feast together</note> used by Aulus Gellius is different, for it signifies the act of holding reciprocal banquets.

<margin>To bleat</margin>
**You babble.** This properly denotes boasting about oneself foolishly with a loud shout and speaking very eagerly; it is also the sound of camels. It is read in Horace <note>Satires 2.7.35</note> with the first syllable shortened: "you babble with a great shout and flee." **You bleat.** This is a frequentative form of the verb "to bleat" <note>original: "balo"</note>, which indicates a repeated action and is quite fitting for expressing impudence. Varro <note>an ancient Roman scholar</note>, in the second book of his *On Agriculture*, explains this sound with an appropriate term. For **Ble** is the voice of sheep, which are said to **bleat**. Ovid (if the short work *On the Voices of Animals* is truly his, which I approve of only with hesitation) writes: "but the pious sheep bleats." Plautus uses the frequentative form in his play *The Bacchides*: "since these sheep go about bleating so." **Why not:** as if to say, "Should I not bleat?" or "Why shouldn't I?" **You skin.** <note>original: "pilatis"</note> That is, you depilate, you pull out the hairs and make one bald. From this we get the terms "to pilfer" <note>original: "suppilare"</note> and "to pillage" <note>original: "compilare"</note>. **Keep yourselves away from here:** leave at once, go back.

<margin>To mock</margin>
**You mock.** You play the fool, you deceive, you lead someone on; for the verb "to mock" <note>original: "ludo"</note> functions this way when it takes the accusative case, as in Plautus's *The Three-Dollar Day*: "I shall mock the man properly." **Furthermore.** Moreover. **Why do you keep:** why do you delay, why do you detain? **In suspense:** keeping them anxious through ambiguity.

<margin>Shame and Regret</margin>
**Are you not ashamed.** Do you not feel awe? Are you not possessed of that modesty which would make you stop? To be **ashamed** <note>original: "pudere"</note> pertains to modesty, just as to **regret** <note>original: "pigere"</note> pertains to repentance.

<margin>To pledge and To promise</margin>
**What you had promised.** This refers to something he was not only asked but even forced to pledge. Otherwise, "pledging" and "promising" <note>original: "pollicitatio & promissio"</note> have the same force, except that a "pledge" <note>original: "pollicitatio"</note> involves greater flattery. To "promise" <note>original: "promittere"</note> means not just to pledge, but also to allow something to grow to a great size; hence we speak of a "promised beard" <note>meaning a long, unshaven beard</note>.

**ARISTOPHORUS.** Are you not sick of such varied lying?
**LIXA.** Or do you perhaps think we are little donkeys?

<summary>This page analyzes a scene where the character Buttubatta is mocked by his companions using animal sounds and sheep-related metaphors. The commentary provides extensive philological notes on the verbs for muttering, babbling, and bleating, citing classical authorities to define the nuances of Roman insults.</summary>
<keywords>onomatopoeia, Diomedes, Plautus, etymology, Roman comedy, bleating, shame vs regret, promise vs pledge, Latin grammar</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's dialogue. While the previous page dealt with the sound of "bleating" and muttering, this page examines the birds and animals used as insults (cuckoos, owls, hens, and pigs) and explores the grammatical origins of words like "autumo" and "instar," citing a wide range of classical authorities to explain the characters' word choices.</meta>

### PAGE XXXIII

->**ACT ONE**<-

**SALAX.** Do you think we are cuckoos or birds of the underworld?
**HERMENEUS.** What? Is it your opinion that we cackle?
Like a hen, or crow like roosters 
usually do so foolishly? We are also
men, and we consider it a fault to be despised.

<margin>I assert</margin>
So varied, so manifold, so wicked. Do you "assert," "opine," "estimate," or "judge"? That is what the word **autumo** <note>original: "autumo"</note> signifies. It is a word very frequently used by Plautus, and as Gellius writes in Book 15, Publius Nigidius thought it was composed from the preposition *ab* and the verb *aestimo* <note>to estimate</note>; he thought it was spoken in a shortened way as *autumo* as if it were *abaestimo*, which means "I value the whole" just as "I count out." But this interpretation by Nigidius seems more clever than true. Apuleius writes: "They assert <note>autumant</note> they dream of cruel and heavy things." **Cuckoos.** This is a very famous bird in our world; in Greek it is called *kokkyx* <note>original: κόκκυξ</note> from the word *kokkyzo* <note>original: κοκκύζω</note>, which we call "cuckooing."

<margin>Cuckoo</margin>
<margin>Owl</margin>
**Birds of Cocytus.** That is, birds of mourning—namely, owls. **To cackle.** This is the voice of hens; a rooster is accustomed to crow, while a hen "cackles" <note>original: "gracillat"</note>. Ovid, in his poem on the voices of animals, contends that men are not "cackling" by using the argument of the thinness of the voice, since a man’s voice is greater; Aristotle discusses this matter in his *Problems*. Furthermore, Lucretius proves that the voice is a physical body in his fourth book on philosophy.

<margin>Likeness</margin>
**Like a hen.** Servius the grammarian denies that the word **instar** <note>meaning "like" or "the likeness of"</note> can take a preposition, although the lyric poet Serenus said "ad instar" <note>to the likeness</note>. In Apuleius, a most Latinate writer, you will also very frequently find it joined to a preposition, especially in the *Metamorphoses* and in his booklet on the teachings of Plato. Likewise, it appears in "coram" <note>in the presence of</note> by way of an **archaism** <note>original: ἀρχαϊσμὸν</note>. The grammarian Phocas says that all words ending in *instar* are declined according to the third declension, as does Priscian in his booklet on accents. Festus Pompeius says the same in his entry on the North wind, and so on. I am weary of bringing forward more examples to refute the dogma of Servius.

<margin>Man</margin>
**We are also men.** The name **man** <note>original: "vir"</note> is usually used as a term of praise. Terence writes: "I judge you a man," and in *The Eunuch*: "at last he felt the pain, you are a man." It was an ancient insult for those reproaching laziness, by which someone was denied to be a man. Quintus Curtius in Book 8: "He began to demand that if he were a man, he should settle the matter with him the next day with the sword." Sometimes the word is added to animals to signify a "husband" or "mate"; Virgil writes: "The goat himself is the man of the flock." There are also times when "man" refers to age and integrity rather than to gender.

<margin>To be despised</margin>
**To be despised.** The word is **spernier** <note>original: "spernier"</note>; the "er" is added in the manner of the comic poets by a "paragoge" <note>adding a letter to the end of a word</note>, just as *potestur* is used for *potest* <note>is able</note>, according to Diomedes. **We consider it a fault.** We disparage or blame. This is a phrase from Terence’s *Andria*.

**ASISTUS.** Rather men, not women. **BUTTUBATTA.** By Hercules, 
I judge you to be heavy pigs, swine, 
and sows! By Pollux, 
humans never grunt in the manner of pigs.
**LIXA.** And you are hardly human, but an exceedingly aborted monster.

<summary>This page provides a scholarly analysis of insults used in the play, focusing on animal metaphors like cuckoos and pigs, while citing classical grammarians to explain archaic Latin forms and the origins of specific verbs.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, Plautus, cuckoo, owl, hen, masculinity, grammar, Servius, animal sounds, insults</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from bird-related insults to insults involving pigs and women. It features a heavy philological focus on the etymologies of "woman" (femina) and "human" (homo), as well as the adverbial forms of animal sounds (such as "grunting like a pig"). It continues the dialogue between Salax and Hermeneus as they trade insults in the style of Roman comedy.</meta>

[PAGE XXXIIII]

->ACT ONE<-

# **Wicked and shameless pimp, thrice-thief**

**SALAX.** Grave-robber, runaway scoundrel, and fugitive!
**HERMENEUS.** Ally in fraud, branded one, most worthless of the worthless!

<margin>Woman</margin>
"Nay, even more" <note>original: "Immo etiam"</note>. With this adverb, he shows that one acts promptly and insistently on what was promised. Not women. Suitable authors think women <note>original: "fœminas"</note> are named from the thigh <note>original: "femen"</note>, either because the distinction of sex is in the thigh, or because they have thicker thighs, or because they are more prone to lust, the seat of which is said to be in the thighs. Juvenal says, "And he forced the lady’s upper thigh to cry out." However it may be, it is established in Aristotle's *On Animals*, Volume 9, that women are more malicious, more cunning, and more treacherous; Pittacus <note>One of the Seven Sages of Greece</note> teaches that they ought to be ruled by men. Conversely, males are more spirited, simpler, and less treacherous. Therefore, the arts, deceits, frauds, and snares are said to be familiar to women. There are proverbial verses among the Greeks regarding women. Such as: "A bad woman is a treasure-trove of evils" <note>original: "θησαυρὸς ἐστὶ τῶν κακῶν κακὴ γυνὴ"</note>. Likewise: "A bad woman is more savage than all wild beasts" <note>original: "ἁπάντων ἀγριώτερα κακὴ γυνὴ"</note>. Likewise: "Sea and fire and woman: three evils" <note>original: "θάλασσα καὶ πῦρ καὶ γυνὴ κακὰ τρία"</note>. Likewise: "Every woman is bitterness; but she has two good hours: one in the bedroom, one in death" <note>original: "πᾶσα γυνὴ χόλος ἐστὶν ἔχει δ’ ἀγαθὰς δύο ὥρας τὴν μίαν ἐν θαλάμῳ τὴν μίαν ἐν θανάτῳ"</note>. And that of Aesop: "The race of women is fickle; in whom it is not granted to see faithfulness" <note>original: "κoῦφoν τὸ τῶν γυναικῶν ἐστὶ phylon"</note>. But against their greed there is that proverb: "Neither a woman nor a lap <note>perhaps referring to the "lap of the sea" or a calm sea</note> should be trusted." But now for the retreat...

<margin>Pig-wise, Dog-wise</margin>
...I shall sing, for no one enjoys revenge more than a woman. And the wise Periander says: "Please everyone" <note>original: "πᾶσιν ἀρέσκε"</note>. He easily proves that the men are not pigs from the uncomposed shouting of those grunting **pig-wise** <note>original: "suatim"</note>. "Porcus" <note>pig</note> is a Sabine word, as Marcus Varro writes.

<margin>I grunt, The Grundile Lares</margin>
**Swine.** "Sus" <note>swine</note> is said from the Greek; for they say *sys, syos* <note>original: "σῦς σὺόσ"</note>, that is, "swine, of a swine." They want it understood that "scrophae" <note>sows</note> are those that have given birth many times, and "sues" those that have once. In the feminine gender, Horace used "swine"; Virgil used it in the masculine. Aulus Gellius, Book 18: **Suatim**, in the manner of swine, just as **canatim** <note>dog-wise</note> and **bouatim** <note>ox-wise</note> according to the author Nigidius in his grammatical commentary. **To grunt** <note>original: "grunnire"</note> is the cry of swine; see Festus, Cicero in the *Tusculan Disputations*, and Juvenal’s *Satires*, 15. The ancients also used to say **grundire**, whence the **Grundile Lares** <note>Household gods associated with a legendary sow</note> are named, established in Rome in honor of a sow that had given birth to thirty piglets. Diomedes [notes that] the Roman Emperor Antoninus Geta maintained a habit of proposing questions to grammarians, asking how each animal emitted its voice. **Onomatopoeia** is a trope involving the coining of a name based on sound; it was held among the Greeks as a great virtue...

<margin>Human</margin>
...hardly permitted to us; Quintilian, Book 8. And "you are hardly a human" <note>original: "homo"</note>. The name "human" <note>homo</note> includes both male and female; see Servius Sulpicius in his letter to Cicero. And that of the satirist [Juvenal] proves it: "Though you may confound the sea and sky together, I am human." Varro says that a human is a bubble <note>original: "bulla"; a metaphor for the fragility of life</note>. However, there are various definitions of a human among writers. Philosophers say we consist of soul, body, and shadow. In Holy Scripture...

<keywords>etymology, misogyny, animal sounds, Lares Grundiles, Aristotle, Varro, Greek proverbs, onomatopoeia</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological examination of insults and the human condition, moving from gendered insults (women and men) to the philosophical definition of "human" and specific legal/theatrical terms for criminals and pimps.</meta>

->ACT ONE<-

[PAGE XXXV]

The poets agree in part, saying that man is ash and mere dust. Persius says: "You will become ash, a ghost, and a story" <note>original: "Cinis & manes & fabula fies"</note>. Horace says: "We are dust and shadow" <note>original: "Puluis & umbra sumus"</note>. Phocylides also plays with this theme: "we are dust being dissolved" <note>original: "λυομένοι κόνις ἐσμὲν"</note>. What is man? Seneca asks. He answers: "a broken and fragile vessel, naked and by nature unarmed, needing help from others, and exposed to all the insults of fortune," etc. And as the prologue of Plautus says, "whom the gods treat like a ball." And yet, while nothing is more wretched or fragile than man, at the same time nothing is prouder or more cruel. Hence, the character in Plautus’s *The Merchant* shows most elegantly that "man is a wolf to man." Plato writes that "man is a plaything of God." Furthermore, he defined man with this statement: "Man is a two-legged animal without feathers" <note>original: "Animal homo est bipes sine pennis"</note>, a definition that did not escape the criticism of Diogenes, because it was too general and vague.

**Abortive** <note>original: "Abortiuũ"</note>: which we also call "hidden away" <note>original: "abditiuũ"</note>. He means to call him an "abortive monster," which is not a common insult; it means something most foul, immature, and unformed. 

**Pimp** <note>original: "Leno"</note>: This name is sacrilegious and unjust, belonging to one who commits iniquity and acts as a patron of injustice. It is therefore a most bitter insult, as in Terence: "Bah, you pimp!" <note>original: "Vah leno"</note>. This differs from a "procurer" <note>original: "perductor"</note>.
<margin>Pimp</margin>
For a procurer is one who deals with those persons for whom shameful acts are punished by law. A pimp deals in harlots. According to Ulpian in the fourth book of the *Digests*, one is said to "act as a pimp" <note>original: "lenociniũ facere"</note>. You will also find the verb "to pimp" <note>original: "lenocinari"</note> in the letters of Pliny the Younger where he says, "when necessity pimps for him." Likewise, "although novelty may pimp for this book."

**Thrice-thief** <note>original: "Trifurcifer"</note>: the "tri-" particle increases the intensity. Plautus in *The Pot of Gold*: "Not a thief, but a thrice-thief" <note>original: "Non fur sed trifur"</note>. A penalty was established by Solon in his laws for anyone who violated or threw down a tomb <note>original: "bustum"</note>.

**Fugitive**: a slave’s insult. Moreover, Varro used the term "fugitiuarium" <note>original: "fugitiuarium"</note> to refer to a place for runaways.
<margin>Place for runaways</margin>

**Ally in fraud** <note>original: "Socifraude"</note>: one who is treacherous toward their companions.

## ARISTIPPUS. Do you not answer? You are worthy of the cross!

## BUTAS. Have you finally finished your speech?
You wretched slaves to blasphemy, devoid of all good,
Did you really think this joke could be wrung
Out of my heart? It should have been handled differently
With a malicious and infamous man
Who has no regard for honor or disgrace.

<margin>Finish speaking</margin>
**Worthy of the cross** <note>original: "digne qui crucium feras"</note>: This refers to the punishment. We derive the noun *crucium* (torment) from *crux* (cross) by analogy, just as *lucium* comes from *lux* (light). Apuleius says: "I wish to go before the dawn" <note>original: "antelucio"</note>. A *cruciarius* is one worthy of torture. Lucilius used the term "cross-wine" <note>original: "cruciũ uinũ"</note> <note>referring to wine so sour it is like torture</note>. From "cross," Plautus also derived *crucifalum* <note>a torture-device</note> in *The Bacchis Sisters* and "tormentability" <note>original: "cruciabilitatẽ"</note> in *The Casket Comedy*.

<margin>Worthless</margin>
**Most worthless** <note>original: "Nequissime"</note>: Aulus Gellius has a chapter on the proper use of the word "worthless" <note>original: "neqʒ"</note> in Book 7, Chapter 11. 

**Have you finally finished your speech?** <note>original: "Perorastis ne tandẽ"</note>: Meaning, have you finally ended your oration? At other times, the word *porare* signifies "to obtain." Donatus uses it in his commentary on *The Mother-in-Law*. Quintilian uses it regarding the corpse of a son. Lucan uses it in the tenth book of the *Pharsalia*: "the unchaste face pleads" <note>original: "facies incesta porat"</note>.

<summary>This page analyzes philosophical and poetic definitions of humanity—emphasizing man's fragility and capacity for cruelty—alongside a legal and linguistic breakdown of insults like "pimp," "thrice-thief," and "fugitive." It also provides philological commentary on a dialogue exchange involving the threat of crucifixion as a punishment.</summary>
<keywords>humanity, Plautus, pimp, legal terminology, crucifixion, Seneca, Plato, etymology, Roman comedy</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on terms used in classical drama, transitioning from character insults to grammatical particles, technical tools mentioned in literature, and the use of moral maxims (sententiae) in comedy.</meta>

### ACT ONE

[PAGE XXXVI]

Cicero writes more on the peroration <note>The final part of a speech, intended to inspire the audience</note> in the second book of *On Invention*, which Victorinus calls the epilogue. **Finally** <note>original: "Tandem"</note>. This is an "expletive" particle <note>A word used to fill out a sentence without adding much meaning</note> in this context. Cicero: "How long, **finally**, [Catiline], will you abuse our patience?" <note>original: "Quousque tandem abutere Catilina patientia nostra"</note>. Elsewhere it means "nevertheless," and sometimes "at least." **Vain things** <note>original: "Inania"</note>: empty things or things lacking substance.

<margin>Slave</margin>
**Slaves** <note>original: "Mancipia"</note>. This insult is derived from one's legal status. "Mancipia" are so called because they are "taken by hand" <note>original: "manu capiantur"</note>, and the word "mancipium" is found used for a girl or a maiden in Terence's play *The Eunuch*. **Blasphemous**: spoken ill of or accursed; this is a Greek word. For they pronounce the verb "to blaspheme" <note>original: βλασφημέω</note> as "I speak ill." Or even, "by blaspheming."

<margin>Jewels</margin>
**Jewel** <note>original: "Iocale"</note>. This refers to a precious necklace, a collar, or similar ornament, such as a bracelet, a ring, and other "iocalia" of that kind, which Plautus recalls in his play *The Menaechmi*.

<margin>Drill</margin>
**To be worn out** <note>original: "Extererari"</note>. A **drill** <note>original: "terebra"</note> is an instrument for wearing down or biting through something; more traditional Latin authors treat this tool as a feminine noun.

<margin>To drill</margin>
Vitruvius Pollio, in the tenth volume of *On Architecture*. Pliny, in Book 7, recorded that it was invented by the craftsman Daedalus. Columella, in Book 3, Chapter 9, derived the verb "to drill" <note>original: "terebrare"</note> from the noun "drill" <note>original: "terebra"</note> where he says: "It pleases some of the ancients that the vine be **drilled**." Virgil, in Book 2 of the *Aeneid*: "Or to **drill** the hollows of the belly and test the hiding places." Persius, in his fifth Satire: "to **drill** the salt-cellar with fingers."

<margin>Sawdust</margin>
We call **sawdust** <note>original: "Scobem"</note> that which is worn down and drawn out by a drill or a saw when perforating wood.

<margin>To be able</margin>
**To be able** <note>original: "Poteſſe"</note>. This is an archaism <note>original: ἀρχαϊσμὸς; an old-fashioned form</note> for the standard word *posse*. Plautus in *The Menaechmi*: "Did you think you **could** [potesse] do those outrages in secret?"

<margin>Was able<br/>(archaic forms)</margin>
And "possetur" for *poset* [might be able], "poteratur" for *poterat* [was able], and "potestur" for *potest* [is able] were used by antiquity.

<margin>What delights<br/>in<br/>comedies</margin>
"It had to be done differently." This sentiment is "proverbial" <note>original: παροιμιῶδης</note> and most true, which the speaker in Plautus supports when he says: "To seek justice from the unjust is folly," and that every good man ought to flee the wicked. Solon, in his admonitions, says: "Hate the wicked" <note>original: κακοὺς μίσει</note>. And the most wise Cato says: "Converse with the good" <note>original: ἀγαθοῖς ὁμίλει</note>. Likewise, Pythagoras: "Make whoever is best your friend" <note>original: ποιεῦ Φίλον ὅσιος ἄριστος</note>. It should be known, however, that very serious sentiments are often given to slaves and ridiculous characters for the purpose of delighting the audience. Such as in Plautus, where the soldier admires his own luck, saying: "It is too great a misery for a man to be too handsome." And Sosia in Terence: "**Nothing in excess**" <note>original: "Ne quid nimis"</note>, which sounds like this in Greek: "nothing too much" <note>original: μηδὲν ἄγαν</note>. But because the characters are lowly, the noble sentiment is not dishonored. For this little verse has been celebrated as a proverb by the most ancient people: "Often even a gardener has spoken very opportunely." You will find the nature of such sentiments in Virgil, Cornificius, or Cicero in the fourth book of his *Rhetoric*. **Among the malicious**: a man who is by habit malevolent and infamous, dishonest and defamatory, wicked and impure. **Who has no regard**: no respect.

<margin>Disgrace</margin>
Of honor or **disgrace** <note>original: "probrum"</note>, reproach, shame, or dishonor. The word "probrum" is sometimes taken to mean insults and abuse. Cicero to Atticus: "Many have read letters full of all kinds of insults [probrorum] against me." Ulpian the jurist considers "probrum" and "opprobrium" to be the same thing.

->A worthy man fears being reproached and slandered.<-
->And his reputation being blackened after death by injuries.<-

<keywords>grammar, Cicero, Plautus, Terence, Greek proverbs, ancient tools, insults, legal terms, comedy, sententiae</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological examination of terms used in classical comedy, moving from insults related to social status to the grammatical origins and proper usage of terms like "frugi" (honest/useful) and "iuxta" (equally/nearby).</meta>

### ACT ONE

[PAGE XXXVII]

<sig>D iiii</sig>

> Against any little sycophant of the lowest rank,
> He values praise and insults equally in turn.
> I judge these curses to be blessings.

> But if you would like this fine and charming— HE: What is it?
> AI SA: What is it? You were just saying "peace." LIX: Come on, what is it?
> ARIS: Finish saying what the glutton had begun.

<margin>**Honest / Useful**</margin>
**A worthy man** <note>original: "Frugi homo"</note>. From the Greek word *chrēsimos* <note>original: χρησιμος, meaning useful or beneficial</note>, it is an indeclinable noun. Among the Romans, Lucius Piso was the first to hold the surname "Frugi," according to the authors Cicero (in his *Tusculan Disputations*) and Asconius Pedianus. This is why Lucius Piso is said to have rejected the name when the customs of his family changed. Donatus <note>A famous 4th-century grammarian</note> interprets it as "useful," hence a "frugi slave" is one who is useful and nearly indispensable. Priscianus, in the fourth book of his work on derivatives, derives *frugi* from "fruit" <note>original: "fruge"</note> just as he derives "worthless" <note>original: "nihili"</note> from "nothing." Ennius, in the ninth book of his *Annals*, left behind the phrase "a frux man" <note>original: "frux homo"</note> instead of "frugi man," which we treat as an adjective. Indeed, the most ancient writers (according to the same author in book five) used both *frux* and *fruges* for the nominative case. From there, *frugi* is said figuratively and can be joined with all other cases and genders, just like "to the heart" <note>original: "cordi," meaning dear</note> or "of nothing" <note>original: "nihili," meaning worthless</note> and the rest. Examples include "an honest man," "of an honest man," "a worthless man," "of a worthless man," "a man of heart," "of a man of heart," etc., according to the same Priscianus in books six and eighteen.

<margin>**To speak against**</margin>
**To be reproached** <note>original: "Opprobrari"</note>. To speak ill, to hurl insults, and to "speak against" <note>original: "obloqui"</note>, or to speak in opposition to one who is speaking. Servius, in his sixth commentary, notes that we are accustomed to take this as "to disparage." For he who speaks against someone disparages them. Other related terms include "encountered," "subjected," "overwhelmed," and "withdrawn." **Against** <note>original: "Contra"</note>. From the other side, on the contrary, or "econtra"—which some say is not proper Latin, with Cicero speaking against it in his work *Brutus*.

<margin>**Against / On the contrary**</margin>
**On the contrary** <note>original: "Econtra"</note>. Used by "the thin ones teaching sharper things" <note>likely referring to the Stoics or subtle grammarians</note>, and by Apuleius in the second book of *Metamorphoses*, Servius in his twelfth commentary, Ulpian in his writings on transactions, and the legal experts Marcellus and Africanus.

<margin>**Of the lowest / Of the highest**</margin>
Any little sycophant **of the lowest rank** <note>original: "infimatis"</note>, born in the lowest place, just as **of the highest rank** <note>original: "summatis"</note> means very powerful and of the highest condition and power. Apuleius writes: "restored by the kind instructions of the **highest** goddess."

<margin>**Equally**</margin>
**He values equally** <note>original: "Æstimat iuxta"</note>. Meaning nearby or similarly. Sallust writes: "I value the life and death of these men **equally**." Priscianus teaches in book fourteen that this is an adverb. "I judge these curses to be blessings." That is the judgment of evil men. The wise Cleobulus warns us otherwise, saying: "Abstain from evil-speaking" <note>original Greek: κακολογίας ἀπέχου</note>. And Pittacus did not permit one to speak ill even of an enemy. Plautus joins the words "curses" and "spoken" in his plays *Asinaria* and *Curculio*. Zeno the philosopher does not approve of those "whose ears flow into their tongue." And Plautus says: "They are evil men who speak ill of the good." In the *Curculio*, Plautus defined it thus: if an evil word is spoken to the undeserving, it is a curse. But if it is spoken to the deserving, it is a blessing, at least in my opinion.

<margin>**Fine**</margin>
**But if you would like this fine and charming.** This is an "elliptical" figure <note>A phrase where words are omitted but understood</note>, familiar to comic writers. **Fine** <note>original: "bellum"</note> means clever, decent, or beautiful, from which we get "pretty" <note>original: "bellulum"</note> and the adverb "finely." And **charming** <note>original: "lepidum"</note> means polished...

<meta>This page continues the philological and grammatical analysis of classical Latin comedy, focusing on specific vocabulary used by the characters in Act One. It bridges the discussion from social status and "frugality" to the nuances of charm, rhetorical silence, and the vocabulary of honeyed speech.</meta>

### ACT ONE

[PAGE XXXVIII]

<margin>Charming [Lepidum], Wit [Lepos], Omitting [aposiopesis], I say [Aio], Speak [Dice], Come then [Agedum]</margin>The word **charming** <note>original: "Lepidum"</note> comes from the Greek word *lepis* <note>original: λεπίσ, meaning a scale or flake</note>. Donatus, in his commentary on the play *Andria*, says that those who possess the greatest amount of wit are called **charming** <note>original: "lepidos"</note>. For **wit** <note>original: "lepos"</note> is a type of elegance; this is both fine and charming. The author uses a technique of partial speech <note>leaving a sentence unfinished</note> to force the eager listeners to ask questions; the word "to see" is missing through **aposiopesis** <note>original: "reticentia," a rhetorical device where a sentence is broken off</note>. Indeed, "ellipsis" <note>omission of words</note> and "aposiopesis" often occur together, as something is missing in both. **I say** <note>original: "Aio"</note> is a defective verb in the preterite perfect tense and belongs to the fourth conjugation, as proven by the imperative ending in "i." The poet Naevius says "say [ai] or deny," and Accius used "aibant" instead of "aiebant." This change is common in this conjugation, according to Priscian in his tenth book. **Speak** <note>original: "Dice"</note> is used instead of "dic" through an **archaism** <note>original: ἀρχαϊσμὸν, an old-fashioned style</note>; it is found this way among the ancient and learned writers, as is "face" and "duce," according to Diomedes. **Come then** <note>original: "Agedum"</note> is the word of one encouraging another, helping the speaker from the heart. Terence says: "Come then, explain this to me first." The word "Age" requires the singular number, and "agite" the plural. "Agedum" can be used for both. **Allow to speak**; to utter what the **glutton** <note>original: "deglutator, lurcho, heluator"</note> had begun, from the verb "to swallow" <note>original: "glutare," which we also call "glutire"</note>, according to Festus.

->**BVT.** But if you wish for this beautiful and charming thing<-
->A playful thing, which I recently tucked into my pocket<-
->To see with your eyes and touch with your little fingers<-
->Not whips, but honey-sweet words will be needed<-
->Petitions, frequent supplication<-
->Kissing, and likewise a hymn and prayers<-
->**SA.** Haha! This glutton is like a ghostly spirit<-
->By Hercules, he will use a hovel as his temple<-
->He wants people to pray to him as if he were some god.<-

<margin>There is need [Opus est]</margin>**To touch with little fingers**: the poet formed a most becoming diminutive word <note>original: "digitulis," from "digitus" (finger)</note>. **But words will be needed** <note>original: "At uerba opus erunt"</note>. Diomedes thinks that among the ancients, the phrase "there is need" <note>original: "opus est"</note> is found with almost every grammatical case. For example: "This thing is a need for me" [nominative], "of this thing" [genitive], "for this thing" [dative], "this thing" [accusative], and "by this thing" [ablative]. But we must be careful not to speak exactly as the Greeks do. For they say *chreian echo* <note>original: χρείαν ἔχω</note>—"I have use" or "I have need"—and *chreian eichon*—"I had need." We, however, say "there is need for me" [opus est mihi]. Donatus says in *Andria* that "there is need for that thing" [accusative] is correct, rather than "by that thing" [ablative]. I would assert that Donatus said this to correct the text of *Andria*, for in the play *Phormio*, he constructs it with the ablative case, where he says: "what need is there for words?" **Honey-sweet words** <note>original: "Verba mellaria"</note> mean honeyed, seasoned with honey, or sweet. From "honey" <note>original: "melle"</note> are derived "honey-sweet," "little-honey," and "honey-like," as evidenced by Plautus and Apuleius. However, we properly call **honey-stores** <note>original: "mellaria"</note> the places where the beehives are located. Marcus

<margin>Honey-stores [Mellaria]</margin>Varro in his work *On Agriculture*, book three, and Aulus Gellius in book two, note that people were accustomed to calling these places "honey-bins" <note>original: "mellitonas"</note> and "honey-nurseries" <note>original: "mellitrophia"</note> as well as "apiaries." **Petitions** <note>original: "Rogationes"</note>: requests. For **to ask** <note>original: "rogare"</note>, besides its other meanings, also means to demand. Plautus in the play *Asinaria* says: "One must watch that he neither gives nor asks anything of anyone." Furthermore, the **petitions** <note>original: "rogationes"</note> which were made by

<margin>I ask [Rogo]</margin>Roman magistrates to the people—what sort they were—is well known to us.

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on theatrical language and character dialogue, moving from definitions of wit and speech to terms of religious ritual (supplication and hymns) and the grotesque (ghosts and hovels). ### ACT ONE [PAGE] XXXIX

<margin>Supplication</margin>**Supplication** <note>original: "Supplicatio"</note> is also frequent. They even call punishments "supplications," as Sallust writes in his *War Against Catiline*: "Not by vows nor by womanly **supplications** <note>meaning entreaties or prayers</note> is the help of the gods obtained."
<margin>Frequent</margin>**Frequent** <note>original: "Frequens"</note> is said of that which is not only present but even clings to a thing. Donatus says this in his commentary on the play *Andria*. Hence historians call a soldier "frequent" when he is present at his standards. Likewise, 
<margin>Hymn</margin>similarly, a **hymn** <note>original: "υμνος"</note> is praise, derived from the word "to hymn" <note>original: "υμνεω"</note>, which properly signifies the act of praising the gods.

<margin>Ghost / Mask</margin>Ho ho! How strange that Salax said this about supplications and hymns, as if he wished to be worshipped as a god himself. Therefore, the speaker calls him a **ghostly god** <note>original: "larualem deum"</note>, as if to say: "If you are to be a god, you shall be regarded by us as a god of the dead." We learn from Martianus Capella that **ghosts** <note>original: "laruas"</note> are the same as "maniae" <note>bogeymen used to frighten children</note>. Augustine and Apuleius, in his work *On the God of Socrates*, write that the souls of men who have been depraved and stained by the evil merits of their lives, when separated from the body, become a source of terror to mortals; as nocturnal encounters, they are known by the name of **ghosts**. From this comes **ghostly** <note>original: "laruale"</note>, meaning deformed, pale, sallow, eviscerated, emaciated, and altogether horrendous, just like a ghost. From this also comes **ghost-struck** <note>original: "laruati"</note>, meaning those who are furious or deranged in mind, as if stirred up by ghosts. In the works of Horace, "Larva" is taken to mean a **mask** <note>theatrical mask</note>, and I do not know if you will find it used that way elsewhere.

<margin>Brothel / Cook-shop</margin>**Ganeo** <note>a glutton or a profligate</note>. The ancients called a **ganeum** a tavern for harlots, derived "from the earth" <note>original: "απὸ τῆς γῆς"</note>, because the place itself is in the earth <note>likely referring to low-level or cellar-like establishments</note>. From this is derived the term **ganeo**, for one who frequents such places and harlots. A witticism about a glutton (whom we also call a *ganeo*) exists by the mime-writer Marulus: "You imitate Hector; you never leave Ilion." <note>This is a Latin pun: Hector was the hero of Ilium (Troy), but the word *ilia* also means the guts or the groin; thus, the glutton never leaves his belly.</note>

<margin>Hovel / Fish-trap</margin>**Gurgustio** <note>a hovel</note>, a small hut, a cottage, or a little house fashioned from wicker-work suitable for catching fish. Plautus, in the play *The Braggart Soldier*, calls it a fish-trap. From this, the most Latin Apuleius—whom some think is less "Latin" because he is an inventor of new words, though they have not read Cicero's third book *On the Orator*, which says that inventing words, if done decently, pertains to illuminating and illustrating speech—formed the diminutive **gurgustiolum** <note>a "tiny hovel"</note>. Job mentions this in the Sacred Scriptures, Chapter 40. 
<margin>To Supplicate</margin>**To supplicate** <note>original: "Supplicari"</note>. We properly "supplicate" the gods, which is usually done in the face of adversity or with tears. Plautus writes in *The Rope*: "If anyone is pious, supplicating the gods."

<margin>To Excel</margin>
<margin>Jesters</margin>
> BUTLER: What then, Salax? When I am the god of actors,
> Shall I not **excel** more with the actor's art
> Above the others whom I now know among the **jesters**?
> But you all, **supplicate briefly**; there is need for few words,
> For that which I bring is worthy of a pious supplication.

<margin>Supplicate briefly</margin>**I excel more** <note>original: "Plus præpolleo"</note>. I prevail, I am more able; from this verb the ancients derived the word "pollentia" <note>power or potency</note>. Plautus in *The Rope*: "Lest the power of fools be stronger." **Whom I now know** <note>original: "Quos nūc scia"</note>. He acts modestly here, prescribing nothing for the future and taking nothing away. But indeed, that modesty is ridiculous in a stage-player, just as are the "maxims" <note>moralizing proverbs</note> found in comic slaves, jesters, and actors dedicated to jokes and play. **But supplicate you briefly**: with short and few words. The particle **briefly** <note>original: "breui"</note> is used in this sense by Cicero. **For that which [I bring]**: He wants his honor to be shown with a pious and religious supplication, as if with sacred rites, though in a joking manner. Thus, he does not cease to make the audience attentive.

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on theatrical and religious terminology. Having previously discussed "ghosts" (larvae), the author now transitions into the ritual act of sacrifice (litatio) and launches a scholarly critique of legal commentators who misunderstand classical Latin vocabulary.</meta>

<page-num>XL</page-num>
<header>ACT ONE</header>

->**ARIST.** Shall we offer a favorable sacrifice <note>original: "litabimus"</note> to you or to these playthings?<-
->**BUT.** To both, for what I carry is said to be sacred.<-
->**HEL.** Show it, point it out; the whole company demands it.<-
->**BUT.** Let it be so. Here, take this little vessel, I beg.<-
->**SAL.** You mean this mask? **BUT.** May the gods "mask" you <note>A curse meaning "may the gods drive you mad with ghosts."</note>!<-
->What do you mean, a mask? You will be more ghostly than the spirits of the dead <note>original: "manendeis," likely referring to the Manes, the shades of the deceased.</note><-
->If you continue to speak in this way; quiet down, hey!<-

---

<margin>To sacrifice favorably</margin>
**To you.** <note>original: "Tibi"</note> This is as if to say: "since you wish to be called and held as a god of actors." **Shall we offer a favorable sacrifice?** <note>original: "Litabimus?"</note> Shall we propitiate you or the plaything? To offer a favorable sacrifice <note>term: "Litare" — in Roman religion, this specifically meant to perform a sacrifice that successfully obtained a favorable omen or "propitiated" the deity.</note> means to win over a god and, once the sacrifice is performed, to appease the divinity. Macrobius is the authority for this. Hence that line of Plautus from *The Little Carthaginian*: "Then Jupiter will make it so that I am always sacrificing, but never obtaining a favorable omen."

<margin>Sacred</margin>
**For what I carry.** <note>original: "Nam quod gesto"</note> He adds the reason: it is called "sacred." The Emperor <note>referring to Justinian I, the Byzantine Emperor who codified Roman law.</note> teaches what "sacred" is in the second volume of the *Institutions*, in the section on the division of things. Those who cite this book as the "Institutes" <note>original: "Instituta"</note> do not seem to have read the Imperial preface. And in the letter of the *Old Digest*, which meets the student at the threshold of the work, the Emperor says this: "Having called together these illustrious men, we have specifically commanded that they, by our authority and our urgings, should compose the **Institutions** <note>original: "institutiones"</note>." He does not say "Institutes," but "Institutions." Therefore, those speaking of civil law who call them "Institutes" are not speaking in a "civil" <note>or "refined"</note> manner. These are the people (to use the words of Columella) without whom cities have been and shall be happy enough. 

We read that Antistius Labeo was most learned in civil law and especially skilled in the science of **grammar** <note>original: "grammatica"</note> for the purpose of untying many of the snares of the law—a science which our "pettifoggers" <note>term: "legulei" — a derogatory term for lawyers who focus on the letter of the law rather than its spirit or history.</note> reject and cast aside. It is for this reason that we see such monstrous interpretations in their most uneloquent little glosses <note>marginal notes</note>, produced by men who have earned a very poor reputation regarding such elegant Latin. <margin>The words of Philippus Beroaldus</margin> These interpretations are like: "he smelled it" <note>original: "olfecit"</note>, that is, "he placed his nose to the buttocks"; or "hay" <note>original: "foenum"</note> is "dry grass." Likewise, regarding the third book on legacies: where the text reads "Hypothaustra" <note>a Greek-derived term for a hearth-damper or vent</note>, the commentator explains it as "the place where the sick lie down." 

Likewise, where Caesar says a certain study was "innate" <note>original: "innatum"</note> to someone, the commentator says it means he was studious "before birth and after birth." Likewise, in the third book on legacies, regarding "two light dishes" <note>original: "lances duas leues"</note> which "I bought from the *sigillaria*" <note>term: "sigillaria" — a Roman festival and market where small clay figurines and vessels were sold.</note>, good gods, what a frivolous, futile, and ridiculous interpretation they give! There, the *sigillaria* is explained as a "type of vessel"; then, as if he had thought of something more brilliant, he says it is a "type of seller." Is this interpreting the laws or defiling them? Such interpretations undoubtedly infect students of the law rather than instructing them. 

Yet this "excellent" narrator has not a few followers, so devoted that they think it a sin or a crime to depart even a fingernail's breadth from his interpretation, as if he were the one and only illustrator of civil law under the whole heaven, who could extract not just the skin but the very blood and marrow of the laws. Hence he may rightly claim the name of **Accursius** <note>A famous 13th-century legal commentator; his name implies "one who runs to help," but the author puns on it here.</note>, but he seems to "run slowly" <note>original: "tardigradus recurrere"</note> and is often seen hallucinating in a daze, especially under the title of "the deposit" where he di...

<meta>catchword: di- (likely continuing to "disputes" or "discusses")</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes a scene from the play involving a "sacred" mask or vessel, using it as a springboard to criticize medieval legal commentators (like Accursius) for their poor understanding of Latin grammar and classical context. The author argues that true legal expertise requires the "science of grammar" to correctly interpret ancient texts.</summary>

<page-num>XLI</page-num>
<header>ACT ONE</header>

...about to explain the [a]ction <note>The text likely reads "actionem," though the first letters are missing.</note> of Costrophinas, he says, "or I do not know." He confesses quite frankly—and indeed never better—he for whom Uguccio <note>Uguccio of Pisa (died 1210), a famous medieval lexicographer whose work "Derivationes" was a standard reference for Latin vocabulary, though often criticized by later Renaissance humanists.</note> is the authority for grammar and history. From this, they produce a "dog-like" pursuit of study <note>original: "caninum studiū"; a metaphor for aggressive, snarling criticism.</note> (as the ancients used to say), which was neglected by our ancestors and even allowed by us, etc. They often say that to know the laws is not to know their words, but to retain their force and power. However, they sometimes blink and stumble in both aspects. Although Marcus Cicero discusses this matter in his speech *In Defense of Lucius Murena* before the judges, let us let these things pass for now.

<margin>I show</margin>
**Show, point out.** <note>original: "Ostende, monstra."</note> From these come the words for "omen" <note>term: "ostentum" — a supernatural sign or display.</note> and "monster." <note>term: "monstrum" — originally meaning a divine portent or something shown by the gods.</note> But things specifically sought out are "shown."

<margin>I point out</margin>
**Company.** <note>original: "Societas."</note> With one word he wins over their goodwill. For we do not ask as actors, but as partners <note>original: "socii"</note> to whom you owe more than to those boastful stage-performers.

<margin>I seek out</margin>
**And from the thing itself,** he says "we do not just ask, we seek out." <note>original: "expetimus"</note> We "seek out" only those things that are honorable and clearly good. Hence, philosophers have written "concerning things to be sought out," <note>original: "de rebus expetendis"</note> which are things that clearly ought to be asked for.

<margin>But</margin>
**But.** <note>original: "At"</note> An introductory particle among the ancients. As in Terence’s *The Brothers* <note>original: "in adel." referring to the play *Adelphoe*.</note>: "Dona." Sometimes it even corresponds to a state of agitation.

<margin>Behold</margin>
**Behold.** <note>original: "Ecce."</note> This adverb is not used except for the sake of signifying a horrifying and sudden thing. It was used familiarly by Marcus Cicero and the poet Virgil. Virgil says: "But behold, twin serpents from Tenedos through the tranquil deep." It pronounces an unexpected evil, both heavy enough and violent, and always a thing unforeseen and not previously noticed. Virgil in book 11: "Behold, the great Diomedes from the sorrowful city." In book 7: "But behold, she was returning from Inachian Argos."

<margin>Likewise</margin>
**You mean to say.** In this place: "May the gods mask you" <note>original: "Dii te laruent"</note>; may they make you "crazed" <note>term: "cerritum" — anciently believed to be a madness sent by the goddess Ceres.</note>; may they strike you down "delirious" <note>original: "lymphatum"</note> and insane with fury. Plautus says: "Surely you are masked <note>term: "laruatus" — meaning both 'wearing a mask' and 'possessed by ghosts' (larvae).</note> or crazed." A certain German poet recently corrupted the first part of this name, though Horace says: "He was crazed or [guilty] of a disturbed mind."

<margin>Masked / Haunted</margin>
**More masked** means more terrible.

<margin>Crazed</margin>
**To the spirits of the dead.** <note>original: "Manendeis" — an archaic or corrupted form of "Diis Manibus."</note> These are the gods of the underworld, the terrors of the ghosts. Country folk call them "little eyes," <note>original: "ozellos"</note> while the Greeks call them "phantasms," as found in the works of Pliny the Younger in his letters, and in Plutarch, Festus, Marcellus, and Apuleius in *On the God of Socrates*.

<margin>Spirits of the dead</margin>
**Mutter, hey.** <note>original: "Mutito heus."</note> The ancients used "to mutter" <note>original: "mutire"</note> instead of "to speak." Ennius says: "To mutter openly is a sin for a commoner." Persius in Satire 1: "Is it a sin for me to mutter?"

---

> **HE.** Wow! Is this pottery corpse worth so much?
> This mud-like, clay-like, and shriveled head?
> I fear the shape, and I shudder at the bones of the dead man.
> **BVT.** How delicate this handsome boy is!
> Do you fear a face by Apelles <note>Apelles was the most famous painter of ancient Greece; the speaker is mocking the other for being afraid of a mere representation.</note> or a dead one?
> You should dread a face if you were wise that is triple-tongued, <note>term: "trilinguidum" — literally 'three-tongued,' implying someone deceitful or a monstrous entity.</note>
> And wet, and thieving. **LIX.** This man makes gold out of dung
> Whenever he wishes; we must be grateful.
> Our status is good; we shall be saved by this trick. <note>term: "præstygio" — a stage trick, illusion, or sleight of hand.</note>

**Wow!** <note>original: "Papæ"</note> Heluoni’s wonder rises because Buttubatta makes such a great deal of a skull. <note>original: "caluaria"</note> **Wow!**

# LXII
# ACT ONE

<meta>catchword: V</meta>

<margin>Corpse</margin>
For we use that interjection whenever we suddenly receive marvelous news; see Terence in *The Eunuch*. 
A brick-like corpse. <note>original: "Cadauer testaceum"</note> Servius <note>A 4th-century grammarian famous for his commentary on Virgil.</note> calls a funeral a "corpse" [cadauer]; the Greeks call it *nekron* <note>original: νεκρὸν</note>. 
<margin>Necromancy</margin>
From this comes that famous species of magic, necromancy or *necyomantia*, from the word *nekyos* <note>original: ἀπὸ τοῦ νέκυος</note>, meaning "from a dead person." Necromancers are those by whose incantations the dead seem to be brought back to life, to divine the future, and to respond to questions. Such things can be seen in Homer’s account of Ulysses, the summoning performed by Scipio in Silius, by Aeson in Valerius Flaccus, by Tiresias in Papinius <note>Referring to the poet Statius.</note>, and by the witches in Horace. 
<margin>Brick-like</margin>
"Brick-like" [testaceum] comes from *testa*—meaning made of clay. Muddy, foul-smelling, worn out by mud, and filthy. Also clayey, chalky, and smeared with thick mud, somewhat bristly, foul, and to be avoided. "I dread the form." I am horrified and terrified by the very sight. I even dread the bones of the dead; I avoid, flee, and turn away from them. "How delicate!" The words of the glutton [Heluo] strike back at Buttubatta—that is, the foolish prince. Whenever such a prince is possessed by a vain courtier endowed with no wisdom, he considers the one he has to be delicate and beautiful, while everyone else dreads him. 
Now is the time to inaugurate the morality from the premise, by skillfully weighing what follows. 
<margin>Skinless face</margin>
"You fear the skinless face." <note>original: "Os times apelle"</note> A face without hide, without leather, without skin; "recuticum" [circumcised/flayed]. If you are wise, you are "three-tongued" [trilinguidum]: sly, malicious, a double-dealer and a deceiver; this refers to the mind. For there is no mouth that actually abounds with three tongues. 
<margin>Three-tongued</margin>
Therefore, the sentiment is this: "three-tongued" can also be taken in a good sense. Apuleius in the eleventh book of the *Metamorphoses* calls the Sicilians "three-tongued," meaning they speak three languages. Saint Jerome calls a man "five-tongued" <note>original: pentaglottam</note>—that is, five-tongued—who speaks all five dialects of the Greeks. And Quintus Curtius writes in his sixth book about the Branchidae: "But they were already bilingual [bilingues], gradually degenerating from their native speech to a foreign one." 
<margin>Hairy</margin>
And "pilax" means hairy, covered in hair, frightening, long-haired and shaggy. "This man is made of dung." Thus Marcus Cicero says in his *Speech for Plancus*: "What I have said was so that I might extol Plancus’s merit toward me; I would make a fortress out of a sewer and worship a stone from a tomb as a god." This is a very fitting allegory if you apply it to the premise. For out of such men, princes make gold and popular gods. You shall calculate the rest for yourself. 
<margin>Illusion</margin>
"Through this illusion we shall be safe." We will trade and scrape together what is needed to sustain and satisfy the gluttony of a parasite. "By illusion" [Præstigio]: a trick for mocking men. Saint Augustine writes that Mercury was the inventor of illusions, and this is repeated in the canonical decrees regarding pleasure. They are called "illusions" [praestigia] because they dazzle <note>original: præstringant</note> the eyes of the mind.

->**For while Buttubatta is starving for swine**<-
->**By offering this dry bone like a beggar**<-
->**You will stir up the hard-working farmers**<-
->**So that each one, led by religion, might offer a small coin**<-
->**Every old woman, and whoever is a funeral-feaster.**<- <note>term: "filicernii" — literally those who attend funeral feasts; here used as a derogatory term for the elderly or those "with one foot in the grave."</note>

# PAGE XLIII

->### ACT ONE<-

Old men, lethargic ones, will provide food;
They will kiss this "sacred" face (as you call it).
You will also feign a touch of divinity,
Pretending that through the contemplation of sacrifice
You remit sins, pardoning their guilt in this way,
And defending them from Pluto’s underworld.
O, thief of the human mind!
**BUTTUBATTA:** Why are you shouting?
Why do you bark? Or why do you rave? You hoarse babbler!
Is it not rightly permitted to me to do what is common,
Trite, trivial, and long-practiced by the officials?

<margin>Hunger</margin>
While there is a famished plague. When the "hungry holidays" <note>original: "Esurialibus feriis." A reference to times of fasting or scarcity.</note> press in, it is Hunger that Virgil describes most elegantly in Latin with the epithet "ill-persuading." Clearly, the plague of hunger is most wretched, as the Greeks cry out: "it is most pitiful to die of hunger" <note>original: "λιμῷ οἰκτιστον θανέειν"</note>. This is represented by a parched bone and a dry, light skull. Doctors treat it through medications and healing arts.

<margin>Farmers</margin>
Hard-working farmers, simple and rustic folk, whom new superstition easily moves. Virgil writes in Book VIII: "Even then, the religious awe of the place terrified the trembling rustics." He enumerates three types of men prone to superstition. If a skull is found among princes, it terrifies them and vexes them; as Horace says: "Whatever madness the kings commit, the Greeks [the common people] pay the penalty." They are led by religion, fear, and superstition. Statius says: "Fear first created gods in the world."

<margin>A two-penny harlot; An old woman</margin>
Let him offer an obol, a tiny little coin. From this comes the "two-penny harlot" <note>original: "diobolare scortum"</note>, who is hired for two obols in the plays of Plautus. An "old woman" [Anus] is an aged female, corpse-like and weighed down by years. The etymology of the word explains why old women specifically are said to "rave" [delirant]; it comes from the prefix *a-* (meaning "without") and *nous* (meaning "mind"), because they are without mind or sense. There is a Greek proverb about those who rave and talk nonsense: "old wives' tales" <note>original: "γράων ὕθλος"</note>. In Virgil’s *Aeneid* VII, an old woman is festively mocked by Turnus.

<margin>The stooped</margin>
Those bent with age <note>original: "Silicernii"</note>. We call these "silicernii"—old men who are stooped by long life, looking down at the ground as they walk, or perhaps because they are already on the verge of seeing the silent shadows of the grave. Placiades approves of the first etymology from Donatus, while Servius mentions another meaning for "silicernium" in Book V of the *Aeneid*. 

"Lethargic" [Veternosi] means languid, sickly, or suffering from a deep, heavy sleep. 
<margin>Lethargy</margin>
Probus says that doctors call "lethargy" <note>original: "λήθαργον"</note> the opposite of the "frenetic" disease; it involves a numbness and an almost unconquerable need to sleep, which kills quickly unless help is given. Pliny, in his nineteenth volume, instructs those suffering from lethargy to eat the sharpest possible radishes to aid and cure the disease of drowsiness.

<margin>Rations</margin>
"Rations" [Cibarium]. This refers to the coarse food of gluttons. Used as a noun, this word signifies a meager or poor kind of food. Sextus Julius Frontinus mentions this in Book IV.

E ii <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to keep the pages of a book in the correct order during binding.</note>

<summary>This page analyzes a passage of dialogue where a character is accused of using religious deception to exploit "lethargic" old men and simple farmers. The commentary provides philological origins for terms related to hunger, old age, and the physical symptoms of lethargy.</summary>

<keywords>superstition, hunger, farmers, old age, lethargy, Virgil, etymology, religious exploitation, Pliny</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's text, specifically analyzing the vocabulary used in Act One. # PAGE XLIIII

->### ACT ONE<-

Ulpian in book 34 of the *Digests* <note>A massive collection of Roman legal opinions</note>, Pliny in book 11, and Horace in book 2 of the *Odes*, use the term "fill the provisions" <note>original: "cibaria exple"</note>. However, Porphyrio <note>a 3rd-century commentator on Horace</note> reads this as "Ciboria" <note>original: "Ciboria," referring to Egyptian drinking cups</note>. Columella in book 9 says: "Four cups are the daily provisions" <note>original: "cibaria"</note>. Diomedes <note>a Latin grammarian</note> writes that this word should be pronounced in the plural number, though Marcellus used it singularly <note>original: "ἑνικῶς"</note>; yet you will find it used almost always as an adjective among the more refined Latin writers. Hence, we find "coarse bread" <note>original: "cibarius panis"</note> in Cornelius Celsus, and in Apuleius, and in Marcus Cicero in the fifth book of the *Tusculan Disputations*. Marcus Varro also spoke of a "food law," "cheap food," "common grapes," and "common wine" <note>original: "Cibaria lex, uilitas cibaria, uua cibaria, uinum cibarium"</note>. The same author says Aristoxenus was a "common brother," where the word "common" <note>original: "cibarius"</note> is used improperly but is understood to mean sordid, plebeian, and lower-class. "They will kiss it eagerly" (as you say), as if to say it is not "sacred" in everyone’s opinion, but only "as you say."

<margin>Sacrifice</margin>
Regarding the "contemplation of the sacrifice" <note>original: "piaculum"</note>: that they may contemplate this sacrifice. He means a sacrifice more worthy of expiation than of veneration. In the Cretan dialect, to "piously offer" <note>original: "piare"</note> is to expiate; the opposite of this is to "impiously violate" <note>original: "impiare."</note> From "piously offering" comes "sacrifice" <note>original: "piaculum"</note>, a deed committed for which expiation is required. Servius in book 6 of the *Aeneid* says: "From the Plutonian Tartarus."

<margin>Tartarus</margin>
From the prison of infernal punishments. Plato thinks that Tartarus is a reservoir into which all rivers fall and from which they flow back out. Hence "Tartarean." So says the poet: "A virago born from the body of the Tartarean swamp."

<margin>Indulge</margin>
"To indulge in guilts in this way." This usage has grown old, and a false habit has taken hold to the extent that we accept "to indulge" <note>original: "indulgere"</note> to mean "to remit" or "to forgive." This usage, I say, has surpassed authority, rule, and logic. For this reason, Gellius rightly called Custom the mistress of things, and especially of words. Terence uses the word "indulge" properly in his third comedy, where Chremes says to Menedemus: "Menedemus, you indulge him too much" <note>original: "egre nimiū illi Menedeme indulges"</note>. On this, Calphurnius notes that no more appropriate word could have been found for a father who permits everything to his son. And in the *Eunuch*: "This softness of mind must be cast out, by Hercules, I indulge myself too much," and elsewhere, "they indulged you." This is how the ancients spoke, whereas we would say "to me." Valerius Maximus has a chapter on indulgence, where you will learn through evidence more than through some "parerga"—that is, an internal digression. Our poet has used the word "indulge" in the popular manner.

<margin>
Defendant
Guilt
</margin>
"In guilts" <note>original: "reatibus"</note>, meaning faults and crimes. According to Quintilian, Messala was the first to use the word "guilt" <note>original: "reatus"</note>. However, the "defendant" <note>original: "reus," from which the word "reatus" is derived</note> is said to be one who pleads a case, who is accused, or likewise one who has promised or vowed something and owes it. Aelius Gallus is the authority for this in book 2 of *On the Significance of Words*, as is Marcus Tullius [Cicero] in book 2 of *On the Orator*.

<margin>Thief</margin>
"O seducer of the human mind!" The authority of the ancient Romans agrees that "thief" <note>original: "fur"</note> is named from "dark" <note>original: "furvus," that is, from "black"</note>, because the night, obscure and black, is opportune for their evil deeds. Mercury <note>original: "Μῐχῐοφ," likely a Greek-inflected name for Hermes</note> presides over thefts, so called in Greek because he carries himself as an inter-messenger. From Fulgentius, we get "thievish" <note>original: "furatrinus"</note>. "Why do you rave?" You bark with rage; you babble. Varro asks: what is it, why do you tear at me, why do you rave? From this comes "shysters" <note>original: "rabulae"</note>, those who are litigious; and a lawyer or a patron of the forum is called by this name. Cicero says in the *Orator* that Sallust practiced "canine eloquence."

<margin>Trivial</margin>
"Common triviality." Vulgar and used everywhere at the crossroads <note>original: "triuiis"</note>. From "crossroad" <note>original: "triuio"</note> comes "trivial" <note>original: "triuiālis"</note>. Quintilian speaks of "trivial masters" who teach at the crossroads. Calphurnius in his *Bucolics* says: "Not in this trivial manner of a traveler, but a god himself sings." Any common song is "trivial," as in Juvenal.

<summary>This page provides a philological analysis of the dialogue in Act One, exploring the etymologies and classical usage of terms related to food rations, religious expiation, guilt, and the social status of "trivial" or "common" people.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, Roman law, philology, sacrifice, underworld, Mercury, theft, social class, crossroads</keywords>

# PAGE XLV

->### ACT ONE<-

"Let a common coin strike a trivial song" <note>original: "Communi feriat carmen triuiale moneta"</note>. From this is also derived the adverb *triuialiter* (trivially), which means commonly, everywhere, and in all places. **Possession claimed by the Aediles** <note>original: "Usucaptum ædilibus"</note>. The Aediles were plebeian magistrates <note>officials elected from the common people</note> who cared for sacred and private buildings. Dionysius is an authority on this, as are Marcus Cicero in the seventh book of the *Verrine Orations*, Pomponius the jurist, Plutarch in his *Life of Caesar*, and Fenestella. **Claimed through use** <note>original: "Usucaptum"</note> refers to a right claimed by long-term possession; regarding this word, see the commentaries on Cicero’s *Topics* and the writings of Modestinus the jurist. There is also a title in the laws regarding "usucaptions" <note>the legal process of acquiring property by possessing it for a specific period</note>.

> SA. Whatever you are saying, you wear a foul face,
> Dreg-filled and overgrown with moss and mud.
> ARI. Good heavens, what is this? HE. An earthen pot where you might cook worms,
> Or a human skull serving as a hovel for vermin.
> LIX. Indeed, his head is a tortoise-shell and a tomb.
> HE. It reeks heavily. SA. It stinks badly and smells even more foully.
> ARI. At least wash it and place it under a gutter,
> In this hollow where it rains, or take a bronze kettle
> Full of hot water; with that, prepare the body for burial,
> As an undertaker is accustomed to cleanse corpses.

<margin>Dreg-filled
Overgrown</margin>
**Whatever you are saying** <note>original: "quicquid hoc ais"</note>. Whatever speech or exclamation you may use, however much you adorn it with any words you like, you nevertheless wear a **foul face** <note>original: "os impudicatum"</note>. This means a face that is impure, defiled, muddied, foul, obscene, contaminated, and filthy—**dreg-filled** <note>original: "feculentum"</note>, smeared and as if disgraced by dregs. **Overgrown** <note>original: "obsitum"</note> means wrapped in filth, sordidness, and uncleanness. We also say people are "covered <note>original: "obsitos"</note> by age and years." Virgil writes: "The king went along, covered <note>original: "obsitus"</note> by age." Donatus says this kind of speech is a metaphor taken from the fields. <margin>Mossy</margin> **With moss** <note>original: "musco"</note>. With downy growth, neglect, or squalor, from which the word **mossy** <note>original: "muscosus"</note> is made. Virgil mentions "mossy springs." **With mud** <note>original: "luto"</note>, with clay-like filth. **Good heavens, what is this?** <note>original: "papæ quid est"</note>. This character also sees the skull, which Buttubatta honored with a very long speech, making it into gold and sacred relics. <margin>Earthen pot
Pot-cooked</margin> **Earthen pot** <note>original: "testus"</note>, a small cooking vessel of the fourth order. From this comes *testuacium*, which is food cooked in a hot pot, just as Varro says the matrons still do during the *Matralia* <note>the festival of the "Sacred Mother" Matuta</note>. <margin>Worms</margin> **Worms** <note>original: "lumbricos"</note>. Columella mentions worms in his seventh volume, writing of remedies to exterminate them. But truly, we properly call *lumbrici* those round and sometimes flat worms that occupy the belly, which are passed out sometimes from the lower parts and sometimes, more foully, through the mouth. Cornelius Celsus teaches that in the sick, it is a good sign if some worms descend near the end of the illness. Macer says of drinking wormwood: "In this way it drives out worms and softens the belly." It is pronounced with the middle syllable long. Pliny in book 11 writes that there is a fish named *lumbricus*. **A hovel for vermin** <note>original: "vermibus gurgustium"</note>, a little house, a pen, or a dwelling. **Indeed** <note>original: "nampe"</note> is an archaism <note>original: "ἀρχαϊσμὸς," an ancient Greek style</note> used instead of *nempe* <note>meaning "certainly" or "of course"</note>.
