<meta>This page continues the detailed philological commentary on the dialogue from Act One. Having just described the "foul face" and "earthen pot" of the previous page, the author now explains the specific terms for the ritual washing and burial preparation of the skull/vessel mentioned in the play's verses.</meta>

# PAGE XLVI

->### ACT ONE<-

<margin>Tomb-dweller</margin> And a "tomb-dweller" <note>original: *bustuarium*</note> is one taken from a tomb or found in a tomb. It reeks heavily; it is stinking and troublesome with its stench. Wash it at least, if you wish that filthy shell to be worshipped.
<margin>At least</margin> "At least" <note>original: *saltem*</note> is a word born from the mouths of captives, who seek nothing else besides safety <note>original: *salutem*</note>; when their affairs are so desperate, they use this word while seeking only safety. Thus Terence says in the *Andria*: "At least do it carefully, so that I may seem to be feared." "Underneath the gutter-tile" <note>original: *subter imbricem*</note>: place it under the tile and the dripping water, wash it, and rinse it, lest we seem to be bound by the religious obligation of filth.
<margin>Underneath</margin> The preposition *subter* [underneath] has the opposite meaning to *super* [above]. Furthermore, both are joined to either the accusative <gloss>or the ab-</gloss>lative case, according to Priscian in book 14. By "gutter-tile" <note>original: *imbricem*</note> he means that the vessel should be placed under the central roof opening <term>impluvium and compluvium; the openings in a Roman house designed to catch rainwater</term> where the dripping is more abundant, which the ancients also called "icicle-dripping" <note>original: *stiricidium*</note> from the root word for "icicle" <note>original: *stiria*</note>.
<margin>Icicle</margin> Virgil says in the third book of the *Georgics*: "And the rough icicle hardened on his unkempt beard." Its diminutive is *stilla* [a drop], which, since it falls frequently,
<margin>Falling repeatedly</margin> is called "to fall repeatedly" <note>original: *casitare*</note>. Paulus the jurist used this frequentative verb in the *Digest* <note>a collection of Roman legal writings</note> regarding urban property rights, when he says: "If a right of dripping water is imposed, the owner of the servient area is not permitted to build where the dripping had begun to fall repeatedly [casitare], if previously the dripping fell from the tile." But he said "underneath the gutter-tile," that is, an upturned channel from which water flows more freely. Therefore the poet called it "the hollow." And the jurist calls it a pipe <note>original: *fistula*</note> in the eighth book of the *Digest*.
<margin>Gutter-tiles</margin> Gutter-tiles <note>original: *imbrices*</note>, <gloss>which</gloss> catch the rains <note>original: *imbres*</note>, are used in both the masculine and feminine gender. Servius the grammarian thinks it is better to call this a feminine *imbrex*. Plautus
<margin>Rain</margin> wrote: "You broke my gutter-tiles while you, a monkey, followed one worthy of you." Virgil, regarding choosing
<margin>Rain-sender</margin> a place for breeding bees in the fourth book of the *Georgics*, makes mention of the *imbrex*. *Imber* [rain] signifies a mass of celestial or rainy waters, although authority on the matter varies today. From this comes "rainy weather" and a "rainy sign," such as we call Aquarius, whom the Greeks call the water-pourer <note>original: *hydrochoos*</note>; and Palladius spoke of a "rainy sky." And the god Apollo is named "Rain-sender" <note>original: *imbricitor*</note> by Macrobius in the first book of the *Saturnalia*—in Greek *Ombraios*. And "tiled rubble" <note>original: *imbricata cementa*</note> is mentioned by Vitruvius in book 2. Furthermore, Pliny mentions an "overlapping" <note>original: *imbricata*</note> roughness of the tongue in seals; also in book 11, he says all animals with fingers have nails, but in monkeys they are overlapping/curved [imbricatos], while in humans they are broad, etc. Likewise, certain insects have "overlapping" [imbricatis] flexible vertebrae. Most significantly, however, the same polymath Pliny in book 20 shows what he wanted to be understood by "overlapping." For he writes that the two kinds of *Conyza* [fleabane], male and female, differ in leaf, and adds: "The female's is thinner, more constricted and narrower; the male's is overlapping and more branched." Therefore, we shall call the branched and twisted pipes for catching rains "gutter-tiles" <note>original: *imbrices*</note>, which the same
<margin>It rains</margin> author mentions at the end of book 8, chapter 6. "Where it rains" <note>original: *ubi pluit*</note>: the verb "it rains" [pluit] is among those that do not refer to a person, which is said only in the third person. It can, however, be found in the first and second person through personification <note>original: *prosopopoeia; the technique of giving a voice to an inanimate object or abstract concept</note>, as in the figure of a certain poet or the response of a god, or through an address <note>original: *apostrophe*</note> as if to Jupiter himself present. Priscian calls these "excepted actions" in book 1 of *On Construction*. "Take up the bronze cauldron" <note>original: *ahenum tollito*</note>: take the bronze vessel in which you may wash; it comes from *aes* [bronze], from which come the adjectives *aereus* and *aeneus*, and the noun formed through the dissolution of the diphthong and the insertion of an aspiration [the 'h'].
<margin>Bronze cauldron</margin> If this vessel is quite large, such as dyers use, it is called a "cauldron" <note>original: *cortina*</note>; also from *ahenum* comes the diminutive *ahenulum* [small bronze pot]. "You shall prepare for burial" <note>original: *pollincias*</note>: you should cleanse, wash, and care for it just as
<margin>To prepare for burial</margin> undertakers <note>original: *funerarii*</note> are accustomed to—that is, the caretakers of funerals and the anointers of the "polluted," meaning the corpses.
<margin>Undertaker</margin> Whence a "prepared corpse" <note>original: *pollinctum funus*</note> is said to be anointed by the undertakers [pollinctores], for to prepare for burial [pollincere] is to...

<summary>This page provides an exhaustive philological analysis of words related to rain, water management (tiles and pipes), and the ritual washing of corpses. The author uses classical citations from Virgil, Pliny, and Roman legal texts to define the exact nature of the "cauldron" and "gutter-tile" mentioned in the play.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological examination of the play's dialogue, specifically focusing on the vocabulary of Roman funeral rites and luxury ointments. The previous page discussed the cleaning of the "sacred vessel" (the skull), and now the author delves into the social classes of undertakers and the specific botanical origins of the perfumes mentioned in the text.</meta>

# ACT ONE XLVII

<margin>
Wasp-men
Nocturnal undertakers
Cheap-bier carriers
Tomb-watchers
The Coffin
</margin>
It is rare to anoint corpses. As I mentioned regarding the "undertaker’s assistant" <note>original: *pollinctor*</note> in Plautus, who had prepared a body for burial. We call the porters and carriers of the dead "wasp-men" <note>original: *vespae*</note> and "nocturnal undertakers" <note>original: *vespiliones*</note> from the word for evening time <note>original: *vespertino tempore*</note>, because they would carry out the bodies of the poor at night. The "cheap-bier carriers" <note>original: *sandapilarii*</note> were those who carried the common biers <note>original: *sandapilarum*</note>; they were also known as "tomb-watchers" <note>original: *bustuarii*</note> and "funeral directors" <note>original: *libitinarii*</note>. According to the author Fulgentius, a *sandapila* is a stretcher or a small box for the dead which carried not the bodies of the noble, but of the common people and the condemned. This is also called a *capulum* <term>capulum: a Roman term for a coffin or a bier, though it shares a root with 'taking hold'</term>. From this comes the name "coffin-dodger" <note>original: *capularis*</note> for an old man in the plays of Plautus. Similarly, Lucilius writes of "matters of the coffin" and "a coffin-ready corpse."

---

> BUTLER: What if I wash and generously prepare it?
> Do you want me to smear it with exotic ointment,
> So it smokes with the scent of cinnamon and balsam oil?
> Nay, rather I would prefer that the smell of a dung-pit
> Were offered first to your curling nostrils,
> You thieves born of rustic parents.

---

<margin>
Exotic
Ointment
Cinnamon
Balsam
Dung-pit
</margin>
**Do you want?** This is as if to say: "Perhaps you will impose another burden on me if I prepare this body," since today he cannot satisfy them regarding the religious duty of the vessel—the honoring and kneeling before this sacred trinket—with **exotic ointment**, meaning foreign, imported, and external. Plautus says, "not everyone can smell of exotic ointment." The legal expert Pomponius writes that **ointment** is obtained for the sake of pleasure and health. The rest of the crowd of classical writers believe it is made for elegance and delight; in Greek it is called *myron* <note>original: μυρον</note>, from which we get *myropola* [ointment-seller] and *myropolion* <note>original: μυροπώλιον</note> [ointment shop]. **With the oil of cinnamon:** with an ointment smelling of the scent of cinnamon, which is called *cinnaminum*. It is the same as *cinnamomum*. Persius says: "Whether the cinnamons breathe a faint scent." The cinnamon shrub grows in Ethiopia; it is most fragrant and its bark is most highly prized. The wood, which is called "wood-cinnamon" <note>original: *xilocinnamum*</note> in Greek, is considered inferior. **Of balsam juice** <note>original: *opobalsami*</note>: from the juice of the balsam tree, for *opos* <note>original: οπόσ</note> signifies "juice." Indeed, **balsam** is preferred above all scents; the shops even stock the wood itself. The highest proof of balsam's quality is if it curdles like milk and does not leave stains on clothing. You will find more in the works of Pliny. It is a thing worthy of wonder that balsam only grows in Judea. **Nay, rather:** Furthermore and even more so. **That it smells of a dung-pit** <note>original: *oletum*</note>: human excrement. More properly, however, it signifies the place where we discharge and empty ourselves. Tacitus proves this in his writing on Nero. Cato takes the term to mean a place planted with olives <note>original: *olea*</note> in the first and sixth chapters of his work *On Agriculture*. **Curling** <note>original: *crispantibus*</note>: hooked and wrinkled, vibrating and bent, twisted. Servius, in his twelfth commentary, writes that hair is "curled" <note>original: *crispari*</note> with a curling iron <note>original: *calamistro*</note>. Persius says: "He redoubles his trembling laughter with a curling nose." Nostrils <note>original: *nares*</note> are named from "rarity" because, as nature teaches us, they are ready to smell and perceive what is nearby. See more in book 11 concerning the nose. **You, born of rustic parents:** He insults them regarding the cleanliness of the vessel, as if to say: "You are more worthy of smelling dung than this sacred object." To be **born of rustic parents** is an insult against the low-born, but it is usually done by the low-born themselves, who celebrate lineages and family trees rather than virtue.

<vocab>pollinctor (undertaker's assistant), vespiliones (nocturnal undertakers), sandapilarii (porters for the poor), bustuarii (tomb-watchers), libitinarii (undertakers), capulum (coffin/bier), exotico (foreign/exotic), cinnamum (cinnamon), opobalsami (juice of the balsam), oletum (dung/latrine), crispantibus (curling/sneering), myropola (perfumer), xylocinnamum (wood-cinnamon)</vocab>

It explains the various social tiers of undertakers (from those who handled the noble dead to the 'vespiliones' who buried the poor at night) and analyzes the botanical origins and properties of cinnamon and balsam.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's first act. While the previous page focused on the grim vocabulary of Roman funerals and the low social status of undertakers, this page pivots toward a discussion of true nobility—defined by virtue rather than ancestry—and moves into a scene involving the character Buttuhata preparing to wash or "purify" a vessel. The commentary explores the linguistic roots of "washing" and the famous philosophical maxim "Nothing in excess."</meta>

# LIV. IX ACT ONE XLVIII

### For who would consider mute animals noble unless they are brave? And who would celebrate gems unless they were endowed with some inherent power? In the same way, we must account for human beings: virtue is the one and only nobility. Furthermore, all mortals share the same parentage. I commend the words of Marius in the writings of Sallust <note>Gaius Sallustius Crispus, a Roman historian</note>, who does not hide his obscure and ignoble birth, but rather points out that he cannot display the ancestral wax masks, triumphs, or consulships of his forefathers for the sake of winning trust.

### LIX. There is also a need for playful work if you wash with water.
### For then, what is it? BU. I wish, therefore, to wash thoroughly in the basin,
### So that I may satisfy those cross-bearers today.
### Come here! Who comes with me as an attendant in service?
### SA. But whom do you command? BU. Come, Aristophorus and Salax,
### For let the others wait here for us to return,
### Shortly and quickly. LIX. We shall philosophize in the meantime.
### BUT. Let it be so, but hey, take care: nothing in excess.

**There is also a need for playful work.** This refers to something we play at, not something to which we are strictly bound. **I wish, therefore, to wash thoroughly.** <note>original: "Volo ergo pelluere"</note> Here, "pelluere" is used for "perluere" (to wash through). Because of the relationship between certain letters, the *r* transitions into an *l*. **In the basin** <note>original: *in labro*</note>, a vessel. A *labrum* is a large, capacious vessel; according to Cicero in his letters, he says: "If there is no basin in the bathhouse here, see that there is one." Virgil also writes: "The basins foam and boil with must throughout the autumn." **Cross-bearers** <note>original: *cruciferis*</note>; those worthy of being led to the cross. **Come here! Who comes with me as an attendant?** Referring to service or domestic duty. Virgil writes of "the grim service," and sometimes "ministries" <note>original: *ministeria*</note> is used for the "ministers" themselves, just as "the ministry" is used for the "administrator" in the works of Pliny.

**Let the others wait.** That is, let them stay and expect us. **We shall philosophize.** This is a fitting joke about actors or clowns about to "philosophize." But the response of the other is far more beautiful: **Nothing in excess.** <note>original: *Ne quid nimis*</note> This is a serious maxim of the wise if it is carefully weighed. You may seek how long one should philosophize in the fifth volume of Aulus Gellius, chapters 15 and 16; for Ennius’s character Neoptolemus says: "One should taste of philosophy, not gorge oneself on it." 

<margin>
Philosophizing in few words
</margin>
We read of Nero that his mother turned him away from philosophy, warning him that it was contrary to one who was destined to rule. There is also a common opinion that those who devote themselves to philosophy do not believe the gods exist. Apuleius mentions that it is a common charge against philosophers that they are thought to be irreligious and that they deny the gods. But on the contrary, Marcus Tullius <note>Cicero</note> said that philosophy is the guide of life, the seeker of virtue, and the expeller of vice. This is particularly illustrious in comedies involving slaves, a thing which affably moves the audience to joke and laughter.

<margin>
Nothing in excess
</margin>
Hence Buttuhata says here, "nothing in excess," a sentiment which the Greeks express as *nothing in excess* <note>original Greek: μηδὲν ἄγαν</note>. It is a common assertion that this was the saying of Pittacus; others attribute it to Chilo, and some to Solon. Diogenes Laertius investigates this matter in his first volume. Donatus writes that something is missing from this speech when it is said, "nothing in excess"—specifically, the word "do" or "act," which must be supplied to complete the thought: "do nothing in excess."

<summary>This page analyzes a dialogue from the play regarding ritual washing and the character Buttuhata's command for moderation. The commentary draws on various classical authorities to define "nobility" as virtue and explains the Greek origins of the maxim "nothing in excess."</summary>
<keywords>nobility, virtue, Sallust, Marius, washing, basin, cross-bearers, philosophy, moderation, Seven Sages, Cicero, Nero</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological and moral commentary on the play. While the previous page discussed the nature of nobility and the act of washing, this page transitions into Act Two, focusing on the character of the Pharisee and a grammatical defense of the Latin "supine" against the criticisms of the scholar Lorenzo Valla.</meta>

<page-num>XLIX</page-num>
<header>ACT TWO XLIX</header>

# PHARISEE. GLUTTON. CAMP-FOLLOWER.

**PHA.** Why is it that those wicked pettifoggers <note>original: "rabulæ nequam"; a rabula was a low-level, noisy advocate or brawler</note> are going to wash?
They who bind everything they speak in meter
And compose their jokes into poetic verses.
Hey you! What is it they are carrying out in their lap?
**HEL.** What does it matter to you? We barely know this ourselves.
Perhaps a mask fit for playful stage-scenes will be made
When it is washed. Or perhaps it will be some kind of musical instrument.

---

<margin>Rash people</margin>
In this scene, the reckless audacity of those who are "bold" is corrected—if indeed such meddling and impudence *can* be corrected. These are people who pass judgment on matters that do not concern them and which are entirely unknown to them. They fail to heed that rule of Pomponius in the *Digest of Laws, Concerning the Rules of Law*: <note>original: "ff. de regulis iuris"; 'ff' is a standard abbreviation for the Digest of Justinian</note> "It is a fault if anyone meddles in a matter not pertaining to him." Nor do they listen to that saying of Periander: <note>original: "ἐπισφαλὲς προπετεῖα" (episphales propeteia)</note> "rashness is dangerous." These are the people who contaminate everything with poisonous and criminal babbling; they attempt to strip away from others the very humanity they have discarded themselves. They disparage and carp with ignorance and malice at whatever is spoken with honor simply because it does not harmonize with their own delusions. The shrew-like trifles and overly factious tongues of such people ought to be beaten back with a Menalian club <note>original: "Menalio ramo"; likely a reference to the heavy club of Hercules, associated with Mount Maenalus</note>. <margin>Ignorance is the plague of men</margin> For they walk the earth as a hateful burden, less useful than a rotten mushroom. In ancient times, according to Apuleius in his *Florida*, this type of person was called a "Libitina" <note>Libitina was the Roman goddess of funerals and undertakers; the term implies someone associated with death or filth</note>. But why do we rail against them? For nature itself has added the sharpest punishment to them: error and foolishness. Nothing is more ruinous than these, as Cebes—than whom no one is more truthful—testifies. For he says: <note>original: "ἡ γὰρ ἀφροσύνη τοῖς ἀνθρώποις σφίγξ ἐστιν" (hē gar aphrosynē toīs anthrōpois sphinx estin)</note> "For foolishness is a sphinx to men," which is to say, a monster that kills. But now let us hear our Pharisee, who, though he thinks poorly, will be seen by everyone to act well.

**Why is it that.** Look, even in the very entrance-hall of this scene, the danger of rashness lies in wait. **They go to wash.** <note>original: "Lotum eunt"</note> Lorenzo Valla—a man otherwise learned and thoroughly practiced in knowing ancient writings—would have been more admired if he had abstained from rashness himself. He marveled at the "public hallucination" which he claimed had gone unnoticed for so long by everyone who speaks in such a way as: "I go to read" (*eo lectum*) or "I come to read" (*venio lectum*). He even adds that he does not know where this error started or from what fountain of ignorance it flowed. <margin>Against Lorenzo Valla</margin> I wish that Lorenzo had not written quite so rashly (for he did not even spare Saint Jerome from his rather impudent accusations), but perhaps he was seized by the same disease of error as the others. I return to the "I am going to hear." <note>original: "auditu"</note> That which Lorenzo calls an "error" is actually the imitation of the most Latin and most learned writers. Thus Cato, in the first book of *On Agriculture*, when collecting the duties of the farm-manager, says: "Let him be the first to rise from his bed <note>original: "cubitu surgat"</note>, and the last to go to bed <note>original: "cubitum eat"</note>." So too Plautus in the *Casina* (whom we proclaim to be the delight of the Latin language): "See, he returns at last from buying provisions <note>term: <term>obsonatu</term> (the supine form of 'to buy food')</note>." And in the *Menaechmi*: "the guests are already walking before the door before I..."

<sig>F</sig>

<summary>This page opens the second act with the Pharisee's critique of poets and transitions into a scholarly defense of the Latin supine. The commentator attacks the "rashness" of critics like Lorenzo Valla, using classical authorities like Cato and Plautus to justify specific grammatical constructions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's text, specifically addressing the dialogue of the Pharisee from the start of Act Two. It transitions from a defense of Latin grammar (specifically the supine) against the humanist scholar Lorenzo Valla, into a series of etymological and literary definitions of terms like 'verse,' 'poet,' and 'musical instrument.'</meta>

<page-num>L</page-num>
<header>ACT TWO</header>

"I return from shopping" <note>original: "obsonatu redeo"</note>. Thus Papinius <note>The poet Statius (P. Papinius Statius)</note> says in his poem the *Achilleid*: "Then, returning from the hunt <note>original: "uenatu rediturū"</note> at the very threshold, waiting..." I kiss your erudition and industry, Laurentius <note>Laurentius Valla, a famous 15th-century humanist scholar known for his critiques of Latin usage</note>, but I attack your rashness, by which, badly provoked, you have raved against all the authors of good speech just like that Pharisee. Let us return to the text.

<margin>Pettifogger
Worthless</margin>
**Pettifogger: worthless.** Snarlers, little dogs. **Worthless** <note>original: "Nequam"</note> is properly said to come from a lustful laziness, meaning one who "can do nothing" <note>original: "nihil queat"</note>, and is fit for no thing. From this also come "trifles" <note>original: "nugæ"</note>, which do nothing, or are named from "not acting." We have explained the origin of **Pettifogger** <note>original: "Rabula"</note> elsewhere. **They bind**

<margin>Meter</margin>
**with meter.** They bind with rhythmic numbers and weave them together with measured melodies. For **meter** <note>original: "metron"</note> signifies a measure. From this, verses are called "meters" because they do not lack measure.

<margin>Verse</margin>
**With verses**, songs, and rhythmic numbers; the word comes from "sweeping" <note>original: "uerrēdo"</note>, which signifies to pull along or to clean out.

<margin>Deuerra</margin>
Marcus Tullius Cicero in his *Paradoxes* speaks of "those who sweep, those who scatter." From this comes the goddess **Deuerra** <note>The Roman goddess of sweeping and brooms, invoked to protect a house after a birth</note>, mentioned by Augustine in *The City of God*, book 6. Juvenal says: "Sweep the floor." From this also comes the "dragnet" <note>original: "euerriculũ"</note> mentioned in the final chapter of Varro's *On Agriculture*. And a **verse** in prose speech signifies what we commonly call a "line." Marcus Tullius in his *Brutus* says: "In so many thousands of verses." Suetonius uses it in his *Life of Augustus*, and in Asconius Pedianus <note>A 1st-century commentator on Cicero</note> the mention of **verses** is very frequent. Likewise in the preface of the *Digests* and the *Code* concerning the clarifying of ancient law.

<margin>To make poetry</margin>
**They play the poet.** They fashion, they make; in Greek this is *poioumai* <note>original: "ποιέομαι"</note>. But "to fashion" <note>original: "fingere"</note> does not mean "to lie," as the half-learned common folk babble. For the Greek says that the heaven and the earth were "fashioned" and "made." And God, the best and greatest, is called the **Fashioner** or **Poet**.

<margin>It pertains to us:</margin>
**What does it pertain to you?** This means "it is your concern," and rightly so. For it is a fault to meddle in a matter, etc., as stated above. We say "it pertains to me" <note>original: "attinet ad me"</note>. Terence in the *Adelphoe* says: "As far as it pertains to him." And in the *Andria*: "It pertains nothing to me." I believe the preposition has been removed for the sake of the poetry. Priscian, in book 2 of *On Construction*, writes that the Latins frequently say "it pertains to him" <note>original: "ad illũ attinet"</note> and "to that thing." Plautus says "as far as it pertains to the argument" in the *Asinaria*. But in the *Captivi* he says: "Now he holds the knives" <note>original: "cultros attinet"</note>, where "to pertain" signifies "to hold."

<margin>I shall know</margin>
**We shall barely know this ourselves.** This is an archaic way of saying "we shall know" <note>original: "scibimus" for "sciemus"</note>. Marcellus in his collections gathered many such things regarding changed conjugations. And Plautus in the *Captivi* says: "From me you shall hear [audibis] the truth." You may read Donatus <note>A 4th-century grammarian</note> on this subject in his second edition.

<margin>Chance
Instrument</margin>
**Chance.** Perhaps. Used as an adverb. Priscian, book 15. Virgil: "And perhaps [fors] they would have taken the prizes with equal prows." Or it will be a certain **instrument**, a musical tool. An "organ" <note>original: "organum"</note> also signifies a machine, as found in Vitruvius, book 10. From this we call the makers of instruments "organ-makers." Julius Firmicus in the fourth book of his *Mathesis* says:

> ->**PHA. What kind of instrument do you want? Should the verses**<-
> ->**Shriek with pipes? Or should the shameful poets**<-
> ->**Make the stage-masks resound with verses—**<-
> ->**Light trifles, forbidden and lawless in books?**<-

**Do you want...** etc. **To shriek** <note>original: "Stridere"</note> is a word of contempt used toward a musician. Virgil: "Were you not used, you unlearned man, at the crossroads, to destroy a wretched song on a shrieking straw?"

<margin>To shriek</margin>
For **shrieking** <note>original: "stridor"</note> is properly the sound of ropes. It should be noted that the verb **shriek** <note>original: "strido"</note> is one of those found in different conjugations. Priscian, book 9. From this, the syllable...

While the previous page focused on the definitions of 'verse' and 'meter,' this page moves into a sharp rebuke of a 'sacrilegious' character (likely the Pharisee), defending the divine nature of poetry against those who would defile it. It also includes grammatical observations on archaic Latin forms and citations from classical authorities like Plato and Priscian.</meta>

<page-num>LI</page-num>
<header>ACT TWO</header>

it has a middle or internal part, either at the temples or in the center. For the verb *to screech* <note>original: "strideo"</note> in <margin>Strisi</margin> the second conjugation, it shortens in the third. From *strideo* comes *stridi* and *strisi*, but later, for the sake of a more pleasing sound, common usage abolished this latter form, according to Priscian <note>A famous 6th-century Latin grammarian whose works were the standard textbooks in the Middle Ages</note> in Book 10. From *persona* comes "mask" <note>original: "larua"</note>. *Eapse* is used for "she herself" <note>original: "ea ipsa"</note>, just as *reapse* is used for "in the thing itself." Pacuvius <note>An early Roman tragic poet (c. 220–130 BC)</note> says: "If what I did is not ungrateful in the thing itself." **Lawless** <note>original: "Illeges"</note> means filthy, those who do not obey the law of modesty.

### LIX. Hypocrite and rival of no value

> **You have dared to profane the sacred gifts of poets**
> **Rashly with that impious mouth of yours.**
> **The duck is happy in the dung, the young hen eats**
> **Foul scrapings, the goose feeds in the sewer,**
> **Sows root through the privies, and pigs through the latrines.**

**Lixa.** Lixa <note>A character in the play</note> burns with anger against the sacrilegious man who violates the holy poets with a rash tongue and envy. **Hypocrite** <note>original: "Hypocrita"</note>, a pretender—a most fitting insult for one who is religious in name only, who lies with his religious dress, gait, and habit, but whose deeds are far from his words. The earth supports and nourishes no class of men more intolerable than this. For they are first lazy and idle; soon, covered by a cloak and hood, they convert the manners and benefits of religion into the arts of the tongue and words. While they attack vices with fierce threats, they themselves crawl with hidden vices beneath their skin. **Of no value** <note>original: "Nulli ualoris"</note>: *Nulli* is an old declension according to the rule of other names. Terence <note>The Roman playwright</note> uses *nulli* as a genitive in *Andria*: "So lazy, so of no counsel." Donatus and Priscian (Book 13) agree. **Rival** <note>original: "Æmule"</note>: envious, a bitter word spoken without praise. Elsewhere, "rival" <margin>Rival</margin> signifies one who is a student of the same thing. Virgil, in Book 6: "Triton, the rival, having been caught," etc. **Sacred gifts**: this is most rightly said, for Plato admonishes us in a long and very extensive speech that poetry was derived for mortals not by human invention, but by the gift of the gods through divine providence. The Greeks, considering this, derived the name "poet" from a word that sometimes means to make something out of something else, and sometimes to create out of nothing. And the Latins seem to have recognized the divinity of poets when they called them "prophets" <note>original: "uates"</note> from that power of the mind which is greatest in humans. Therefore, poets are called "holy" by Ennius <note>Quintus Ennius, often called the father of Roman poetry</note> and <margin>Poet</margin> "sacred gifts" by our author here. But that Pharisee, placed in the second kind of madness (which the divine Plato in his *Ion* teaches proceeds from human diseases), pays no attention to these things. **Rashly to profane**: to contaminate and make impious in a frivolous, tasteless, atrocious, and insane way. **With an impious [mouth]**: sacrilegious, requiring atonement, and most blameworthy. **The duck is happy in the dung**: in excrement and filth. Alexander of Aphrodisias <note>A famous commentator on Aristotle (c. 200 AD)</note> asks in his *Problems* why dung, or human manure, is of such a foul smell, while that of certain animals, especially goats, smells with an aromatic sweetness. He says it is because humans eat multi-formed and moist food in abundance and exercise less. Therefore, it is not well digested and is among the foulest of odors. Pontic ducks <margin>Duck</margin> have a powerful ability to clear away poisons. Aulus Gellius <note>A 2nd-century Roman author</note> recites this from the writings of the freedman of Pompey the Great in volume 17, chapter 16.

<vocab>Lixa, Hypocrita, Plato, Ennius, Anas, Aulus Gellius, Pompeius Magnus, Priscianus, Terence, Alexander Aphrodiseus</vocab>

## ACT TWO

<margin>Pullastra</margin>
**Pullastra.** A small and still youthful hen; Marcus Varro <note>A prolific Roman scholar and writer (116–27 BC)</note> calls young hens *pullastrae*. From *pullus* [chick], the diminutive *pullicinus* <note>original: "ὑποκοριστικῶς .i. per diminutionem"</note> is also derived. **Think of filthy things.** <note>original: "Exputa spurca"</note> Spit <note>original: "Sputum"</note> is said to differ from saliva in this way: *sputum* results from an excess of moisture, whereas *saliva* is not only moisture but often arises from a person's ailment. The goose is celebrated by Pliny in Book 10 for the memorable story of the philosopher Lacydes <note>A Greek philosopher who famously had a pet goose that followed him everywhere</note>.

<margin>Cloaca</margin>
**Cloaca.** Sewer-like filthiness. Ulpian <note>An influential Roman jurist</note>, in the 13th book of the *Digest*, says a sewer is a concave place through which certain waste flows, and that the name encompasses both the tube and the pipe; it comes from *colluendo* [washing together]. Therefore, it is a Latin word as Festus <note>A 2nd-century AD Roman grammarian</note> prefers. From this comes the "sewer-like river" used by Cato for all the filth of the sewers. However, in common speech, we also take it for a place which others prefer to call a *latrina* [latrine] from *latendo* [hiding].

<margin>Latrina</margin>
Cicero seems to agree in his oration *Pro Plancio*: "You said," he remarked, "that I was making a citadel out of a sewer." And Pompeius Festus writes: "To sewer" <note>original: "cloacare"</note> means to defile, from which we get *cloaca*. Likewise, Suetonius Tranquillus left it written concerning Nero that he was accustomed to beating those returning from dinner and wounding those who resisted, and submerging them in the sewers. **Sows.** The pig delights in mud, according to Horace: "and the sow, friend to the mud" <note>original: "amica luto sus"</note>.

<margin>Foria</margin>
**Foricas.** Latrines. For *foria* are liquid excrements, hence *foricae*. The Satirist poet <note>Juvenal</note> says: "Then they rent latrines for those who have returned." In the case of *foriolus*, it describes someone who easily emits *foria*, namely, one with a loose stomach. Lauerius says: "You are a *foriolus* and you defecate in your testicles."

->You, long-eared foal of a wild donkey,<-
->Delight in the mud and mire, most ignorant beast.<-
->You teach us to despise the sacred rites of poems,<-
->And say they are things accursed from books:<-
->From whose books? Did the voice of God despise<-
->Terence? Lest you kick against the prick (they say):<-
->Paul invokes Menander, Aratus, and Epimenides<-
->As witnesses, prophets, and those conscious of the truth.<-

<margin>Onager</margin>
**Asinini onagri.** The wild donkey. He not inaptly calls him a wild donkey, for he who despises the studies of humanity casts off all civilization. **Aurit.** [Long-eared] with large and protruding ears. Elsewhere it signifies other things, according to Festus. **Belua indoctissima.** [Most ignorant beast] or wild animal. Antiquity used "beast" <note>original: "belua"</note> indifferently; from *belua* comes "beastly" and "beastly pleasure."

<margin>Belua</margin>
**Execrata.** [Accursed] or cursed, banished, hissed off the stage. **Cuius libris:** [From whose books?] You demand to know, he says, "from whose books?"; as if he were saying, "from yours indeed, but not from those that are divine, human, and philosophical." This he proves through the divine oracles of the New Testament; the following text can be understood from Jerome in his letter to Magnus, the orator of the city of Rome.

<margin>Calcitrones</margin>
**Lest he says you kick against the prick,** <note>A reference to Acts 9:5, meaning to struggle uselessly against a superior force</note> or strike with the heels. Hence *calcitrones* are horses that strike with their heels. Plautus, in the *Asinaria* [The Comedy of Asses], shouts from afar if he sees someone coming toward him:

<margin>Recalcitro</margin>
**a kicker** <note>original: "calcitronem"</note>. Horace used this word in the second book of his *Satires* <note>original: "sermonum"</note>.

**Pullastra (Young Hen), Cloaca (Sewer), Latrina (Latrine), Foria (Liquid Excrement), Onager (Wild Donkey), Belua (Beast), Calcitrones (Kickers), Varro, Pliny, Ulpian, Cicero, Festus, Horace, Terence, Menander, Aratus, Epimenides, Plautus**

# ACT TWO

"If you stroke him clumsily, he kicks back, safe on all sides." <note>original: "Cui male si palpere recalcitrat undiq; tutus" — a quote from Horace (Satires 2.1.20) regarding Augustus Caesar</note>. This was said of Augustus Caesar, who rejected the flatteries of fawners. In the edict of the aediles <note>Roman officials in charge of public order and markets</note>, it is written: "a horse that bites and kicks is defective." Paul [quotes] Menander. Jerome says that this six-foot line of Menander’s is: "Bad company corrupts good character" <note>original: "Corrumpūt bonos mores confabulationes malæ" — 1 Corinthians 15:33</note>. [Paul also quotes] Aratus. In that passage, many of our Bible texts have a flaw in the reading; for where Aratus should be, Horace is substituted. But no one sets a hand to correcting the books of prophecy; we do not wish to understand clearly before the Prince [God], whom we daily worship with solecisms and barbarisms <note>grammatical errors and uncultured speech</note>, contrary to what is written in the canonical decrees: "Since voluntary ignorance is harmful to everyone, it is especially dangerous for priests." Item: "Because you have rejected knowledge, says the Lord, I will reject you from being a priest" <note>Hosea 4:6</note>. Augustine, however, demands a "blessing" <note>original: "benedictionem"</note> more than "good-diction" <note>original: "bonadictionem" — a play on words suggesting that a priest's holiness is more important than his perfect Latin grammar</note> from priests, but things have come to such a point that you more often see dice <note>original: "talos"</note> in the hands of evil priests than a librarian’s pen-knife, and perhaps even a sword or dagger, contrary to the rule: "Clerics carrying arms shall be excommunicated." Item: in the sacred constitutions of the Fathers these words are found: "Clerics who have chosen to take up arms, if found out, having lost the rank of their order, shall be committed to a monastery forever." But because they read it written down, they think it is enough for it to be written, and that it need not actually be done. Hence the satiric poet says: "That there are ghosts and subterranean realms... even boys do not believe, unless they are not yet old enough to pay for a bath" <note>Juvenal, Satire 2</note>. Someone might say: "What is it to you? What does it matter to you whether we handle dice or gambling pieces?" Well, listen: does it not matter to those carried in a ship or a carriage to hire the most skilled driver, lest the safety of all be endangered? You would certainly say so if you were among the passengers. But priests (as read in Distinction 95) are placed to rule the Church; thus, you hold the position of a driver.

<margin>Praise of poets</margin> Paul, writing to Titus, calls Epimenides a Prophet. The holy doctors also confess this. Gerson <note>Jean Gerson, a famous 14th-century theologian</note> in his lecture on Luke says that the Apostle first called this man a poet and then a prophet. It seems to pertain not a little to the praise of poets that St. Thomas [Aquinas] writes that those who lead others to virtue through representations [are like poets], and that poets existed as "theologizers" even before the Seven Sages. The Emperor [Justinian], in the preface of the *Old Digest* in the first constitution, calls the poet Homer "the father of all virtue." Anaximenes is the authority for the fact that the Seven Sages were most studious of the poetic art. I will not even mention the Sibyls. The pagan world gave the first place to Aristotle the Peripatetic; he testified that among the ancients, the poets and theologians were the same, and he himself left behind three books written about poets, as well as two books on the poetic art. But now some little bearded hater of poets <note>original: "barbasculos osor poetarū" — likely referring to pseudo-intellectuals or overly-austere critics</note> will object to me that Saint Jerome was beaten with rods for reading them. I shall let Jerome answer in his *Apology against Rufinus*, where he asserts that it was a dream, and teaches therein that one should trust dreams least of all, based on the authority of the prophets. To which that line of Tibullus chimes in: "Dreams mock the fearful in the deceptive night." And in the greatest of poets [Virgil] it is written: "But the spirits send false dreams to the world above" <note>original: "Sed falsa ad supos mittunt insomnia manes" — Aeneid 6.896</note>. We read that Alexander the Great dreamed so that he would not believe in dreams.

## ACT TWO

->Poetry is the beginning of all things<-
->And before heaven, and before the world, before all things<-
->Which Moses your seer suggests to you<-
->Who himself also toyed with poetic verses<-
->Poets are wise to more learned men<-
->To you, a barbaric, unlearned teller of tales<-
->To a foolish fanatic they seem filthy<-
->"A little donkey at the lyre" is an old proverb.<-
<note>The phrase "Asellus ad lyram" (a donkey at the lyre) is a classical Greek and Latin proverb used to describe someone who has no appreciation for art or music.</note>

---

<margin>The Figure of Poetry</margin> Poetry is the beginning of all things. Thus the Greek adores the almighty God in the creed: "I believe in one God the Father Almighty, the poet or maker of heaven and earth" <note>original Greek: "πιστεύω εἰς ἕνα θεὸν πατέρα παντοκράτορα ποιητὴν οὐρανοῦ καὶ γῆς" — the author highlights that the Greek word 'poietēn' means both 'maker' and 'poet'</note>, that is, I believe in one God the Father Almighty, the poet or maker of heaven and earth. Thus Moses in the Pentateuch <note>The first five books of the Bible</note> says: "In the beginning he poetized," that is, in the beginning he made or fashioned <note>original Greek: "ἐν ἀρχῇ ἐποίησε" (en archē epoiēsen). The author is punning on the verb 'poiein,' to make/to write poetry</note>. And "poetry" signifies a making or a fashioning; however, "to fashion" does not mean "to lie," as the common folk falsely believe, but to "compose." From this comes the word "figure" <term>figura: a rhetorical or artistic shape</term>. This, the holy doctor <note>likely referring to St. Augustine</note> calls "representation," which if you take away, what will remain in the most sacred wisdom? Let him who says poets lie beware, therefore, lest he be guilty of the very vice of which he speaks. 

Because Moses your seer is both a poet and a prophet from the "force of the mind" <note>The author uses the Latin "a vi mentis" to provide a popular (though folk) etymology for 'vates' (seer/prophet)</note>. We accept that Moses not only toyed with verses, but also those through whom the greatest part of divine wisdom has been derived to us: Job the Edomite, King David, Solomon, and Isaiah the prophet. Who does not know that the writings of these men consist of verses in their native tongue? 

Furthermore (to consult our scholars studying grammar), this name "Moses" declines in various ways in divine scripture: *Moses, Mosae*; *Moyses, Moyseae*; *Moseus, Mosei*; and *Moyseus, Moysei*. Josephus, in the second book of *Antiquities*, writes that the name "Moses" was bestowed upon him because of the event itself: that he was saved from the river. For the Egyptians call water *mos*, and *yses* means "saved"; by composing the name from both, they called him "Moses," that is, "saved from the water." 

I adhere to the text, the Hebrew truth, and the Mosaic law, which Josephus himself would not oppose if he were alive; as he writes in Book 2 of *Against Apion*, it is not right to wonder if the Jews most bravely endure death for their laws. An example of their laws being most bravely guarded by the Jews exists in Book 18 of the *Antiquities*, chapter nineteen. I say, I agree with the text. So the text says in the book *Elle sche moth* <note>original Hebrew: 'Elef Shemot' (These are the names), the Hebrew title for the Book of Exodus</note>, that is, "These are the names," which we call Exodus. "And she called his name Moses, and said: because I drew him out from the waters." 

Therefore, it comes from the Hebrew verb *mashah*, which means to lead out, to extract, or to bring forth. My teacher Johann Reuchlin <note>A famous German humanist and the first great non-Jewish Hebrew scholar in Europe</note>, the head and pillar of life-giving wisdom, interpreted this in the work he dedicated to his brother Dionysius <margin>The book *On the Rudiments of Hebrew*</margin>. That book was considered no rarer than a phoenix just a few years ago. But as Horace says quite frankly,

<meta>The catchword 'Sed' (But) indicates the start of the next page, likely continuing the quote from Horace.</meta>

# PAGE LV

## ACT TWO

<margin>Fables</margin>
"Virtue," it is said, "we hate while it is with us; once it is gone from our sight, we mourn it." <note>original Latin: "Virtutem inquit praesentem odimus sublatā ex oculis querimur." This is a paraphrase of Horace, Odes 3.24.31.</note> I, however, consider the day to have dawned for me when I behold the beautiful offspring of my teacher, which had been hidden at home like a family shrine. Poets are wise to the more learned men. There is a witty and charming fable by Aesop the storyteller about the rooster and the jasper <note>A jasper is a type of gemstone. In the fable, a rooster finds a jewel but prefers a grain of barley, representing those who do not value wisdom.</note>. If our "Pharisee" <note>A common term in this period for a hypocritical critic of humanist learning.</note> were to learn what this means rhetorically <note>original Greek: "ῥητορικῶς"</note>, he would certainly find that the final conclusion applies exactly to him. [To the] barbarous, rustic, and boorish. [To the] myth-maker, the mocker, the useless trifler.

<margin>Grammar</margin>
According to Cato (as cited by Pliny), all nations were once called "barbarians" except for the Greeks. The science of Grammar is that which banishes "barbarism"—that is, rusticity in speaking and writing—as well as another vice we might call "solecism" <term>solecism: a grammatical mistake in the construction of a sentence or a breach of etiquette</term>.

<margin>To the most unlearned.</margin>
"To the most unlearned": this term explains what the word "barbarous" is intended to mean.

<margin>To the fanatic.</margin>
"To the fanatic": a fool, someone haunted by ghosts, driven by furies, or a blabbermouth who speaks futile and frivolous things without caution or consideration. Indeed, such fools are called "fanatic," "lymphatic" <note>meaning "driven mad by water-nymphs"</note>, or "crazed" because they are agitated by a divine frenzy. As the Satiric poet says: "An old fanatic with white hair" <note>original Latin: "Et crine senex phanaticus albo." A quote from Juvenal, Satires 4.124.</note>.

<margin>A little donkey at the lyre.</margin>
We use this proverb for someone inept, much like the saying "He teaches his own Minerva" <note>original Latin: "Suam minervam docet." This refers to trying to teach someone who is already an expert, as Minerva was the goddess of wisdom.</note>. However, "inept" does not mean what the common people think it does; for that, you should consult the discussion by Crassus in Cicero's book *On the Orator*. According to Aristotle (as found in Aulus Gellius, Book 4), the phrase "a little donkey at the lyre" describes someone who is naturally unsuited for a task.

It should be noted that there were many proverbs <note>original Greek: "παροιμίαι" (paroimiai)</note> among the ancients concerning the donkey, such as these:
- "At the donkey's peeping out" <note>original Greek: "ἐξ ὄνου παρακύψεως." Used whenever great and unexpected things are suddenly revealed.</note>.
- Also, "About a donkey's shadow" <note>original Greek: "περὶ ὄνου σκιᾶς." Concerning those who argue over useless trifles.</note>.
- Also, "A donkey to the flute" <note>original Greek: "ὄνος πρὸς αὐλὸν"</note>.
- Also, "A donkey at the lyre" <note>original Greek: "ὄνος λύρας"</note>.
- Also, "A donkey into the chaff" <note>original Greek: "ὄνος εἰς ἄχυρα." Concerning those who happen upon good fortune beyond their expectations.</note>.
- Also, "You are shearing a donkey" <note>original Greek: "ὄνον κείρεις." Concerning men who attempt ineffective or impossible tasks.</note>.
- Similar to this is: "You are looking for a donkey's wool" <note>original Greek: "ὄνον πόκους ζητεῖς"</note>.
- And in Aulus Gellius: "A donkey becoming one among the apes."

But if only time and business
Were permitted to me, and I had the leisure, you beast,
I would show you exactly what kind of man you are.
But I hear my companions bursting in.
**PHARISEE.** I am going home, burdened with these terrible insults.
**LIXA.** Now Heluo, you yourself know how much it would benefit the cause if you were to smash this mocker's mouth.
**HELVO.** I will follow the man; if he mutters, he shall eat my fist.

"Were permitted" refers to time; "leisure" refers to freedom from business. "But they burst in" is a phrase not unsuited to signify the tumult of people rushing together in a disorganized way. This is the "preparation" <note>original Greek: "παρασκευὴ" (paraskeuē)</note>, that is, the setting of the stage for the following scene.

It concludes with a short dramatic dialogue where characters threaten a 'Pharisee' figure for his insults against poetry.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the commentary from the previous page, moving from the definitions of "fanatic" and "barbarian" to the formal beginning of Act Three. # ACT THREE LVI

<margin>
Honored
Burdened
</margin>
I am going home; the conclusion of the speech has convicted the Pharisee <note>A recurring term for a hypocritical or legalistic critic of humanist education.</note>. For he admits from his heart that he is defeated by those very foul words with which the foolish fanatics—who are more "fated" than "prophetic"—are beaten back. "Honored" <note>original: "Honustus"</note> and "Burdened" <note>original: "Oneratus"</note> are words that poets often confuse; however, "Burdened" refers to one weighed down by any load, whereas "Honored" <note>literally: "full of honor"</note> refers to one whose weight is a source of prestige. See Servius’s commentary on the first book of the *Aeneid*. Now Heluo <note>The Glutton</note> confirms his argument with the greater protection of Lixa <note>The Camp-Follower</note>, sharing his duty toward Heluo against a man with whom it is easier to fight with fists than with arguments. "It would be for the matter" <note>original: "Esset in rem"</note> means how much it might pertain to the business at hand. A "mocking mouth" <note>original: "Cavillatorium os"</note> is one that is witty, full of futile words, slanderous, and scurrilous. Against those with wagging tongues, a well-worn proverb is circulated: "The tongue kills more than the sword." To this, the Greek verse subscribes: "The tongue has led many to destruction" <note>original Greek: "ἡ γλῶσσα πόλλους εἰς ὄλεθρον ἤγαγεν"</note>. I shall follow the man; if he mutters a word, he shall "eat fists"—this is the "dinner" that ought to be prepared for the stupid. Indeed, Plautus’s character Sosia <note>A servant from Plautus’s play *Amphitruo*.</note> does not like to "eat" where fists are served on the table.

## ACT THREE
->BUTTUBATTA. SALAX. LIXA. ARISTOPHORUS.<-

**BUT.** May all the gods destroy you comic gluttons and boozers! **LIX.** Hey, I want you! **BUT.** What do you want, Lixa? **LIX.** May the gods wish you to perish in a business that is not good. **BUT.** Do you mean in a bad business? **LIX.** No, in the very worst.

<margin>
Catastrophe
</margin>
In a Comedy, there is usually a "catastrophe" <term>catastrophe: the final resolution or "turning point" of a plot in classical drama, often leading to a happy ending</term>, that is, a turn toward a joyful outcome once the knowledge of the facts has been revealed to everyone. And so, in this third act, the whole matter is addressed, and the remaining things are explained. For he wanted to wash it in a bronze basin to satisfy those "cross-bearing" actors <note>Likely a derogatory term for the performers</note>; therefore, Buttubatta comes forward with his washed-up merchandise, and in order to invite his fellow performers to a "kiss," he cooks up some remarkable deceptions. Soon, having played them, once the matter is revealed, he incites them to the most slanderous cursing. "May the gods [destroy] you gluttons." Buttubatta says to himself: "you gluttons" <note>original: "edones"</note>, that is, voracious, greedy eaters; in Greek, they are called "eaters" <note>original: "phagones"</note> from their gluttony. "Boozers" <note>original: "combibones"</note> from the habit of drinking together. "Destroy you all" <note>original: "Perdant omneis"</note>—this uses ancient orthography. "What do you want, Lixa?" Terence writes: "what do you want me to do," adding the word for "do." Plautus in his play *The Pot of Gold* writes: "what do you want for yourself?" "May the gods wish you to perish in business"—this is a salty and festive joke in the style of Plautus.

**BUT.** I would much prefer you to prophesy better on my behalf.
Look at what I’ve brought, hold it, consider
How beautifully and charmingly it shines after its bath!

<summary>This page marks the transition to Act Three of the play, using classical philology to explain character names and the concept of a "catastrophe" in drama. The author analyzes Latin and Greek terms for gluttony and provides a commentary on the comedic dialogue between Buttubatta and Lixa.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions into the third act of the play. The commentary shifts from character motivations to specific linguistic analysis, particularly regarding the vocabulary of bathing, omens, and the physical prop of a skull being handled on stage.</meta>

# ACT THREE LVII

->ACT THREE<-

Truly, I am a wretched man, who must comply with such cheap
**Slaves** and a hanging,
Anxious crowd of plunderers, or rather, of thieves.
It is in your hand; come now, offer it to be honored;
Now let them kiss this sacred object in a circle.

<margin>
I divine
An omen
</margin>
"In my way" <note>original: "Meatim"</note>—just as "in your way" <note>original: "tuatim"</note>—are adverbs born from pronouns, according to the authorities Donatus, Sergius, and Priscian. Priscian writes in his eighth and eleventh books that these adverbs signify specific persons. "To divine" <note>original: "Ominari"</note> means to prophesy or to take an omen; from this we get "omen," "of an omen," and "ominous." We read that Pythagoras observed not only the voices of the gods but also those of men, which they call omens; the ancients used the active form of the verb, "to omen." "You see" <note>original: "Tu vide"</note>—here he points to a skull <note>original: "calvaria"</note>. "I brought" <note>original: "Tuli"</note> is used for "I have brought" <note>original: "attuli"</note> through a shortening of the word <term>apheresis|the loss of one or more sounds from the beginning of a word</term>. "See" with the eyes. "Hold" with the hands. "Behold, observe." "Is it not pretty?" <note>original: "Num bellule"</note>—meaning beautifully, skillfully, or neatly. "And elegantly"—brightly, clearly. "After the bath": in Latin, the word "bath" <note>original: "balneum"</note> is used properly for one that is private. For when we say "baths" in the feminine plural <note>original: "balneas"</note>, we mean the public "hot baths" <note>original: "thermas"</note>, according to the author Marcus Varro, though this rule is not always followed strictly. Indeed, Marcus Cicero, in the second book of *On the Orator*, calls the private bath of Marcus Brutus "baths" using that feminine plural form. Cicero (or perhaps Cornificius) in the fourth book <note>referring to the *Rhetorica ad Herennium*, which was then attributed to Cicero</note> says, "For as he happened to come here into the baths," in a passage on the rhetorical ornament we call "description." The satiric poet <note>Juvenal</note> also says, "and you carry a raw peacock into the baths." <margin>
Bath
Hot baths
Public baths
</margin> Ulpian, in the fiftieth book of the *Digests*, used the term "public bath," as if "bath" could signify both private and public facilities indiscriminately. We derive the word from the Greek *balanion* <note>original: βαλάvιοv</note>, because it drives anxiety from the mind, as Augustine notes in the ninth book of his *Confessions*. Our people should therefore say *balinium*, but they pronounce it *balneum* through a shortening of the sounds. "Truly I am wretched": "Truly" <note>original: "Næ"</note> is used for "greatly" by Cicero, as in "Truly, for them this is better." It is a particle very familiar to Plautus; see Donatus in the prologue to *Andria*. "With such cheap slaves": he is indignant at having to serve such cheap actors <note>original: "ludionibus"</note>; through this, he strikes up a desire to examine the skull, so that he may playfully mock those greedy for knowledge. "Anxious" <note>original: "Suspensili"</note>: meaning ambiguous, worried, or hesitating. "Crowd of plunderers" <note>original: "raponum"</note>: meaning snatchers or pillagers. Antiquity used *rapones* for robbers. "Or rather, of thieves" is a rhetorical "correction" <note>original: "correctio"</note>, called *epanorthosis* <note>original: ἐπαvόρθωσισ</note> in Greek. Cicero or Virginius mentions this in his fourth book. Saint Jerome confirms this work belongs to Cicero, though Fabius and Plutarch disagree, and many of their arguments are quite strong. "It is in your hand": we understand this to mean the skull is being turned over and offered in turn. "To be honored": to be venerated. <margin>In a circle</margin> "In a circle" <note>original: "Circulatim"</note>: man by man in a circle, or orbicularly; an adverbial expression derived from "circus" and "circle."

ARI. It seems to be the cursed skull
Of a dead pimp; take it to the gallows!
Where did you find this dead skull?

<meta>This page continues the third act of the play. The previous page discussed the arrival of a skull (calvaria) on stage; here, the characters Buttubatta and Lixa debate the skull's significance while the commentary provides a deep dive into the etymology of death, reclining, and the medical uses of human remains in antiquity.</meta>

# ACT THREE LVIII

->ACT THREE<-

**BUTTUBATTA.** In the charnel house among the rotting bones
Where both the rich and the poor lie,
The most worthy head of all mortals.

<margin>Charnel house. To lie down. To lie upon. To apply oneself. To recline. Death.</margin>

Aristophorus does not agree with the decreed supplication; he avoids and detests the skull <note>original: "caluariam"</note> out of its foulness. It is the skull of a deceased pimp <note>original: "lenonis"</note>. *Kranion* <note>original: "κρανίον"</note> is the Greek term; the Latins call it a *calvaria*. A little further above we taught that "Catapocia" <note>original: "Catapocia"</note> [pills] were made from a skull, according to the discovery of Artemon; if someone drinks these with spring water, they are said to cure "comitial diseases" <term>epilepsy|historically called 'comitial disease' because its occurrence was seen as a bad omen during Roman public assemblies, or 'comitia'</term>. This is seen in Pliny, volume twenty-eight <note>original: "duodevigefimo", likely referring to Book 28 of Natural History</note>, chapter 1, regarding portents devised from skulls for harmless remedies. It seems execrable, dire, abominable, polluted, and pestilential. "To the gallows with you!" <note>original: "Ad furcas age"</note>—return it, take it back to where you stole it: to the charnel house <note>original: "Craniario"</note>, where skulls are stored and kept. "Among the rotting bones"—moist, wasting away, decayed. "Where they lie" <note>original: "Vbi cubāt"</note>—the grammarians say that "to lie" <note>original: "cubare"</note> and "to recline" <note>original: "cumbere"</note> properly pertain to those who are ill. Horace says: "He lies across the Tiber," <note>original: "Trans tyberim cubat"</note> and elsewhere, "He lies here in the valley of Quirinus." Birds are said to "incubate" <note>original: "Incubare"</note>, as are "tyrant-suppressors" in foreign kingdoms. "To apply oneself" <note>original: "Incumbere"</note> means to give diligent effort; "to recline" <note>original: "Discumbere"</note> is for the sake of taking food; "to sleep together" <note>original: "Concūbere"</note> is for sexual matters. "To lie next to" <note>original: "Accumbere"</note> means to lie with another. Those who seek an omen while in bed are said to "observe the sleepers." "Both rich and poor"—excellently written. For "good death" <note>or "the certainty of death"</note> deceives no one, passes over no one, nor favors this one more than that one, but removes everyone with an equal scale; "it strikes with an equal foot the towers of kings and the huts of the poor" <note>A famous quote from Horace, Odes 1.4.13</note>. Tiberius Caesar forced those wishing to die to continue living. Saint Augustine, in the first book of *The City of God*, says that a greater mind must be rightly attributed to one who can bear a life of toil than to one who flees it; death is a light punishment from God. In the *Tusculan Disputations*, Marcus Tullius [Cicero] discourses most extensively about death.

**LIXA.** Where? For it could be worthy of either the underworld or a thunderbolt. There is a knot in this rush <note>proverb: "Nodum in scirpo quaeris" - looking for a problem where there is none</note>; the things you say are obscure. Answer me: what are you doing with this skull?
**BUTTUBATTA.** The most worthy head of all mortals
To be prayed to; it advises on what must be done,
And whatever it advises, the prince does;
All power is situated in its hand.

<margin>Rush</margin>

"Where," "why"—Terence says: "Whereby I am more fair to Pamphilus." "For it could be": Lixa marvels that Buttubatta called a head "most worthy of all mortals," which he could have known. "There is a knot in this rush" <note>original: "Huic scirpo nodus est"</note>—you speak clearly enough, yet I am nonetheless stuck. This is an old proverb about someone who is hesitating where there was nothing to hesitate about, and about those who act absurdly so that they distress their own minds. "You seek a knot in a rush" <note>original: "Nodum in scirpo quæris"</note>—Terence. Furthermore, a "scirpus" is a rush <term>rush|a grass-like plant known for its smooth, knotless stem, making a 'knot' in one impossible</term>. From this comes the "scirpicula" sickle. Varro, book 1, *On Agriculture*...

<summary>Buttubatta presents a skull as a powerful advisor to a prince, while Lixa expresses confusion and skepticism. The commentary explores the equality of death, the linguistic nuances of reclining and illness, and the medicinal use of human remains.</summary>
<keywords>skull, charnel house, death, Horace, Augustine, Cicero, epilepsy, proverbs, etymology, Roman medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly and dramatic commentary on the skull (calvaria) introduced in the previous scene. While the previous page focused on the physical decomposition of the skull and its medicinal uses in antiquity, this page shifts toward the spiritual and superstitious implications of the object, exploring Roman beliefs about ghosts, household gods, and ancestral spirits.</meta>

# ACT THREE LIX

Points 14 and 15 regarding the "rush" <note>original: "scirpo"</note>. These are obscure matters. He signifies what he wishes to be understood by it. Donatus says a "rush" is without a knot <note>original: "enodem"</note>—which we call "unkotted"—and it is a species of smooth reed. "What do you do?" <note>original: "Quid facis"</note>: at what do you value this? "This head," he says, "does this"; he arranges the virtues of the head for veneration and worship, inviting the actors, the fickle crowd, and those easily persuaded. He advises that this must be carried out. Consultation is a life-saving matter; as Sallust says, "first having consulted," and this order is observed by the wise. For Terence is the authority that everything should be tried by counsel rather than by arms. "All the power of this is in the hand": It is a most miserable thing for all the power of a prince to be in the hand of a foolish head <note>original: "stolidi capitis"</note>. This text is most closely joined to the hypothesis of the argument.

->SALLUSTIUS. It may have spirits, but it has no hands,<-
->Unless it be a ghost, which hides its claws,<-
->Which every night terrifies those at rest,<-
->And marks their faces with scars from its claws,<-
->A monstrous terror and fierce spirits.<-

<margin>Specter</margin>
"To have spirits" <note>original: "Manes habere"</note>. A likeness of spirits, a terror of ghosts <note>original: "laruarum"</note>; there is a pun in the phrasing. "But not hands" <note>original: "At haud manus"</note>; it is observed not to be "handed" <note>meaning: it lacks physical hands to strike</note>. "Unless it be a ghost" <note>original: "lemurium"</note>: a wandering shadow of a deceased person, stripped of the rewards of life. 
<margin>Ghosts</margin>
They are thought to be called *Lemures*, as if *Remures* from Remus; when his brother Romulus wished to appease the shadow of the slain Remus, he instituted the *Lemuria*—that is, the rites for deceased parents <note>original: "parentalia"</note>. Ovid mentions this in the *Fasti*. 
<margin>Lemuria</margin>
Martianus, however, believes that the *Manes* (spirits) are called *Lemures* when they remain hidden in the same bodies after life and stay with them. If they were aided by the integrity of their former life, 
<margin>Household Gods</margin>
they turn into *Lares* <term>Lares|guardian deities of the Roman household and city</term>. But if they turn to depravity, the same author claims they are called *Larvae* <note>original: "laruas"</note>. To this is added the testimony of Augustine in the ninth book of *The City of God*, and Varro in the first book of *The Life of the Fathers*. "It hides its claws": it covers or conceals them. "It terrifies those at rest": this signifies a malicious ghost 
<margin>Malicious Ghosts</margin>
and a terrifying nocturnal image, a monstrous terror to mortals and an evening specter. "And fierce spirits": terrible, violent, and threatening.

->BUTTUBATTA. Please, let me properly recount to you<-
->What benefit and what harm this skull brings,<-
->Or what it is entirely, where it came from,<-
->Whither it tends, and where it will soon arrive.<-
->ARISTOPHORUS. Let us be silent; I desire nothing more eagerly anywhere.<-

"Please" <note>original: "Sodes"</note>: a flattering adverb, meaning "if you dare" or "if you can." It is also sometimes used for encouraging. As Terence says in *Andria*: "Tell me, please, who had Chrysis yesterday?" Or Horace: "A better law, tell me, please." "To recount" <note>original: "Recensere"</note>: to recite in order. "What benefit": for the purpose of venerating. "And what harm": for the purpose of cursing. He then wishes to introduce those who are deceived by the adoration of the skull. "Let us be silent": having been persuaded, they turn to silence to listen about the skull. "Anywhere" <note>original: "Vsquam"</note>: signifies place rather than time. Donatus notes this in his commentary on the *Eunuch*. "More eagerly" <note>original: "Appetitius"</note>: more longingly, more ardently.

manes, lemures, Lemuria, Lares, caluaria, Donatus, Terentius, Ouidius, Martianus, Augustinus, Varro

It maintains the dialogue between Buttubatta and Sallustius while referencing classical authorities on anatomy and logic.</meta>

# ACT THREE LX

->**BUTTUBATTA.** You see this skull, recedingly bald and hollow,<-
->And bony and shell-like; it is useful in three<-
->Ways: it creates number, gives shade, and fills places.<-
->**SALLUSTIUS.** Our Buttubatta, you seem to chatter<-
->This dialectical sophism to mock your own people.<-
->Do you not see how this executioner has<-
->Twisted his mouth; soon, in speaking the play,<-
->**ARISTOTELES.** Ah, speak! **BUTTUBATTA.** You ask? **LIBANIUS.** Ah, we pray, speak! **SALLUSTIUS.** Just speak now!<-

"You see": By a joke, he makes them more attentive. "This skull, recedingly bald" <note>original: "recalvastram"</note>: smooth or slightly smooth, and less hairy. We call them "calvastros" and "calvatulos" by diminutives—those who are not entirely bald and hairless, whom the ancient Romans used to call Lydians. "Hollow": by its own nature, not by handiwork. "Bony": made of bones, as Chrysippus writes in his book *On Providence* <note>original Greek: "περὶ προνοίας"</note>. When nature was shaping human bodies, a more subtle reason and the utility of the work itself required that it compose the head from the thinnest and smallest little bones, 
<margin>
Bone-by-bone
</margin>
the number of which is said by doctors to be twenty-three. Indeed, Macrobius writes in Book 7 that bones lack sensation. From "bone" <note>Latin: "os"</note> is formed "bony" <note>Latin: "osseus"</note>. Juvenal: "The handles of the knives were bony." 
<margin>
Ossuary
</margin>
And the adverb is "bone-by-bone" <note>original: "ossiculatim"</note>. But legal experts use "ossuary" <note>original: "ossuarium"</note> for a small box for the deceased. "Creates number": it increases, makes, or supplies. Macrobius explains what it means to "fill the number" regarding the soul, as stated by a most learned poet, in *Scipio's Dream*. "It fills shade and places": because it is a continuous body, whereas when he said it "creates number," he signified something discrete. 
<margin>
To chatter
Trifles
Triflers
</margin>
"To chatter" <note>original: "Garrire"</note>: this is said of birds, and of those who speak trifles <note>original: "gerras"</note>, nonsense, and absurdities, or make an inept noise. Hence "garrulous" people are also called "triflers" <note>original: "gerrones"</note> and "nuisances." Terence: "You chatter." Plautus in *The Pot of Gold*: "By Hercules, I am accustomed to chatter trifles."

<margin>
Sophists
</margin>
"This dialectical sophism": a trap, a fabrication, or a catch-phrase, with which the books of the scholastic wrestling-ground <note>A metaphor for the schools of logic</note> teem today. And although the danger is not small for many who grow old in the circles and meanders of dialectic—as if at the Siren rocks—nevertheless, there is no limit to that poisonous chattering by which the talents of the youth are swallowed up. The text of Aristotelian wisdom is held in utter contempt <note>original: "susque deque," literally "up and down"</note>. From this it happens that through constant chattering we cease to be men. This is confirmed by Diogenes; when a dialectician had approached him saying, "What I am, you are not," Diogenes nodded; and when the other added, "But I am a man," and he agreed to that too, the man concluded his little catch thus: "Therefore, you are not a man." Diogenes said: "This indeed is false, and if you want it to become true, start the premise with yourself" <note>Diogenes wittily suggests the logic only works if the speaker admits he himself is not a man</note>. A Verse: "Such is the life of men, which two monosyllables overturn" <note>Likely referring to "Yes" and "No"</note>. Seneca the moral philosopher strongly dissuades one from spending a lifetime on such things, along with that most wise Isocrates, regarding philosophy...

D

<vocab>Buttubatta, Chrysippus, Macrobius, Juvenal, Terence, Plautus, Diogenes, Aristotle, Seneca, Isocrates, sophism, ossuary, triflers, dialectic</vocab>

The author cites numerous classical authorities to contrast physical reality and genuine wisdom with the deceptive "traps" of sophistry.</summary>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and dramatic analysis of the play. While the previous page focused on the physical nature of the skull and its anatomical properties, this page shifts to linguistic definitions of "sophistry," "summaries," and "executioners," leading into a dramatic monologue where Buttubatta claims the hollow skull holds absolute power over divine and human law.</meta>

# ACT THREE LXI

<margin>Sophism</margin>
[Philosophy], which makes men good and not merely learned, encourages them. The word **sophism** <note>original Greek: **σόφισμα**, **ατος**; a clever but fallacious argument</note> belongs to the fifth order of Greek imparisyllabic nouns, but we have reduced it to the Latin third declension, just as we shifted their first form from the second declension.
<margin>Summary</margin>
As they say **epitome, epitomes** <note>original Greek: **ἐπιτομὴ ἐπιτομῆς**</note>, we say "epitoma, epitomæ" <note>a summary or abridgment</note>. Columella, in Book 1, circumscribed the whole work with summaries. Therefore, my sweetest teacher, you rightly argue with me:
because <note>original: φ, shorthand for "quia"</note> I wrote "of the summaries" <note>original: "epitomatis"</note> to you, not "of the summary" <note>original: "epitomę"</note>. However, I did this (if it helps to avoid the teacher's rod) led by the authority of your own laws. Indeed, in the *Digests* <note>a compendium of Roman law</note> under the new title "On Appeals," Hermogenianus cites the second book "of the summary of law" <note>original: "iuris epitomatū"</note>, not "of the summaries" <note>original: "epitomarū"</note>. **To mock your own people.** "To mock" means to harm through a game. Virgil says:
<margin>To mock</margin>
"Then various pests mock [him]." He said "you mock your own" in a consistent manner, for Servius <note>a famous commentator on Virgil</note> teaches that "I mock you" <note>original: "illudo te"</note> is a figurative expression. In Virgil's line: "Mock his virtue with proud words," we do not see it as literal; we understand that others have not fully realized they were being played with. **And so, lecherous Executioner.**
<margin>Executioner</margin>
A torturer, a Greenhouse of pain, one worthy of the executioner. "Executioner" <note>Latin: "Carnifex"</note> signifies one who makes "flesh" <note>Latin: "caro"</note> out of a man, or who is worthy of the executioner, or one "stripping the flesh." In Greek, he is a **torturer** <note>original Greek: **βασανιστὴς** (basanistēs)</note>. "Flesh" <note>Latin: "Caro"</note> is so called because <note>original: φ</note> it "lacks soul" <note>Latin: "careat anima"</note> or perhaps from "falling" <note>Latin: "cado"</note>, because <note>original: φ</note> it is now, clearly, without a soul and falls. From this comes the verb "to strip of flesh" <note>Latin: "excarnificare"</note>. Terence says: "where you might strip him of flesh." And
<margin>To strip of flesh</margin>
**Execution-place** <note>Latin: "Carnificina"</note>, a word which denotes a residence of tortures and the office of the executioner.
<margin>Execution-place</margin>
Plautus in *The Captives*. Marcus Cato used "Execution-places" in the plural number to stand for
<margin>na</margin>
supplications, torments, and agonies. **He had twisted.** He had deformed or distended. **Soon, in speaking, a story,** a manufactured little cleverness.

---

<margin>Ah</margin>
**Ah, speak.** They see they have been mocked, and for that reason they ask insistently, so that their comrades do not hide what good or evil the skull <note>Latin: "calvaria"</note> possesses. **Ah, speak.** "Ah" is an interjection sometimes of one weeping, other times of one laughing, and now of one pleading; the Greeks count it among the adverbs. Terence, in *The Eunuch*, introduces a laughing parasite saying, "Ah ah."
<margin>Aha</margin>
In *The Brothers*, Sostrata says while weeping, "Ah, wretched me." Priscian <note>a Roman grammarian</note> thinks a shortening <note>apocope</note> has occurred in that particle. For we read the full form as **aha**, just like **vah** or **puaha**, since a syllable cannot regularly end in "h," which is merely a mark of aspiration.

**BUTTUBATTA.** Do you see this baldness, or this head,
Which, by its nod, its divine power, and its own will,
Reigns, rules, mandates, orders, and commands,
Both those who are unwilling and those who are willing; likewise the courtiers,
And the inhabitants, and the neighbors, and those nearby,
And those most intimate, and those most distant? Against God
And against divine law <note>Latin: "phas"</note>, it turns and overturns all things,
And for God and for divine law it does so again, if it wishes.
**ARISTOTELES.** This empty, this ungraceful, this rotten and vain head?

<summary>This page provides scholarly definitions for terms like "sophism," "summary," and "executioner," drawing on classical authorities like Virgil and Terence. It concludes with a dramatic passage where Buttubatta claims the empty skull possesses a god-like power to command all people and subvert divine law.</summary>
