<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's third act. While the previous page discussed "sophisms" and "summaries," this page transitions from the physical characteristics of the skull (specifically baldness) to the legal and moral definitions of citizenship and the duties of a prince, eventually returning to the character Buttubatta’s dramatic monologue about the corrupting power of the "hollow skull" over the state.</meta>

# ACT THREE LXII

<margin>Courtiers</margin> You see. He points to the skull as if to say, "The truth of your fate does not seem likely to you." But you see this baldness. Aristotle in Book 5 of *On the Generation of Animals*, and Albert the Great <note>original: "Albertus germanorum philosophus illustris"; 13th-century philosopher and scientist</note>, the illustrious German philosopher, in the eleventh book of *On Animals*, have set forth the causes of baldness. Hippocrates writes that neither women nor eunuchs <note>original: "spadones"</note> go bald. This is because everything happens by the nod, guidance, auspices, prayer, desire, and will of the **Courtiers**. These are the purple-clad cultivators of the court and the prince, who make it their business to know the prince's secrets. Suetonius <note>Roman historian</note> records that the cause of a work was revealed by the "inner courtiers." The word **courtier** <note>original: "aulicus"</note> comes from **court** <note>original: "aula"</note>, just as "spearman" <note>original: "hastaticus"</note> comes from "spear" <note>original: "hasta"</note>, and
<margin>Hostile</margin> **hostile** <note>original: "hosticus"</note> comes from **enemy** <note>original: "hostis"</note>, with the middle syllable being pronounced quickly. Ovid writes in Book 4 of the *Fasti*: "The hostile <note>original: "hostica"</note> vintage is vowed to the Tyrrhenian king." Also in *Letters from the Black Sea*: "Smyrna held the man, not the sea and the hostile <note>original: "hostica"</note> land." Likewise in the *Tristia*: "The hostile <note>original: "hostica"</note> crowd burns the innocent cottages." And to the inhabitants: the newcomers, the foreigners, and the native dwellers. Pomponius <note>a Roman jurist</note> says that a **resident** <note>original: "incola"</note> is one who has established his home in another region, whom the Greeks call a <term>paroikon</term> <note>original Greek: **πάροικον**; a resident alien or neighbor</note>. Not only are those who stay in a town considered residents, but also those who have a field within the territory of a town such that they retire to it as if to their own seat. But how a **burgess** <note>original: "municeps"</note>, a **citizen** <note>original: "civis"</note>, a **resident** <note>original: "incola"</note>, a **newcomer** <note>original: "advena"</note>, and a **neighbor** <note>original: "accola"</note> differ is usually disputed by legal experts in Book 50 of the <term>Digests</term> <note>The *Digest* or *Pandects* was a massive compendium of Roman law organized by Emperor Justinian</note> under the title "On Municipalities and Residents." Against God and against divine law <note>original: "phas"</note>. The two primary virtues of a prince are fortitude and justice.
<margin>Fortitude Justice</margin> This is what Homer taught in that verse observed by Alexander the Great, who was a most diligent student of Homer. Thus Homer says: "He was both a good fighter and the best leader." Therefore, there is no justice where a prince is led astray against God and against divine law. To act against God is madness, for what can the weapons of the gods not do? As Statius says in Book 9 <note>of the *Thebaid*</note>: "No joys stand for long when the gods are deceived." And Silius <note>Italicus</note> in Book 15: "A prince ought not to act against divine law, for they will fear more what is permitted to them; we accept it as a saying to them as well: 'It is a glory to do what is fitting, not what is legal.'" For those who ought to be feared ought rather to be loved, and to command just things is the **duty of the prince**. This skull, empty 
<margin>The Prince's duty</margin> of justice, is unrefined, contaminated by vices, and rusty. It is rotten, dissolving, inconstant, slippery. And it is vain, endowed with no virtue, and deceitful.

BUTTUBATTA: Nay, this thing is powerful, pleasing, and praiseworthy,
->That which rules the prince and drags him where it desires,<-
->It creates magistrates, then drives them away again,<-
->It discharges the old senate and the consuls,<-
->It replaces them with a new senate and new consuls,<-
->The record-keepers of the Republic<-
->It burdens or relieves with the rotation of office,<-
->It negotiates all the prince’s business<-
->Without the prince, and gives counsel without the consul.<-

It concludes with Buttubatta’s speech on how a corrupt "inner court" (symbolized by the hollow skull) effectively usurps the power of the state.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the play's third act. While the previous page discussed the physical causes of baldness and the nature of courtiers, this page focuses on the moral and legal implications of corrupt leadership, using the "hollow skull" as a symbol for a ruler who places themselves above the law. ACT THREE LXIII

### Concerning this single little head of his

# Which you see, how illustrious, how outstanding it stands out.

Rather, it is powerful in lies. It is pleasing to a dull-witted leader. Cicero in *On Old Age* says, "Like easily gathers with like" <note>original: "Pares cum paribus facillime congregant"</note>, and as the prophet says, "as the people, so the leader" <note>original: "qualis populus talis præses"</note>. It is praiseworthy to fawning courtiers. Because it rules the prince. Let the law rule the prince. A true man. Virgil says, "They choose laws and magistrates and a holy senate" <note>original: "Iura magistratusque legunt sanctumque senatum"</note>. He mentioned "laws" first, then "magistrates," for where the law does not command the magistrate, but the magistrate stands above the law, destruction is prepared for that city. I am silent about the wicked magistrate. For we are weighed down by a heavy age, where crimes reign and raging impiety seethes, shameful lust, powerful in Venus <note>referring to physical desire</note>. **He creates magistrates.** He arranges courtly honors and distributes them to the inept, which we notice in the word "create." Truly, a magistrate ought to be **elected**, not **created**. Virgil says: "They choose laws and magistrates." It was the Roman custom for magistrates to be elected in assemblies <note>original: "comitiis"</note>, not from a vain head—and to be accepted, not snatched. But oh, seductive power, and the ill-counseling love of the scepter! <note>Statius, *Thebaid*, book 11</note>

<margin>I discharge</margin>
**He discharges.** He dismisses, repels, banishes, releases from the bond, and removes <note>original: "Exauctorat"</note>. To "bind" <note>original: "auctorare"</note> means to oblige someone. Pliny writes of a vine-dresser being "bound" to service. The opposite of that is to "discharge." From this comes the terms for one who is bound and the "binding-oath." Cicero, in book 1 of *On Duties*.

<margin>He binds</margin>
The commentator on Horace takes **bound** <note>original: "auctoratum"</note> to mean "assigned" or "condemned"; for he says those who sell themselves to the gladiatorial games are called "bound gladiators." The "binding" <note>original: "auctoratio"</note> is the sale of gladiators. Properly, however, we call a soldier bound by an oath "bound" <note>original: "auctoratum"</note>, and one released from the oath "discharged" <note>original: "exauctoratum"</note>. Seneca in book 4 of *On Benefits* mentions the "binding-oath." Quintilian also mentions it, and in Livy, a "bound" person is called a traitor. And the constant epithet for servants is "bound," as in Manilius: "And the bound servants under the laws of the third." Suetonius <note>Tranquillus</note> in the *Life of Tiberius Caesar* speaks of "choosing or binding a soldier."

<margin>Senate</margin>
**The old Senate.** The very name "senate" <note>derived from *senex*, meaning old man</note> declares the character and customs of that order. The ancients used to decline this in the second declension <note>e.g., *senati* instead of *senatus*</note>.

<margin>On the fourth declension</margin>
For a double custom was held by the ancient grammarians for nouns retaining *us* before the end, such as **senate**, **commotion**, and **adornment**; the genitive could be "of the senate" <note>original: "senatus" or "senati"</note>, "of the commotion" <note>original: "tumultus" or "tumulti"</note>, and "of the adornment" <note>original: "ornatus" or "ornati"</note>. In this declension, they sometimes ended the dative case in *u*, as in Virgil: "Spare your fear, Cytherea" <note>original: "parce metu"</note>, derived from a genitive ending in *us*. Otherwise, when it ends in *ui*, the syllables of the genitive are divided, as in "of this senate" <note>original: "senat-us"</note> and "to this senate" <note>original: "senat-ui"</note>.

<margin>Substitutes</margin>
**He substitutes.** He places under, puts in another's place, and suffices in the room of the former ones. In military language, **substitutes** <note>original: "succenturatos"</note> denotes those who put themselves forward to fill the ranks for the sake of completing a century <note>a unit of 100 soldiers</note>. Terence in *The Phormio*: "Now you go first; I will be here as a substitute in ambush if you fail at anything."

<margin>Scribes</margin>
**Tax-scribes.** Those who are now called scribes were also once of an honorable order, according to Cicero. The Greeks call a scribe a *grammateus* <note>original: "γραμματέας"</note>.

<margin>Chief Scribe</margin>
And the **chief scribe** <note>original: "archigrammatea"</note> is the prince of scribes, whom the common people call a **chancellor**; but in Latin, thanks to Flavius Vopiscus, who gave this word Roman citizenship <note>i.e., naturalized the term into Latin</note>.

<margin>Vicissitude</margin>
**By alternation.** Through turns, in succession, by change. For which the old Latins pronounced **vicissitude** <note>original: "vicissitatem"</note>, as Accius writes.

<vocab>Cicero, Virgil, Seneca, Quintilian, Livy, Manilius, Suetonius, Terence, scribes, senate, declension, bound, chief scribe, vicissitude</vocab>

### ACT THREE

<meta>catchword: re-</meta>

...the reversal of a prince without a prince. He manages everything alone. Columella writes in his second volume that a record of both leisure and business should exist <note>original: "tam ocii quā negocii rationem extare oportere"</note>; Marcus Portius Cato proves the same in his *Origins*. And Cicero thinks this Catonian saying is magnificent and distinguished: "Plans without a consul" <note>original: "Consilia sine consule"</note>. Romulus wanted the senate to consist of a hundred fathers, not one or two. Jurists circulate a common proverb: "The eyes discern what the eye does not see" <note>original: "oculos cernere quod non uidet oculus"</note>. It stands out; it is prominent. How illustrious! There are three words which we use for both praise and blame: *illustrious*, *distinguished*, and *outstanding* <note>original: "illustris, insignis, & egregius"</note>; the leaders of the Latin language are the authors of these terms.

->**He shuts the prince in the dining room**<-
->**So that there can be no access for anyone without him,**<-
->**He milks the embassies dry**<-
->**Before they can enjoy the presence of the prince.**<-
->**Edicts and prohibitions, laws and oracles,**<-
->**He dictates laws for both the profane and the sacred,**<-
->**Heavenly and earthly things obey**<-
->**This little head, because he raises up and casts down,**<-
->**He impoverishes and enriches, he does what he wants,**<-
->**He commands what he wants, he forbids what he wants, the head of the Head.**<-

<margin>Dining rooms</margin>
"He shuts the prince in the dining room" <note>original: "triclinio"</note>, on a reclining couch. For the ancients reclined on spread couches; hence the term *Triclinium* comes from the three couches on which they used to dine. Horace says: "You might often see four people dining on three couches" <note>original: "Sæpe tribus lectis uideas cœnare quaternos"</note>. The luxury of the ancients even devised rotating dining rooms, as Suetonius mentions in his *Life of Nero*. "So that there can be no access for anyone." The primary gift and principle of kings is clemency. Quintus Curtius, writing about Ptolemy (a leader under Alexander the Great), gathers the things worthy of praise in any king as follows: "The same man was the guardian of the king’s body and the readiest warrior, and greater and more famous in the arts of peace than in those of war; moderate and civil in his dress, <margin>Praise of the prince</margin> especially liberal, and easy of approach; he had assumed nothing of royal pride. Because of these things, it was doubtful whether he was dearer to the king or to the common people." And Seneca says that clemency is a great remedy for fear in a prince, whom the sword indeed protects, but loyalty protects better. And though it is proper for him to be feared, he should nevertheless be loved more. For whatever is forced is burdensome. Truly the most beautiful of all things is to stand out among illustrious men, to look out for the fatherland, to spare the afflicted, to abstain from savage slaughter, to give time to reflection rather than anger, and to give rest to the world and peace to his own age. This is the highest virtue; heaven is sought by this path. 

<margin>Maximilian the Great</margin>
Thus does our Caesar today, Maximilian the Great <note>Refers to Holy Roman Emperor Maximilian I, 1459–1519</note>, always Augustus, whom fortune tossed for a long time by land and sea; through the heavy turns of war, he conquers the enemy, crushes them, and subdues them with an unbloodied hand. With a simple nod, he moderates the reins; the author of peace, chosen as the arbiter of the human race, he rules the world with a sacred appearance...

<summary>This page describes a corrupt advisor who isolates the prince in a private dining room to control all access and legislation. It contrasts this behavior with classical virtues of clemency and accessibility, ultimately praising Emperor Maximilian I as the ideal embodiment of these virtues.</summary>

### ACT THREE

LXV

G v

...acts as the parent of his country <note>original: "patriæ parens"</note>, a name he strives to preserve so that, always undefeated in battle and a conqueror of all nations, he may be sought after by all those whom sacred Rome commends as fellow citizens. And when the final hours are demanded as the day is spent, may he who earned heaven by his virtue—the divine Augustus—ascend to the stars worthy of his majestic chambers; a man in whom the almighty has brought together all praises of lineage, beauty, integrity, and faith.
<margin>Extorted</margin>
The fates willed such a man to be born for their ruler. First milking the embassies dry,
we understand "extortionists" <note>original: "concussores," literally "shakers"</note> to be those who increase the public salaries decreed for them for their own private profit and gain by slandering and shaking down <note>original: "concutientes"</note> the wealth of others. They are guilty under the rebuke of Saint John the Baptist. A title exists in the laws concerning extortion <note>original: "de concussionibus"</note>.

**Edicts.** Donatus says regarding the tenth book of the *Aeneid* that "to issue an edict" <note>original: "edicere"</note> is to command something to be cared for with authority. *In the beginning, he commands the allies to follow the signals.* And elsewhere in book eleven: *You, Volusus, command the Volscian maniples to arm themselves.* Likewise: *I command the allies to then take up arms.*
<margin>Edict</margin>
In the second comedy of Terence, the same author says: "We command" <note>original: "edicimus"</note>, which means we order or pronounce by right. From this come "edicts," which belong to the emperor or the praetor <note>a Roman magistrate</note>. Ulpian, in the fiftieth book of his *Acts*, says: "He who acted while the praetor forbade it is properly said to have acted against the edict." Terence: *Now I command you not to use any force.* Legal experts also want "interdict" to be signified by "edict" sometimes in book thirty-nine of the *Digests*.
<margin>Interdicts</margin>
However, "interdicts" <note>prohibitions</note> are properly those things spoken between two parties. Donatus says in *The Mother-in-Law*: "We say <note>original: "dicimus"</note> what we command to be done. We interdict <note>original: "interdicimus"</note> what we forbid to be done." We also "say" to one class of men, but "interdict" to diverse ones. Thus, the edicts and interdicts of the praetor are properly named. **Oracles:** responses. "Oracle" is used for the temple from which the oracles are given. Virgil, book two of the *Aeneid*: *We send to consult the oracles of Phoebus.*
**For the profane.** For the common
<margin>Diction</margin>
people, whom we call "laity" and "secular." **He dictates.** A frequentative form of "to say" <note>original: "dicere"</note> which belongs to orators. It also means to "dictate" so that another person writing it down may receive it and note it with hand and pen. To "dictate" <note>original: "dictitare"</note> also means to speak and narrate at frequent times and places. From this comes "lessons" <note>original: "dictata," literally things dictated</note>. Persius: *That you should value as nothing a hundred lessons of the curly-haired boys.*
<margin>Lessons</margin>
**They obey.** They comply. Legal experts are accustomed to use the word "to hear" <note>original: "audire"</note> for "to obey." Ulpian, on the receiving of arbiters: *If one of the litigants was not obedient to the word.* **Little head.** A second diminutive form <note>of "caput," head</note>. **He raises up and casts down,** he extols and dejects. **He impoverishes,** he thins out and makes poor. And he **enriches** <note>original: "diviitat"</note> for "makes wealthy," he makes them rich. Nonius says: "The head of the head" <note>original: "capitis caput"</note> means that he possessed the whole presidency.

ARI. Do you preach the truth? I ask you seriously.
BVT. Indeed, the truth. SA. The truth? BVT. Truer than the truest!
SAL. Alas, what a strange fate for a skull <note>original: "caluariae"</note> of that sort.
BVT. Why don't you lick-spittles <note>original: "liguriones"</note> kiss this head?
SAL. Why? BVT. Because for those whose heads have been struck off, this is the head—
And for all infamous petty thieves...

<meta>catchword: And for all</meta>

While the previous page focused on the corruption of the prince's advisors and their "shaking down" of the public, this page examines the specific types of sycophants—gluttons and "soft" followers—who worship the corrupt advisor as a god. ### ACT THREE

### To the feasters, the gluttons, and the chewers <note>original: "Epulonibus, mandonibus, mansuciis"</note>
### To whom as many soft men as there are anywhere,
### And women softer still, now offer supplications,
### Whom, in both sacred and profane houses,
### They worship, adore, and praise as if he were a god.

<margin>Seriously</margin> **Indeed.** <note>original: "Quin"</note> Furthermore, or rather. Terence says: *Nay, I am staying here.* I ask you **seriously.** <note>original: "serio"</note> Buttubatra has reported strange things about the skull; Aristophorus asks whether they are true. Indeed, "by Hercules" and "seriously" are adverbs used for confirmation, according to Donatus <note>Aelius Donatus was a highly influential 4th-century Roman grammarian and teacher of rhetoric</note>. **Of this kind.** An archaism <note>original: "ἀρχαισμὸς," a Greek term for an imitation of an ancient style</note>, that is, an old expression used in place of "of such a sort." **Give a kiss.** <note>original: "Basiate"</note> To kiss thoroughly. Donatus identifies three types of kissing: the *osculum* belongs to duty or etiquette, the *basium* to modest affection, <margin>Lickers</margin> <margin>Dish-lickers</margin> and the *suavium* to lust or carnal love. **Lickers.** <note>original: "Liguriones"</note> Derived from the Greek <note>original: "ἀπὸ τοῦ λιγυροῦ"</note>; for thus in Greek they call something "sweet." From this, the ancients called gluttons "lickers," and also "dish-lickers" <note>original: "catillones," literally those who lick the plates</note>.

<margin>To be punished</margin> **With heads struck.** <note>original: "Capite plexis"</note> Lopped off, cut, or punished by the head. Among legal experts, to be "struck" <note>original: "plecti"</note> means to be punished. For we say someone is "struck by the head" <note>meaning executed</note>. Silius Italicus <note>A Roman consul and epic poet</note> in the second book of the *Punic War*: *May the gods avert this; it is a crime if such things are ever punished.* In the commentary on Terence's *Phormio*, Donatus wishes to derive "blows" <note>original: "plagas"</note> and "quills" <note>original: "plectra," used for striking lyre strings</note> from "striking" <note>original: "plectendo"</note>, which sounds like hitting. Thus the character Geta says: *You will now hear the lawsuits; I will be struck while hanging.* <margin>Eaters</margin> <margin>Chewers</margin> **Gluttons.** <note>original: "Mandonibus"</note> From "chewing" <note>original: "mandendo"</note>, from which also comes "to eat" <note>original: "manducare"</note>, and the "chewing-mask" <note>original: "manducum"</note> used in plays, and "big-eaters" <note>original: "manducones"</note> who are also called "devourers," "gourmands," "gluttons," and "chewers." Marcus Varro mentions this in his Atellan Farces. <margin>Woman</margin> **Of softer women.** A woman is a soft animal. Virgil says:
> A woman is always a fickle and changeable thing.
Calpurnius in his third Eclogue: *O woman, more changeable than the winds.* If nature had given her strength equal to her boldness, this animal would surely be invincible. As Seneca says:
> To woman, nature gave a mind prone to evil and a heart equipped for deceit, but she denied her force so that she would not be invincible; rather, fear or punishment might break her sickly strength.
**Praise.** They laud him.

### They establish games, jokes, holidays, and honors;
### He commands, they approve; he countermands, and they approve the same;
### He condemns and grants pardon; he vexes the good
### And loves the wicked, so that whoever may be a scoundrel
### Proscribed for a crime, he seizes the capital punishment,
### Or bargains with him for the industry of a seditious faction
### With the intent of committing robbery.

**Holidays.** <note>original: "Ferias"</note> Festive days. **And grants pardon.** <note>original: "Et dat gratiam"</note> This is metaphorical, for the granting of grace belongs to God alone. The word "holidays" does not have its proper meaning among the common people, but civil law supports this word and preserves its original significance. Horace, in his fourth volume writing to Julius Antonius, uses a circumlocution: *the joyful days of the citizens and the city,* etc. It used to be...

<meta>This page continues the philological and legal commentary on the play's third act. While the previous page discussed the types of sycophants and the nature of public celebrations, this page moves into a detailed dictionary-style breakdown of Roman legal and social terminology, specifically regarding holidays, legal harassment, and the vocabulary of crime and punishment.</meta>

# ACT THREE

...judge <note>The text likely refers to the "praetor," the Roman official responsible for the administration of justice</note> for the purpose of declaring the law, or on account of some great success, and these were called **commanded holidays**. <note>original: "feriae imperativae"</note> Sometimes an uproar would interrupt here, which is called a **stay of justice** <note>original: "iustitium," a period when legal proceedings were suspended, often during a time of public mourning or crisis</note> or legal vacation. There were also **fixed holidays**, as well as **appointed holidays**, and the **market days** of the villages and country folk. From these come both the **rural festivals** <note>original: "paganicae"</note> and the **sowing festivals**, as well as those for conducting trade. To these were added public holidays, called in Greek **truce** <note>original: "ἐκεχειρία" (ekecheiria), literally a "staying of hands"</note>, which is to say, an armistice. Aulus Gellius, in his first book, notes that during these times it was a sin to attack an enemy in war, for which reason **battle-days** <note>original: "praelíares díes"</note> were established. There are also **private holidays** for one's own sacred rites, such as birthdays. And there are **festive holidays** and **non-festive** ones, such as the **Saturnalia**. <margin>Death-cleansing</margin> There are also **death-cleansing holidays** <note>original: "feriae denicales"</note> when a family was purified because of a dead man; for the Greeks call the dead *nekyon*. <note>original: "νέκυον"</note> And there are **preliminary holidays**, according to Aulus Gellius in his fourth book. From holidays we derive the word **to keep holiday**, <note>original: "feriatum"</note> which means something festive and joyful, though sometimes it refers to being sluggish or idle. You may seek more on holidays in Marcus Varro’s *On Origins* and Macrobius’s first book of the *Saturnalia*. Concerning a busy person, we say **proverbially** <note>original: "παροιμιακῶς" (paroimiakos)</note> that "a holiday never happened for him, even when he was a boy." Seneca mentions this in his book *On the Brevity of Life*. Diomedes thinks the word for "holidays" <note>original: "feriae"</note> is always used in the feminine plural.

<margin>I harass</margin> **He harasses the good.** "To harass" <note>original: "vexare"</note> is a heavy and unbearable word for enduring an insult. Aulus Gellius notes this, and Cicero in his first book of accusations against Gaius Verres says: *who now, all having been pillaged and harassed, have often come to me publicly.* It is used like a proverb: **his own fraud harasses each man**, which is said regarding one's conscience. <margin>Let him be</margin> **Let him be a gallows-bird**: <note>original: "Furcífer fuat"</note> "let him be" <note>original: "fuat"</note> is "he shall have been," an old defective verb of the future tense. Servius, in his tenth commentary, says that grammarians want *fuere* to be the verb "to be." From this comes **leaky** <note>original: "futilis"</note>, as well as **to argue** <note>original: "futare"</note> and **to refute** <note>original: "confutare"</note>. <margin>I argue</margin> But "to argue," which elsewhere means to prove wrong, is used by Cato to mean **to have been frequently**. And we find it used for "let me be" or "let me become" on the authority of Marcellus.

<margin>I proscribe</margin> **Proscribed for a crime.** "To proscribe" <note>original: "proscribere"</note> means to offer for sale and to make public through writing. Cicero, in the third book of *Duties*, says: *Claudius proscribed the island.* However, in the case of living things, it means "to lead forth." Hence we say that slaves for sale are **led forth**. Likewise, "to proscribe" is to banish, as in Horace: *The pus and poison of the proscribed King Rupilius.* Cicero says in his speech against Verres: *They do not only proscribe that which they are forbidden to.* Juvenal says: <margin>Capital</margin> *If three disciples should speak against the list of Sulla.* **Capital** <note>original: "Capital"</note> refers to the **head**, <note>original: "caput"</note> which has cast the danger of one's life <note>original: "periculum capitis," a legal term for a trial where one's status or life is at stake</note> upon many. Plautus says in the *Menaechmi*: *Even if it were a capital matter.* "Capital" was said by the ancients to be a danger to the head; it also signifies a **headband** <note>original: "fasciola"</note> with which the ancients tied the hair on the head.

<margin>Faction</margin> **Factious.** Of a **faction**. <note>original: "factio"</note> "Faction" was once an honorable word, but now it signifies **sedition**. Tertullian thinks the name "factious" should be applied to those who conspire in hatred of the good and the honest. Sallust writes that among the good there is friendship, but among the wicked there is **faction**. Of a factious faction: that is, one that is senseless, hateful, full of traps and arms, seditious, and riotous. <margin>Factious</margin> Apuleius calls a faction "insane." And Aulus Gellius called a tongue "excessively factious" with seditions and overflowing with violence. <margin>Little bandit</margin> **With the spirit of a little bandit.** "To commit banditry" <note>original: "latrocinari"</note> is a word for highwaymen that pertains to the robberies and plundering of bandits, of which multiple mentions are made in the books of Roman law; but the term "little banditry" <note>original: "latruncularí"</note> is also used by some. From this we call a man a **bandit-judge** <note>original: "latrunculatorem"</note> who judges concerning bandits, as in the fifth book of Ulpian’s *Digests*. **He bargains.** He agrees. He comes together by a pact.

<summary>This page serves as a philological glossary for Act Three, defining Roman legal terms for holidays (feriae), the evolution of the word "faction" from neutral to negative, and the legal implications of "proscription" and "capital" punishment.</summary>
<keywords>Roman law, feriae, iustitium, vexare, proscription, capital punishment, factio, latrocinium, etymology, Cicero, Aulus Gellius</keywords>

# ACT THREE

->Come hither, you men who are pimps<-
->This head never loved modest spouses<-
->Come hither, you adulterous women<-
->That skull delights in prostitutes<-
->Let the seller of virgins also come hither<-
->This head has often deflowered virgins<-

<margin>Adultery and Debauchery</margin>
**Adulterous women.** The Julia Law <note>original: "lex iulia"; referring to the *Lex Julia de Adulteriis Coercendis* enacted by Emperor Augustus to regulate Roman morality</note> uses the terms **adultery** and **debauchery** <note>original: "stuprum"</note> interchangeably, but strictly speaking, **adultery** refers to a crime committed with a married woman. **Debauchery** is committed against a virgin. The legal expert <note>original: "iurisconsultus"</note> writes that the punishment and accusation of unchaste matrons pertains primarily to the father and the husband. The laws intend for the word **adultery** <note>original: "adulterium"</note> to be used as if it were an "approach to another's bed." <note>This is a common, though perhaps folk-etymological, Roman derivation: *ad* (to) + *alter* (another).</note> Furthermore, a father has the right to kill an adulterer found with his daughter, provided he kills both parties. <note>original: "l. patri. ff. eodem"; a citation to the *Digest* of Roman law</note> A husband who keeps a wife whom he has discovered in adultery is to be punished as a **pimp**. But let us grant these points to the legal experts, whose wisdom is the true philosophy. They themselves are also true philosophers, even if they do not cast away money, as **Accursius** <note>A famous 13th-century Italian jurist</note> says. **Prostitutes** are common harlots who grant power over their own bodies. A **seller of virgins** is one who, through force and against her will, procures a girl for a debaucher. **Deflowered** <note>original: "devirginavit"</note> means corrupted or violated; hence we call girls "deflowered" who, having been picked like a flower and ravished, have cast off their virginity. There is another meaning of that word when we say a youth is "deflowered," for then we mean to signify that he has moved out of the age of the **ephebe**. <note>original: "ephoebis"; a Greek term for a male youth transitioning into manhood</note>
<margin>Legal experts and Philosophers</margin>
<margin>I deflower</margin>

**ARI.** Where is he from, then? **BU.** From Arabia. **AR.** What is his name?
**BUT.** Sergius—or are you ignorant of that Sergius?
**LIX.** The grammarian? The same one who teaches Latin thoroughly?
**BUT.** Not at all, but that apostate and Persian [man]
Who is learned in neither Latin nor Greek,
A hireling of the wicked leader Moniosthenes.

<margin>Arabia</margin>
**From Arabia.** Although it is called **Arabia Felix** <note>original: "eudæmon" (Greek) / "fœlix" (Latin), meaning "Happy" or "Fortunate" Arabia</note> by our people and the Greeks because of the many fragrant things pertaining to ointments which are brought to us from there, the Christian religion produces "monsters" from there when it wishes to signify something portentous, because of the heretical depravity and the wonders with which that land teems. **Sergius**, about whom we spoke above. <note>In medieval tradition, "Sergius" was often the name of a heretical monk who was claimed to have influenced the Prophet Muhammad.</note> **The Grammarian.** The dull and troublesome opinion of the common people, which is the corruption of all Latinity, tramples the name of "grammarian" underfoot, relegating it to the common school as if to a mill-house. It is unmindful of Greek heritage and has forgotten the force and virtue of this Greek word. For what else does **grammarian** mean but a **man of letters**? <note>original: "literatum"</note> Among the ancients, it was the duty of such men to examine, explain, compare, and judge every kind of writing: poets, historians, orators, philosophers, doctors, and legal experts; for which reason...
<margin>The duty of the Grammarian</margin>

# ACT THREE 69

### HEADER: ACT THREE 69

**Critics** <note>original: "Critici"</note>, that is, judges, they are called; by which name you, my sweetest and most distinguished teacher, have often called me in your honeyed letters smelling of ambrosia and nard—but enough on these matters for now. For it is no longer our role to punish the laziness of the common people. **Diogenes** the philosopher (if Lactantius allows him the name of philosopher) also toothily seized upon the **grammarians** <note>original: "grammaticos"</note>, just as he spared no one. For he used to say that grammarians search for the misfortunes of Ulysses but remain ignorant of their own. <margin>Apostate</margin> But as for that **apostate** <note>original: "apostatam"</note>—a defector, a retracter, a rebel, a discarder of his professed religion (in Greek *apostates*)—Augustine interprets an "apostate" as one going backward and an enemy of God, who is not to be tolerated among any people (Canon Law 26.q.ult. *Non observetis*). Nor was he learned in Latin. Pompeius says the term "to speak Latin" <note>original: "latine loqui"</note> comes from the region of Latium, a speech that has become so corrupted that hardly any part of it remains pure. <margin>Unlearned</margin> Furthermore, Aristotle compared the **unlearned** <note>original: "indoctum"</note> to the dead, according to Laertius. Wherefore the most wise **Cato** in his fourth book—a little book which the learned Planudes graced with a Greek translation—says: "Instruct your mind with precepts," etc., which he translated into Greek in this manner: "Regulate your mind with teachings and never cease, for a life without instruction is a formless image of death." <margin>Hired harlots</margin> **Meritorious** <note>original: "meritorium"</note> means hired for a price. From this we get "hired vehicles" and "hired dining rooms" mentioned in Juvenal. For those "meritorious" rooms allow sleep, and **hired harlots** <note>original: "meritoria scorta"</note> are those who trade and sell their modesty for a price.

> **59. How then was he a monk? BU: He was a babbler,**
> **And sarcastic, and a man of many words, a sophisticated man.**
> **He said nothing, yet he spoke, a bold little fellow,**
> **Rash, cunning, insolent, talkative.**
> **But when religion could no longer bear**
> **His madness, he was corrected**
> **By the brothers and fathers according to the rule,**
> **As was fitting; which Sergius did not take well.**
> **Seeking a way to leave the monastery,**
> **He broke out, escaped, and fled backward as a runaway,**
> **Searching for someone who might be a match for his own character.**

How then was he a monk, if he was learned in neither Greek nor Latin? A **babbler** <note>original: "blatero"</note> is a talkative chatterbox. **Sarcastic** <note>original: "dicax"</note> means a mocker. Regarding the **man of many words** <note>original: "multiloquus"</note>, there exist verses of Hesiod, the most prudent of poets, against an immoderate, wandering, and rash tongue, recited by Aulus Gellius in the first volume, chapter 15. There he also shows how troublesome a vice is futile and empty talkativeness. <margin>Sophist</margin> A **sophisticated man** <note>original: "vir sophisticus"</note>: a mocker, a deceiver, a pretender. Boethius interprets **sophistry** <note>original: "sophisticam"</note> as the art of mockery. Alcinous the Platonic philosopher separates the sophist from the philosopher...

The text explores the etymology of "meritorious" and "critic" to reinforce the moral failings of the subject.</summary>

# ACT THREE 70

<margin>I say</margin>
In this way, I say that a **sophist** <note>original: "sophista"</note>—someone who uses clever but fallacious arguments—would rather be thought of as honest and good than actually be so. He has said nothing of value. To have spoken "honestly and eloquently" is the specific trait of **Orators**. From this we get "fluent diction" and "oratorical speech." **Reckless** <note>original: "Audaculus"</note> describes one with a rash, bold, and insolent tongue. **Cunning** <note>original: "Versutus"</note> refers to one who is clever in the service of malice. **Insolent** means inept or troublesome. A **Chatterbox** <note>original: "Loquax"</note> is a foolish, empty babbler; it usually happens that in one who possesses the greatest amount of talkativeness, you will find the least amount of wisdom, as **Sallust** <note>Roman historian known for his biting political commentary</note> writes. **Cato** <note>Cato the Elder, known for his strict morality</note> encourages us to restrain the tongue. Likewise, **Planudes** <note>Maximus Planudes, a 14th-century Greek monk and scholar</note> wrote: <note>original Greek: "πρώτιστην ἀρετῶν ὄιμαι τὸ γλῶσσαν ἐπέχειν"</note> "I consider it the first of virtues to restrain the tongue."

<margin>Religion</margin>
Wait! He explains how he fell into **apostasy** <note>the abandonment of a religious profession</note>. **Religion** <note>original: "Religio"</note>: the etymology of this word is varied, as recorded by the very pure writer **Aulus Gellius** in his fourth book, chapter nine. **Marcus Cicero** seems to take it to mean "anxious observation" in his book *On the Perfect Orator*. **Madness** <note>original: "Amentiam"</note> refers to a raving and senseless vanity.

<margin>Monastery</margin>
**Monastery** <note>original: "Monasterio"</note>: a place of solitude; the Greek word is *monasterion* <note>μοναστήριον</note> and *monachos* <note>μοναχὸς</note> means "solitary."

<margin>Escaped</margin>
He broke out, he escaped. "Broke out" <note>erupit</note> is less than "escaped" <note>evasit</note>, for we "escape" through steep and broken paths. **He drew back**: he failed the religious rule of living. Therefore, he is an **apostate** <note>original: "apostata"</note> according to the interpretation of **Saint Augustine**. **Fleeing** and **fugitive**. As the proverb says: "Seeking his match." It is said that a bad thing is best suited to a bad person because of their shared nature.

## To be brief: the thief found his own kind,
## Yet one more powerful than himself, so he might
## Look to him as the god **Tutanus** <note>an ancient Roman protective deity</note> bringing aid,
## A man named **Mahomet**, of the **Cyrenian** race,
## Whom his homeland calls **Monosthenes** <note>Greek for "single-strength" or "steadfast"</note> in Greek.
## Then he threw off his cloak and scapulars,
## And even the cowl and every adornment of his order,
## His constancy, his uprightness, and his whole faith.
## And to explain many things in a brief speech:
## He who had been a monk was made an apostate,
## The greatest persecutor of his own brothers;
## And as often as he was defeated and cast into chains,
## So often did Mahomet free him when he was captured,
## Either by flattering or by threatening the brothers.

<margin>Tutanus</margin>
<margin>Tutilina</margin>
<margin>Cyrene</margin>
"To be brief": that is, concerning the fugitive apostate. "To protect" <note>original: "tui"</note> comes from the infinitive *tuor*. "Like the god Tutanus": a protector god, from the act of protecting <note>tutando</note>. This is mentioned by **Varro** <note>a prolific Roman scholar</note> in his work *Hercules*. From this also comes the goddess **Tutilina** <note>a Roman goddess who protected the grain</note>. "The Cyrenian race": from **Cyrene**, a city in Africa founded by Battus the Laconian. From there comes the region of Cyrenaica, also called **Pentapolis**, once made famous by the oracle of **Jupiter of the Sands** <note>Jupiter Ammon</note>. Mentioned by **Lucan** and Quintus Curtius.

# ACT THREE 71

<margin>Curtius, book 4</margin>
**A small cloak** <note>original: "Amiculum"</note>. An attachment for the shoulders; also called **scapulars**. By using the word "amiculum," he indicates what he wants us to understand about the character’s dress. **Adorned**: Poets are permitted to add or remove a letter or syllable so that a verse might stand, as in **Virgil**’s <note>The Roman epic poet</note> "And the very hard [eduramque] pear." **Constancy**: This signifies that along with his garments, he had made progress in modesty, integrity, and the other benefits of religious life. **Whole faith**: A faithful integrity, than which nothing more becomes religion. **Apostate**: The earth nourishes no kind of man more savage than this.

<margin>How often</margin>
**How often** <note>original: "Quoties"</note>. **Caper** <note>Flavius Caper, a 2nd-century Latin grammarian</note>, that most skillful investigator of antiquity, teaches that "quoties" and "toties" [so often] ought to be pronounced and written without the "n," just like "decies millies" [ten thousand times]. **Priscian** <note>A late Roman grammarian</note>, however, inserts the "n" in his fifteenth book. **Conquered**: Caught or captured. **The imprisoned apostate**: Muhammad. The **guardian god** <note>original: "deus Tutanus"</note> of the apostate. **Either by flattery or by threats**: the two-fold path of proceeding.

The same Muhammad first held **Damascus**,
Where he ruled both Syrians and Arabs.
This head swelled with a heavy pride,
Sitting upon a gentle horse and a swift steed
With four fellow-equestrian **dromons** <note>swift racing horses; original: "dromonibus"</note>.
If he decided to pray before the commoners
Or the patricians, chewing on **foot-and-a-half words** <note>meaning overly long, pompous words; original: "sesquipedalia uerba"</note>,
Most elegantly dressed in a **pontifical headband**,
He appeared in the **trabea** <note>a ceremonial robe of state</note> and augural dress.

<margin>A smooth-pacer</margin>
<margin>Ponies</margin>
<margin>Steed</margin>
<margin>Dromons</margin>
**Damascus first**: The Syrians and Arabs, the land made subject to his power. Damascus is a city in Syria, as noted in **Curtius** book 4, **Strabo** book 16, and that **Josephus** (or Josippus) who is called the "Greek Livy" by **Jerome**, in his second book of *Antiquities*. **Arabs** <note>original: "Arrabas"</note>: The author added a letter relying on poetic license to lengthen the syllable for the sake of the verse. **Swelled**: Made inflated and tumid. **Heavy**: Most troublesome by far to his subjects. **On a gentle horse**: This signifies he was carried by a **smooth-pacer** <note>original: "gradario"</note>, a horse moving with a soft gait and a gentle trot; not a skinny or ill-kept horse, but one that is fat, sleek, gentle, and in peak condition. It should be known, however, that **ponies** <note>original: "mannos"</note> are either small horses or carriage horses, as **Pliny** <note>the Younger</note> mentions in his letters. **Swift**: Fast. **Steed** <note>original: "caballo"</note>: A horse. Indeed, the Greeks call it a "satyric horse," and among us it is a common or rustic word. **Dromons**: Fast, swift racing horses, from the Greek word *dromos*, meaning a race. Hence *Dromikos* means "pertaining to running." **Foot-and-a-half words**: Ample, great, and many-syllabled. **Horace** <note>Roman satirist</note> says: "And foot-and-a-half words." **Chewing** <note>original: "ruminans"</note>: Murmuring, muttering, recalling and repeating. **Pontifical headband**: A band, a linen sacrificial head-covering. **Clad in the trabea**: Girded and dressed in a *trabea*. **Pliny** <note>the Elder</note> understood that kings used the *trabea*. **Virgil** grants it to augurs in the seventh book of the *Aeneid*: "He sat girded in a short trabea." **Persius** <note>Roman satirist</note> also mentions it: "Or because you salute clad in the trabea."

The author provides deep philological roots for the terms used, citing numerous classical Roman and Greek authorities to explain the symbolism of his "foot-and-a-half words" and ceremonial robes.</summary>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>ACT THREE</header>

## But the day would fail me, though newly dawned,
## If I wished to set forth his deeds with perfect precision. <note>original: "ad unguem"; literally "to the fingernail"</note>
## With what pride and force he acted from the start,
## And with what threats then, now, and constantly;
## For he followed as apostates are wont to do.

**But the day would fail me.** <note>original: "Sed me dies deficiet"</note> It will leave, or abandon. **Cicero** <note>The famous Roman orator</note> says, "letters would fail me," and in his work *Brutus* concerning the perfect orator: "If by chance the force of genius should fail someone."

<margin>To the fingernail</margin>
<margin>Pride</margin>
<margin>Constantly</margin>
<margin>Enduring</margin>
**Newly dawned** <note>original: "Recens ortus"</note>. From this comes the opinion of some that a "new sun" rises every day. **Virgil** <note>The Roman epic poet</note> says: "Or when the morning star bedews the lands with the new sun." **With perfect precision** <note>original: "Ad unguem"</note>. Exactly, most perfectly. This is a metaphor taken from stonemasons, who test the joints of stones with their fingernails. **Persius** <note>A Roman satirist</note> says: "as when a joint pours its harshness over the smooth nail." **Horace** <note>The Roman lyric poet</note> mentions a "man made to the fingernail" <note>meaning a man of perfect character</note>. **Virgil** in the second book of the *Georgics* says: "No less all [should be] to the nail." This is said of those things which we signify to be finished and absolute. **With what pride** <note>original: "Quo fastu"</note>; swelling, arrogance, just as he deigned to notice no one. **Pride** [fastus] is declined in the fourth declension in this sense; we take **calendars** [fastos] of the second declension for a book in which the calculation of days is kept. **Servius** <note>A 4th-century commentator on Virgil</note> relies on the authority of **Ovid** in his fourth commentary, which is great. **Quintilian** <note>A Roman educator</note> considers it honorable to err with great authors. For **Aulus Gellius** <note>A 2nd-century author</note> thinks grammatical definitions should not be looked at everywhere, calling them "stinking and half-rotten." From this we receive the principle that in grammar, the authority of the highest men especially must be held as reason, and we must flee to it as to an anchor. **And constantly** <note>original: "Et perpetim"</note>. **Enduring** [Perpes, perpetis]. **Lucretius** <note>An Epicurean poet</note> uses this in more than one place. It is a noun obtaining the force of an adverb, from which the adverb "constantly" [perpetim] is formed, which signifies "continually." But from "enduring" [perpes] is also drawn the word for "perpetual" [perpetuus] regarding time. I once asked a common, trivial schoolmaster about this name, whether it could be used in comparison <note>i.e., whether one thing can be "more perpetual" than another</note>; by this inquiry, I caught that he had not read **Priscian** <note>A famous Latin grammarian</note>. If he had even greeted him from the threshold <note>meaning, had even a basic acquaintance with his work</note>, he would have noticed that there was no knot in this bulrush <note>an idiom meaning there was no difficulty or problem here</note>. But we have long complained that the same thing happens to Priscian, the prince of grammarians, as happens to the texts of **Aristotle**; thus it happens that our **pseudo-philosophers** <note>original: "philosophastri"</note> babble out ghosts and portents in the Latin language. **As they are wont**; for an **apostate** who trades his shame for a [religious] garment—if he is a perjurer toward God, what can we hope he will do among men?

## SAL. By Pollux, and by the Temple of Pollux, and Hercules, and the Dog!
## You now explain things about this man more memorably
## Than anything I have ever heard about any matter.

<margin>By Pollux</margin>
**By Pollux** <note>original: "Per pol"</note>. Referring to Pollux. **Terence** <note>A Roman playwright</note> says: "Not by Pollux" [Haud pol]. From this comes **by the Temple of Pollux** [ædepol], that is, through the temple of Pollux. These are particles suited for formulas of swearing. **And Hercules and the Dog.** A festive joke of a jester; others swear by Hercules, but this one adds the Dog <note>This may be a reference to the "Socratic oath" or simply a character-specific absurdity</note>.

<meta>catchword: Good [Bona]</meta>

<summary>This page provides a philological commentary on the dialogue of Act Three, explaining idioms like "ad unguem" (to the fingernail/perfectly) and the grammatical roots of terms for pride and constancy. It features a sharp critique of unlearned teachers (philosophasters) who do not study classical authorities like Priscian or Aristotle.</summary>

<keywords>philology, grammar, Cicero, Virgil, Horace, Priscian, apostasy, oaths, Pollux, metaphors, stonemasonry, pride</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and moral commentary on the third act of the play. While the previous page discussed the apostate’s arrogance and his departure from Roman authority, this page focuses on his specific actions against his own religious order and the stripping of ecclesiastical revenues (prebends), using a vast array of classical and biblical citations to explain the terminology and the gravity of his "war" on the clergy.</meta>

<page-num>LXXIII</page-num>
<header>ACT THREE 73</header>

**BUTTONIUS.** Not yet everything; first of all, with formal declaration, he announced a horrific war against his own little brothers <note>original: "fraterculis"; a diminutive term for fellow friars or monks</note>, relying on Moniosthenes as his leader. Accordingly, he took away the annual sustenance from the holy priests (the common people are accustomed to call these "holy prebends" <note>a stipend or portion of revenue granted to a member of a cathedral or collegiate church</note>). That apostate, under the name of that man Muhammad, announced to these good men, the holy priests, that they must surrender their modest prebends and die a death by fasting. In this way he treats all honest men; yet for gluttons and wastrels, this leader is a father and protector above all others.

**With formal declaration.** <note>original: "Foecialiter"</note> Publicly, solemnly, with military preparation, and through the *fetials*. These were priests who declared war and oversaw treaties and public trust between peoples. **Numa Pompilius** <note>The legendary second king of Rome</note> established this type of priesthood; the Greeks call them "judges of peace" <note>original: "ειρηνοδίκαι" (eirenodikai)</note>. Regarding these, **Dionysius of Halicarnassus** and **Plutarch** in his *Roman Questions* <note>original: "proplematis"</note> write, as do **Marcus Varro** and **Titus Livy**, who records the specific formula for a treaty. It is a wonder concerning the apostate monk who solemnly declared a horrific war against his own brothers—and a "horrible" one at that—contrary to what is read in the canonical decrees: that the servants of God ought not to litigate, but should imitate Him who bore sinners on His back.

**Relying on Moniosthenes as his leader.** Utilizing the secular arm <note>civil or military power used by the church to enforce its will</note>, while holding the judgment of the most blessed Pope in contempt.

**Prebends.** <note>original: "Præbendas"</note> Canonries. The common people call them prebends. Those skilled in pontifical law sometimes take "benefices" and "prebends" to mean the same thing, though elsewhere they distinguish between them.

**He announced to the good men.** He dismissed them in the name of his leader and declared their oaths void. To "renounce" <note>original: "Renunciare"</note>, as **Asconius Pedianus** says in his commentaries on the *Verrine Orations*, is to declare something void or invalid.

**To die a death by fasting.** By hunger or starvation. A "way of death" by which one dies of starvation, as **Apuleius** in the eighth book of the *Metamorphoses* suggests, seems to him peaceful and quiet; yet the Greeks sing otherwise, saying "the most piteous thing is to die of hunger" <note>original: "οἴκτιστον λιμῶ θανέειν"</note>. **Jeremiah** also in his *Lamentations* says: "It is better to be killed by the sword than to waste away with hunger." And in **Ezekiel** we read that the "arrows of hunger" are deadly. By **Pliny the Elder**, death by hunger is called "dreadful." For those who intend to die by starvation, they usually meet death on the seventh day. Pliny, however, is the authority that some have lasted beyond the eleventh. **Silius Italicus**, the writer of the *Punic Wars*, perished by hunger.

**But.** <note>original: "Sed"</note> Conversely, from the opposite side, on the contrary. "But" always signifies a contrary thought, and it transfers the meaning to a different matter than the one being discussed. **Terence** <note>The Roman playwright</note> says: "Excellent, but follow me inside." He announces good things to the parasites <note>those who lived by flattering the wealthy to get a place at their table</note>, words pleasing to hear, whereby he invites them to a kiss.

<sig>H</sig>

<summary>This page describes the apostate's (Muhammad's) systematic dismantling of the financial support for the clergy, portraying his actions as a formal declaration of war against his own religious brothers. The commentator uses Roman legal and religious history, along with biblical and classical literature, to frame this act as a violation of both sacred law and natural mercy.</summary>
<keywords>prebends, fetials, Moniosthenes, Muhammad, apostasy, starvation, canon law, Numa Pompilius, Pliny, Jeremiah</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the third act, moving from the apostate's declaration of "war" on the clergy to a dramatic scene where the characters mockingly salute a skull (representing the apostate's hollow authority). The commentary continues to use classical philology to explain the derogatory terms used for the apostate's followers.</meta>

<page-num>LXXIIII</page-num>
<header>ACT THREE</header>

They kneel down, one by one, and salute the skull <note>original: "caluam"; likely referring to a bald head or a skull used as a prop</note> with kisses. Soon, however, the tone changes, and the light speech leads the common crowd to cursing and reviling.

> **ARISTIPPUS.** O lovely head! **BUTTONIUS.** Will you stop with the eyes?
> **ARISTIPPUS.** I kiss it because I am a pimp; hail, head!
> O head of all wantonness, smooth head!
> Hollow head without spirit, without light!
> Hail, head—a refuge for gluttons,
> For wastrels, for gluttons, for good-for-nothings,
> And the sweet glory of buffoons! Hail, head!

<margin>Gluttons
Good-for-nothings
Buffoon
Deceivers</margin>
**Gluttons.** <note>original: "Glutones"</note> **Festus** <note>Sextus Pompeius Festus, a 2nd-century Roman grammarian</note> says they are named from gluttony and engorgement. **Good-for-nothings.** <note>original: "Nihiliuiris"</note> Frivolous, futile, and triflers; an imitation of the playwright **Plautus**, who spoke of a "man of nothing" in his play *The Bacchides*. And in *The Haunted House* <note>original: "Mostellaria"</note> where he says, "The man who is fearful in uncertain matters is not worth a nutshell." **Buffoons.** <note>original: "Scurrarum"</note> Biting jesters, whom the Greeks call *altar-lickers* <note>original: "τοὺς βωμολόχους" (bomolochous); beggars or jesters who hung around sacrifices to snatch food</note>, and whom our people call *scurrae* from the word for "following." From this comes "scurrility" and "scurrilous jokes," as well as "scurrilous wit," about which **Cicero** discusses at great length in the second book of his *Dialogues*. The ancients called "deceivers" <note>original: "planos"</note> those who were, as it were, vagabonds and seducers. **Apuleius** employs this sense in the tenth book of the *Metamorphoses*.

> **BUTTONIUS.** But so that I may openly declare the truth to each man:
> This empty head that you see so clearly
> Is such that it makes its own purses full;
> It never gives, but always receives. It does not ask
> Where the money comes from, only if there is someone
> Who gives, and gives generously.

**But so that.** He begins, through a contrary persuasion, to move them toward cursing and detesting the skull. **Truth.** He makes them attentive, as if he had only been reciting scurrilous jokes until now. **Clearly.** <note>original: "Planiter"</note> Plainly. **Its own purses.** <note>original: "Loculos suos"</note> A money bag or pouch. These are all satirical terms. **Juvenal** <note>A famous Roman satirical poet</note> says: "For they do not go with purses attending them to the hazard of the gaming table." And elsewhere: "Friends buried in the purses which the dole-basket <note>original: "sportula"; a gift of food or money given by a patron to a client</note> has made." **Never gives.** By this word, the anger of the players <note>original: "ludionibus"</note> can be provoked; we perceive, therefore, that their brows are already beginning to wrinkle <note>original: "caperare"</note>, that is, to contract and fold. **But receives.** They find this even more undignified. **And always.** Not once, twice, or thrice, but always. **Nor asks where it comes from.** As if to say, he takes from the wicked and the righteous alike; therefore, beware, actors <note>original: "histriones"</note>.

> But I know you as greedy and famished thieves;
> Do not play anywhere from which nothing
> Can be given for you to enjoy.
> For then he also loves no one but an apostate.

<vocab>skull, pimp, gluttons, good-for-nothings, buffoon, deceivers, purse, dole-basket, actors, apostate, altar-lickers</vocab>

The passage serves to characterize the "apostate" as a parasitic figure who drains the wealth of others to feed his band of low-life followers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological and dramatic analysis of the third act. Following the previous page's mockery of the "hollow head," the text now identifies the apostate’s followers with Eastern nations and the "Alkoran," while providing scholarly commentary on the terms used, drawing from classical authors like Virgil, Cicero, and Silius Italicus.</meta>

<page-num>LXXV</page-num>
<header>ACT THREE</header>

Someone like himself, or who is a follower of Muhammad <note>original: "Mahometista"</note>
By profession, or a Phrygian or Persian by race,
Or a Mede, or from Damascus, or a Syrian, or an Arab,
For over those he dominates, rules, governs, and falls upon.
For these he made the Alkoran <note>the Quran</note> and brought the law
Which the Saracens use and the fierce Turks.
Thus in word and deed he wishes all to become
Equal to apostates, so that he may cover his own crime.

You, however, [call them] voracious and famished. "Parasites" is composed of "voracious" from hunger and eating. Donatus <note>Aelius Donatus, a 4th-century Roman grammarian</note> thinks the name "Famished" <note>original: "Famelici"</note> is as if it were "hunger-speakers" <note>original: "famedici"</note>. "It is able" <note>original: "potestur"</note>
<margin>Electrum</margin>
can be said in the ancient manner, as Virgil says: *Whatever can be done with iron or liquid electrum* <note>a natural alloy of gold and silver; quote from Aeneid 8.402</note>. Regarding this, it does not escape me what Landino <note>Cristoforo Landino, a Renaissance commentator on Virgil</note> brings up; nevertheless, I am not a little surprised that Virgil placed it with the same quantity elsewhere, where he directs a eulogy to Messala; I would have thought the poet measured the first syllable of *electrum* with the same shortening. For the Greeks are accustomed to put *eta* [long e] instead of *epsilon* [short e]. Silius <note>Silius Italicus, Roman epic poet</note> in Book I lengthens it: *The veins grow pale from the twin seed of electrum.* Or [someone who is]
<margin>Phrygians</margin>
a Phrygian by race. A barbarian, and lacking in mental skill. Concerning the Phrygians, a proverb
<margin>He falls upon</margin>
is used by Marcus Cicero in his letters: *they are wise too late.* "He falls upon" <note>original: "Inuolat"</note>
<margin>Alkoran</margin>
implies he works his way in or seizes, from *vola*, the inner part of the hand. "For these he made the Alkoran." Thus they name the volume of the Muhammadan law, which for the most part contains the Nestorian poison <note>a reference to the heresy of Nestorius, which the author claims influenced Islamic thought</note>. "And the fierce Turks." Two nations
<margin>Saracens</margin>
especially infest the Christian faith: the Turks and the Saracens.

**ARI.** Alas, I regret that I kissed this head,
For I should have known it is hollow and has nothing,
Whereby it can provide anything to its companions.
**SAL.** And I too regret with equal sorrow,
And I take this poorly indeed concerning Buttubatta,
Who gave us good hope of a skull <note>original: "caluariæ"</note>.
**LIX.** I am coming to my senses with a similar regret,
Wherefore a retraction <note>original: "palinodia"</note> must now be sung by me:
May the gods destroy you, you hollow and empty head,
And may lightning and thunder from above burn you,
And may whatever evil is in the underworld turn you to dust.

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on the third act of the play. While the previous page discussed the "hollow head" of the apostate and his followers, this page transitions into the psychological state of regret and the classical definitions of hope, followed by a detailed linguistic and scientific breakdown of lightning and a ritualistic curse against the antagonist.</meta>

<page-num>LXXVI</page-num>
<header>ACT THREE</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<margin>I wish it undone</margin> **He regrets it. I would prefer it not done.** Hollow. Because it contains nothing; these words are most fitting for our hypothesis. And I regret it with an equal error; I too would wish it undone. The ancients said "less done" and "I wish it undone" when they regretted something. Terence in *The Mother-in-Law*: *Who [has] good, great hope.* Persius: *But a good part of the leaders.* It shows that he is moved by a just grief. There was a proverb among our ancestors about hope: *I do not buy hope for a price.* Likewise, regarding 
<margin>Hope</margin> excessive hope, Cato said in his censorial oration: *I have often heard that many things can intervene between the mouth and the morsel* <note>original: "inter os & offam," a version of "there's many a slip 'twixt the cup and the lip"</note>. The ancients worshipped the goddess Hope, who presides over gamblers. Ovid says: *Hope is the goddess of gamblers, who encourages many, but also deceives many.* Tibullus the poet writes: *She catches birds with a snare and fish with a rod, when the bait has first hidden the slender hooks.* There are Greek verses and adages, *into hope* <note>original: "εἰς ἐλπίδας"</note>. With similar regret. Repentance; Pacuvius in *Teucer* speaks of "the desires of regret." I come to my senses. I regain my wisdom, I am recollected.

<margin>Recantation</margin> **Recantation** <note>original: "Palinodia"</note>, a taking back of words. For previously he sang well and spoke well, but now he wishes to sing back and speak ill. It is called *palinodia* in Latin, a "re-singing" or recantation. It is a compound word from *again* <note>original: "πάλιν"</note> and *song* <note>original: "ὠδὴ"</note>. Horace is said to have written a recantation against Gratidia in the first book of his *Odes*. **May they destroy** <note>original: "Perduant"</note>,
<margin>May they destroy</margin> let them ruin; an ancient way of speaking, just as *duant* was used for *dent* [let them give]. Among the ancients, there was a law of Numa Pompilius, King of the Romans, regarding a parricide, composed in these words: *If anyone knowingly brings death to a free man, let him be a parricide* <note>original: "Si quis hominem liberum mortisciens duit, parricida esto"</note>. And he separates **lightning flash** and **thunderbolt**, which are different things according to the Stoic authors. For when the vapors of the earth enter a cloud and begin to divide and break apart its thinnest parts, doing so frequently and violently, then both 
<margin>Lightning-flash
Thunderbolt
Flash-process
Striking-process</margin> **lightning flashes** and thunders arise. But if a fire is pressed out and emitted by the clash of clouds, that is a **thunderbolt**. Lactantius says these glimmers are called *fulgura* from *fulgore* [brightness]. And the *lightning-flash* is distinguished from the *striking-bolt* according to Seneca in the second book of his *Natural Questions*. For he wants the lightning-flash to be a threat without a strike, but the thunderbolt to be a hurling with a strike. Therefore, he says the flash is when the fire is spread out widely, and the thunderbolt is when it is concentrated. **May it turn to dust.** May it be scattered into dust.

> SALT, Snow, fire, unbridled storms and hail,
> Malignant winds, and motions of the earth,
> Famine, thirst, heavy cares, and starvation,
> Fevers and ulcers and pestilence,
> The falling sickness <note>epilepsy</note>, and if there be any other things
> Harmful to mortals, let them vex you, O head!

**Unbridled.** Fierce, relentless. Poets call such storms "insane." **Malignant winds.** Difficult, troublesome, unhappy. **Motions of the earth.** Earthquakes; may the chasms of the earth swallow you. **Famine, thirst.** He recites the evils which the poet collects in Book VI. For he adds whatever evil exists in the underworld. **Starvation.** Extreme hunger.

<meta>This page continues the philological and medical commentary on Act Three. The previous page ended with a discussion on "recantation" and regret, leading into the physical and psychological consequences of sin—expressed here through the imagery of disease (ulcers and epilepsy) and a ritualistic curse against an antagonist associated with Muhammad.</meta>

<page-num>LXXVII</page-num>
<header>ACT THREE</header>

...starving [dogs]. And ulcers. <margin>Wound, Ulcer</margin> They say a **wound** differs from an **ulcer** in that a wound is inflicted on the body by external force or a blow, whereas an ulcer arises from the corruption of internal humors. Greek orthography suggests this word should be aspirated, as they write it ελκος <note>original: "helkos," meaning a wound or sore</note>. **Pestilence**; plague. **The falling sickness** <note>original: "Morbus caducus"</note>; Galen calls it *epilepsy*, while the Latins call it the **comitial disease**, which was so detestable among the ancients that the assemblies (*comitia*) would be dissolved because of it. For it seemed to be an ill omen <note>original: "piaculum"</note> to hold an assembly once a person with the comitial disease was seen. So says Serenus: *And the species of sudden disease which takes its name from that fact, because the just voting is refused to us. Often also, when limbs fall in dark faintness, a horrific blight breaks up the council of the people.* It was a custom among the ancients to spit at the falling sickness. <margin>Assemblies</margin> Plautus says in *The Captives*: *And do not fear that this is the disease for which I must be spat upon; that disease torments many men, for whom being spat upon was a means of health.* See Pliny, Book 9 of the *Natural History*; Suetonius in his life of *Julius*; and Cornelius Celsus, Book 3, Chapter 23. <margin>Falling, To Vex</margin> It is called **falling** because he who is afflicted by this disease falls down. It is called **lunatic** by some, although the "lunatic" and the "spastic" are different from the "falling" patient. **May they vex** <note>original: "Vexent"</note>; a verb very well suited for imprecations and curses, because of its weightiness.

> ARI. I myself call down an equal vow upon your filthy head:
> That which is a terror to the underworld, may it tear you apart;
> May the gaping of the earth swallow you down by devouring you;
> May the furious war, and Cerberus, and Tartarus
> Torture you in the dark prison, in the Tullianum <note>The deepest, most dreaded dungeon in ancient Rome</note>,
> Living and still bound with iron chains.
> And may filth, horror, and a forever-parched trembling,
> Thick chaos, and the dark chorus of shadows
> Tear you to pieces along with your Muhammad <note>original: "Mahometo"; here used as a figure of ultimate heresy or enmity</note>.
> May they strangle you, choke you, and blot you out;
> And may the knife, and poison, and incantation
> Bitterly end your life, which is itself a death.

<margin>The Furies</margin>
**Furious war.** The lashes of the Furies. Cicero explains what the poetic fiction concerning the Furies means in his oration *Against Lucius Piso* and *For Sextus Roscius of Ameria*, where he writes: *Men ought not to think, as is often seen in plays, that those who have committed something impious and wicked are pursued and terrified by the burning torches of the Furies. Each man is terrified by his own evil thoughts and the consciousness of his mind.* And in truth, no witness is more heavy than **conscience**, <margin>Conscience</margin> that hidden executioner, who mangles and tortures the vitals more than crosses, more than lashes, more than torments. As the Greeks say: συνειδησις ωλκηζει την ψυχηv <note>original: "syneidesis helkizei ten psychen," Conscience lashes the soul</note>. **Cerberus.** In the sixth...

<sig>H iiii</sig>
<meta>The catchword "Sexto" refers to the beginning of the next page, likely continuing the citation from the "Sixth" book of Virgil's Aeneid regarding Cerberus.</meta>

It concludes with a philosophical reflection on the Furies as a metaphor for the tortures of a guilty conscience.</summary>
