<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Act Three. Following the previous page’s discussion on physical diseases and rituals, the author now turns to the etymology of names and legalistic definitions of punishment and death, specifically referencing the character Butes and the imagery of the skull.</meta>

<page-num>LXXVIII</page-num>
<header>ACT THREE</header>

Servius <note>a 4th-century commentator on Virgil</note> and Fulgentius Placiades <note>a late-antique mythographer</note> set down the origin of the name "Aeneid." Other mythic historians and the chronicler Eusebius record that Orcus, the king of the Molossians, had a dog of immense size named Cerberus, who devoured Pirithous when he descended to kidnap the king’s wife. <margin>Prison</margin> **Tartarus**: concerning which we spoke a little earlier according to the opinion of Plato.

**In a dark prison.** The Greeks call a prison *desmoterion* <note>original: Δεσμωτήριον</note>. But "prisons" <note>in the plural</note> are not the same thing as a "prison" <note>in the singular</note>, as the diligent reader will find in Servius’s commentary on Book 1 of the *Aeneid*; the poets often confuse the two. **And squalor**: filth, the horror of death, defiled by <margin>Chaos</margin> pollution. Terence <note>the Roman comic playwright</note> says: *I see him rough, squalid, and sick.* **Dense chaos**: a horrible confusion, named from its capacity and from the act of "taking in" <note>the author links *chaos* to the Greek *chasko*, meaning to gape open</note>. See Ovid, Book 1 of the *Metamorphoses* and Book 1 of the *Fasti*; Virgil, Book 6; Statius, Book 4; and Silius Italicus, Book 11 of the *Punic Wars*. Hesiod himself also describes chaos. <margin>I strangle, I bind</margin> **Let them tear apart**: let them pull asunder and lacerate. **Let them strangle**: to suffocate; the word comes from the Greek. Varro agrees, for the Greeks use *spargalio* <note>original: σπαργαλίῳ</note> for "I suffocate."

**They shall bind**: they shall hinder, run against, or resist. The ancients used this word, as seen in Varro’s *Manlius*. <margin>I erase</margin> **Let them obliterate**: let them condemn to oblivion and tread down into eternal darkness. **Both the knife and the incantation**: this signifies a sudden and unexpected death. Regarding the bringing about of death, the Greeks write that three things are ready: the sword, the noose, and poison. Thus they say: *three things for death: sword, snare, hemlock or poison* <note>original: τὰ τρία εἰς θάνατον ξίφος βρόχος κώνειον</note>. **And incantation**: the actor speaks in the manner of the common folk, who are convinced that sometimes something can be achieved by the power of a magic song by witches. **Sanguinary. Your life which is death**: who denies that the life of the wicked is death? <margin>The life of the wicked</margin> Since Saint Jerome, in his testament, calls the life of the world not life but death; conversely, the mortality of good men is ended by death more so than their life, as found in Pliny’s second book of letters to Romanus.

->BVTES: It is well done; they are frustrated by this skull,<-
->Whoever places their hope in this hollow skull.<-
->I have done my duty, best companions,<-
->I have cleverly brought light games with my mocking<-
->Before your eyes, as I was permitted to do.<-

<margin>To deceive or frustrate</margin> **It is well done**: you have prayed for worthy things, you have performed your parts abundantly; what remains is mine. **They are frustrated** <note>original: *Caluuntur*</note>: they are deceived; the term is drawn from "hollow enemies," because they are a source of frustration to all. <note>The author is likely making a pun on *calva* (skull) and *calvor* (to deceive or play tricks).</note> **Whoever places hope**: the poet prescribes a lesson for us, teaching that we should not place any hope in a man of no account. <margin>Mockery, I mock</margin> **Best companions**: we have mentioned companions sufficiently above, although there is a Greek proverb: *twice and thrice a good thing* <note>original: δὶς καὶ τρὶς καλόν</note>. **With my mocking** <note>original: *Cauillo*</note>: by mockery. Apuleius uses the phrase "a clever mockery." Marcellus thinks that in the age of Cicero, *to mock* was taken to mean "to joke," while Festus Pompeius understands the name *mockery* as "derision," but elsewhere takes it more significantly for a "jocular false accusation." Laurentius investigates this matter with a meticulous hunt.

<summary>This page provides a philological analysis of Act Three, explaining the etymology of terms for death, prison, and chaos while citing classical authorities like Virgil, Ovid, and Jerome. It concludes with a commentary on the character Butes and the symbolic significance of the skull as a metaphor for the frustration of worldly hopes.</summary>
<keywords>Cerberus, Tartarus, Chaos, etymology, St. Jerome, Pliny the Younger, Butes, mockery, legal terminology, classical mythology</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page concludes the philological commentary on Act Three and provides the play's epilogue. It continues the author’s scholarly method of citing classical grammarians and moralists to explain both the vocabulary and the ethical lessons of the performance.</meta>

<page-num>LXXIX</page-num>

Lorenzo Valla <note>a famous 15th-century Italian humanist and philologist</note> in the sixth book of *The Elegances of the Latin Language* writes on this. But so that it may be more easily understood and better explained, let one example suffice, which you may seek in Aulus Gellius <note>a 2nd-century Roman author of *Attic Nights*</note>, Book 6. Priscian <note>a late-antique Latin grammarian</note> thinks it is derived from "bald" <note>original: *calvus*</note>, so that "to mock" <note>original: *cavillo*</note> is said instead of *calvillo* <note>a diminutive form of mockery</note>; the root of this word was used by Sallust in the third book of his *Histories* where he says "to mock or deceive" <note>original: *calvificatus*</note>.

### EPILOGUE

If anyone desires to wisely conduct every business,
So that it may serve well his private property
And—what is even more—the public community,
Turning out fruitfully and fitting together just right,
Let him remember the things said in this comedy.
Let him do nothing with an empty-headed fool, nor consult one
Where there is neither wisdom nor constant faith.
Especially where he has already sworn falsely again,
For nothing is ever worse than perjury.
Now applaud, and farewell; the performance is finished.

<margin>To swear falsely</margin>
<margin>Perjury</margin>
Intending to make the effort worthwhile for the expectant audience, the reciter provides healthy counsel with these clever and learned warnings. For what else does comedy offer, if Cicero is to be believed? And what else is the life of man but a comedy? Although "Polycratus" <note>referring to John of Salisbury’s 12th-century work, the *Policraticus*</note> says it is more like a tragedy because of its often sorrowful end. Something of this sort is seen in Plautus, where the play ends with the *Captives*. **Wisely**: that is, circumspectly, deliberately, and with counsel. **So that it may serve well**: that it may succeed usefully, as he himself explains. **And fitting together**: that it should agree without being out of place. **Let him remember**: let him hold it in his mind. For it is proper to remember what we have read. So says Cato: "Remember what you have read and learned" <note>original Greek: τῶν ἀνεγνωσμένων μέμνησο</note>. **Empty-headed**: a stupid person, with whom nothing can be done wisely. **Sworn falsely**: God hates a perjurer, as Phocylides <note>a 6th-century BC Greek gnomic poet</note> says. Bede <note>the English monk and scholar, d. 735</note> instructs that the word be written and spoken as *peiurium* <note>with the extra 'i'</note>, not *periurium*. Marcus Cicero defines it in the fourth book <note>though Cicero’s *On Duties* contains only three books, the author likely references a specific commentary or a medieval division</note> of *On Duties*; a book which is held in the highest regard for moral teaching, if we believe the Saint Augustine. **Now applaud and farewell; the performance is finished**: if the end of a speech was pleasing, it used to be followed by a roar of approval. We read of this in Quintus Curtius <note>a Roman historian of Alexander the Great</note>, Book 8. He says: "Never before was such a spirited shout returned by the army." However, it should be known that these words were once usually spoken by the reciter to the people, who were so eager and greedy for hearing and watching that they might not realize the comedy had ended; therefore, he would urge them to applaud, so that they might signal with the clapping of their hands that the play had pleased them. It is said that Diogenes <note>the Cynic philosopher</note> had been reading for a very long time, and when he showed that nothing more was written at the end of the book, he told his companions to be of good heart, because "he could see land" <note>a joke meaning the long journey of the text was finally over</note>.

->T H E   E N D<-

<vocab>Valla: Lorenzo Valla, Italian humanist. Aulus Gellius: Roman author. Sallust: Roman historian. Cicero: Roman orator and philosopher. John of Salisbury (Polycratus): Medieval philosopher. Plautus: Roman playwright. Cato: reference to the "Distichs of Cato," a school text. Phocylides: Greek poet. Bede: Anglo-Saxon scholar. Augustine: early Christian theologian. Quintus Curtius: historian of Alexander the Great. Diogenes: Greek philosopher. Comedy: here, a mirror of life. Perjury: the sin of false swearing.</vocab>

<summary>This final page of the manuscript provides an epilogue that summarizes the moral utility of comedy and concludes with a scholarly commentary on the tradition of theatrical applause and the etymology of perjury.</summary>
<keywords>epilogue, ethics, public vs private good, perjury, classical scholarship, Diogenes, Cicero, John of Salisbury, theatrical tradition</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page serves as the formal dedication of the work. It follows the play's epilogue from the previous page, transitioning from the performance text to the scholarly apparatus provided by Georgius Simler, a student of the famous humanist Johannes Reuchlin.</meta>

# TO JOHANNES REUCHLIN OF PFORZHEIM, DOCTOR OF LAWS
## TO HIS TEACHER AND MASTER
->Georgius Simler<-

¶ First, right from the beginning, I declare a favorable greeting to myself and to you, my sweetest teacher. By that sweet-sounding charm of your rhythmic verses, even if my learning were somewhat lacking, I could not restrain myself from learning something additional, by which I might guide my students—those scholars, not the young men to whom nothing more seductive can happen than "night, woman, and wine" <note>original: *nox, mulier et vinum*; a common Latin proverbial trio of vices</note>—I mean those students of mine waiting with benevolence, though with difficult and troublesome delay, for your *Sergius* <note>a satirical play by Reuchlin attacking the corruption of the clergy</note> and my annotations, just as you have pledged that posterity should be perpetually adorned for your praise. 

But I might fear somewhat that I have entered that wrestling-floor where one exhausts oneself with losses, and I might dread "contributions" <note>original: *symbolas*; refers to a shared cost or, metaphorically, a blow or punishment</note> on a substitute back—just like that slave in Plautus <note>referring to the clever but frequently beaten slaves in the comedies of Titus Maccius Plautus</note> with threats hanging over my shoulders—because of those whose brows will wrinkle with morning-time severity. This would be so, unless Marcus Varro <note>a prolific Roman scholar and writer</note>, a most weighty authority, delivered me from this fear, saying that we ought to bring into the open what we know, for no one can know everything. It rarely happens that anyone finds the right and perfect measures in all things (as they are found in you). 

Columella <note>a Roman writer on agriculture</note> also consoles us when he says that whatever mortals have been held as the wisest, they knew many things, but not all. It is not absurd, then, if I cause myself mental anguish because I have not arranged everything with perfect precision <note>original: *examussim*; literally "by the mason's rule"</note>, avoiding many words for the sake of brevity; perhaps I have even admitted some error or neglected something necessary to know. Let Cicero respond for me, if it deserves to be called a fault that I have approached the task boldly. Thus says Cicero on the perfect orator to Marcus Brutus: "It is fair for everyone to try everything, who have desired great things and things greatly to be sought after. But if someone’s own nature, or that force of an outstanding intellect should happen to fail him, or if he is less instructed in the disciplines of the great arts, let him nevertheless hold whatever course he can; for while following the first, it is honorable to stand in the second or third rank." 

Therefore, this web was begun by your virtue and under your auspices, sweetest teacher. If I please you, I have bid for great goodwill with a very small price, and you are much dearer to me than the twelve gods <note>original: *duodecim deis*; a classical expression for the Olympian pantheon</note>. I consider you, whom I value above all others, to be fighting on my side; as for the rest, those "mouse-killers" <note>original: *muricidas*; a term of contempt for insignificant or cowardly critics</note>, I do not care a hair for them. No one can please such ill-mannered people, even if he had the soles of his shoes stuffed with gold. We have neither sown nor reaped for them, but for you. If I do not please you, then (as the old proverb goes) "I have upset the wagon" <note>original: *plaustrum... perculi*; a Latin idiom meaning to ruin a situation or make a blunder</note> at the wrong time. 

But truly, if we have erred in anything, let those who would reproach us forgive us, not forgetting that line recited by a certain most learned Greek: "To err is a human condition" <note>original: *ἀνθρωπινον ἐστι πάθος τὸ ἁμαρτάνειν*; a Greek maxim often attributed to various poets</note>. And if they are not satisfied with that verse, let that Roman thunderer, Marcus Cicero, intervene in his twelfth *Philippic*, saying: "To err is any man's lot, but to persevere in error belongs to no one but a fool." For second thoughts (as they say) are always accustomed to be wiser. But soon another matter must be washed away and removed...

<summary>In this dedication, Georgius Simler presents his commentary on the play *Sergius* to his mentor, Johannes Reuchlin. Simler uses classical authorities like Cicero and Varro to humbly acknowledge his potential for errors while dismissing the potential scorn of petty critics.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Reuchlin, Georgius Simler, dedication, humanism, Sergius (play), classical education, Cicero, Marcus Varro, Columella, error, scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page concludes the introductory dedication from Georgius Simler to his mentor Johannes Reuchlin. It continues the thought from the previous page regarding the "measures" or scholarly additions Simler has made to Reuchlin’s work.</meta>

[...com]pleted—namely the things omitted, most worthy of being known and understood—I can say to one so supportive, if you are endowed with such dexterity of mind and do not consider this common interpretation <note>original: "interpretationem uulgariam"; likely referring to a translation or commentary in the common tongue or a simplified Latin style</note> to have been hammered out with a hasty and hurried zeal. Revisit it, and if anything is missing, add to it and patch it together, so that it may come forth more perfect; in doing so, you will perform a service most pleasing not only to me but also to posterity. I return to you, most beloved teacher. For I am not so much tormented by how easily or fortunately things turn out for me, as I am concerned that you may be at peace—which I hope for, if your grace is not deaf to my duty. For you labor to be of use to all, paying no mind to those...

<image-desc>The following lines of text narrow progressively to a single word before widening again to form the base of a chalice shape, a style of visual poetry known as a carmen figuratum.</image-desc>

->...whose hearts envy wounds; and as the winds blow through all the highest<-
->peaks, you conquer the coming age with your true reputation;<-
->so wise are you in your full heart, you who earn the<-
->gratitude of students of literature with the<-
->fullest devotion; wherefore a sacrificial<-
->offering <note>original: "piacularem"; a term for an atonement or a victim offered to appease gods</note> must<-
->be made<-
->for that folly<-
->worthy of the hellish<-
->regions <note>original: "acheruntica... regione"; referring to Acheron, a river in the Greek underworld</note>, by him who<-
->interrupts your kindness and glory<-
->with a stubborn objection—you who<-
->with sail and oar <note>original: "uelo remoq;"; a Latin idiom meaning "with all one's might"</note> lead all<-
->to good harvest. Therefore may the gods love you,<-
->and may you be as well as you wish, ever safe and<-
->fortunate; may the gods grant you years (it pleases<-
->me now to sing with Ovid <note>referring to the Roman poet Publius Ovidius Naso</note>) for you shall take the<-
->rest from yourself. The glory of Pforzheim <note>original: "Phorcensis"; Reuchlin's birthplace</note> and the praise and<-
->honor of the land. From our Lacydium <note>likely a humanist name for Simler's place of study, named after the Greek philosopher Lacydes</note>.<-

<summary>In this concluding dedication, Georgius Simler asks his mentor Johannes Reuchlin to refine his scholarly annotations and praises Reuchlin's enduring legacy against envious critics. The text is visually arranged as a 'carmen figuratum' in the shape of a chalice, symbolizing devotion and the offering of his work.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Reuchlin, Georgius Simler, carmen figuratum, Pforzheim, humanism, Ovid, scholarly dedication, Lacydium</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the colophon, or final publication statement, for the volume. It follows the completion of the alphabetical index on the preceding page and provides the logistical details of the book's production, including the printer's name, location, and date.</meta>

LXXXVII

<meta>Pencil notation at bottom center: Z 1. 6. 100</meta>

->Printed at Tübingen in the house of Thomas Anshelm<-
<note>Thomas Anshelm (c. 1470–1523) was a significant early Renaissance printer known for his high-quality scholarly editions of Greek, Hebrew, and Latin texts.</note>
->of Baden. In the year 1513.<-
->In the month of April.<-

<image-desc>The printer's mark of Thomas Anshelm of Baden. Within a rectangular frame, a circular medallion features a stylized monogram of the letters "TAB" <note>representing Thomas Anshelm Badensis</note>. Above the medallion, a flowing banner contains the Hebrew Tetragrammaton <note>יהוה, the four-letter sacred name of God, often rendered as Yahweh or Jehovah</note>. The background of the frame is filled with dense vertical hatching.</image-desc>

<summary>This is the colophon page of the book, identifying Thomas Anshelm as the printer and providing the location (Tübingen) and date of completion (April 1513). It features Anshelm's personal printer's mark, which incorporates his monogram and the Hebrew name of God.</summary>

<keywords>Thomas Anshelm, Tübingen, 1513, colophon, printer's mark, Renaissance printing, Tetragrammaton, publishing history</keywords>
